PREFACE 

To study the mind as a physician studies the body, that is, in terms of signs and symptoms, will doubtless be a new method to readers of this book. Nearly all the rules are original observations, as far as the author is concerned, although it is probable that every truthful observation recorded here has been made by some one else in the years gone by. Insofar as the author knows, no one heretofore has attempted to set down in print any extensive number of observations of this kind nor for this definite purpose. 

This method appears to the author as the most intelligent and the most feasible way for the layman to learn mental qualities. With this method the student needs no psychological laboratory, no guinea-pigs, no monkeys, and no detailed set of questions and tests with which to begin and to continue his study. All that he needs is to learn a rule, a dozen rules, or a hundred rules-and then proceed to use what he has learned. 

An intelligent student can prove any one of the rules true, if it is true, by using it a month or two, at most, in case the rule does not appear true to him the instant he reads it the first time. And, once learned and applied in a dozen cases, the rule will not be forgotten. The persons with whom one comes in contact daily afford all the material necessary for study. 

The comments are observations and opinions that reflect the experience of the writer with people and with his reading over years of time. To the mature physician who is well-informed in this subject they will, for the most part, appear simple and logical. To the laymanfor whom the book is written-both the rules and the

PREFACE 

comments doubless will arouse a great variety of new thoughts, and approval, as well as a little disapproval. 

This last statement is based on the reaction of several persons who read the manuscript before it was published. The comments, variable as the physician's daily experiences, range from the disagreeably frank to levity, from the esoteric to the immediately obvious, and from things readily understood by a youth in his 'teens to things that can be understood only by the wellinformed, mentally mature adult.

The lay reader, if unaccustomed to scientific frankness, should bear in mind that the physician is looking for facts about both the body and the mind; and that in doing so and in describing what he finds, or thinks he has found, he is not concerned with being either agreeable or disagreeable, pleasant or unpleasant; his purpose is to analyse rather than to defend or to condemn.

The book may be read through in a few hours and laid aside, after the manner of reading a novel; or it may be studied for years. All will depend upon the reader's interest in the subject. 

Fort Worth, Texas. 

Feb. 5, 1935. 

J. P.

PART ONE THE PROBLEM 

When the layman sets out to study mental qualities, either his own or those of other persons, he will at once meet with a few difficulties that it would be well to consider first. It is not a debatable point that there are good minds, nor should it be a debatable point that good minds are made up largely of good individual mental qualities. Surely any one who would read a book of this kind knows that some persons have good memory and that some have a poor memory; that some persons can learn mathematics easily and that some have great difficulty in learning mathematics; that some persons have talent for drawing and painting and that other persons have no talent for these things; and that some persons limit their personal responsibilities rigidly while other persons, who have had the same environment, assume personal responsibilities very carelessly. 

This point, then, needs no further illumination. 

As an original study it takes months or years of time to recognize superior mental qualities. But, once set down in print, nearly any one of these mental qualities may be recognized by a good mind in a few seconds or in a few minutes. The difficulty or lack of interest in learning to see what is before us daily and yet remains unseen-is a mystery, an unsolved problem even to the scientific mind. Let us illustrate the point. 

Physicians are supposed to be trained observers-but are they? One can only answer that some are and some are not. Medical men have learned many valuable physical signs of disease by means of their five special

KNOW THYSELF 

senses-and not by instruments or laboratory testsduring the last twenty-five to fifty years that they might have learned centuries ago. These signs are so easily recognized once they are discovered and pointed out to us that even the layman can see them; and, yet, we physicians overlooked them century after century. 

What then of the mental world? It is logical to suppose that most men and women will be even more blind about seeing mental qualities-unless they are pointed out to them-than physicians have been with respect to seeing the physical signs of disease or health in the body.

In some ways mental qualities are more difficult to recognize and to understand than the physical signs are; for reason, rather than the five special senses, must take the lead in discovering them or in understanding them if and when someone points them out to us. In this particular work, however, reason has oppositionan enemy-that is prone to hinder if not actually to prevent its working. That enemy is wish-thinking or wishful thinking, as some writers use the term.

Throughout this study wish-thinking must be mentioned often.

Also other hindrances to the study of the mind, now happily of less influence than they were in the past, are mystery, superstition, and erroneous traditional beliefs. 

It is no easy matter for the layman to get rid of mystery, magic, superstition, and untrue traditional beliefs, not only in his religion, but also in his first efforts to learn anything useful about the mind. 

Doubtless from the earliest ages of mankind men and women have judged each other's minds. One may surmise that such judgments consisted very largely of excuses for immediate relatives having poor minds, hostile criticism or pity for other persons of poor mentality, and fear or admiration of the enemy, the shrewd, and the subtle. Until more recent years very little has been

THE PROBLEM 

9 said or written about good minds, save that occasionally some person was called wise; and less still has been said or written relative to the evidences of mental superiority. More has been learned about the mind during the last century than was learned during all the centuries before.

In these days one hears and reads many opinions from immature intellectuals, the intelligentsia, amateurish students of eugenics, genetics, sociology, and economics, as well as physicians, concerning the improvement of the human race. Many of these writers. and talkers are very vague and indefinite; so much so, in fact, they give one the impression they are only reciting well-worn shibboleths and generalities of the wish-thinking type. This fact may be readily proved by asking a few of them, one at a time, to state in detail some of the evidences of mental superiority, it being assumed that good minds are the chief thing desired in the improvement of the race. If this is done one will see most of these critics and theoretical uplifters look stupidly into space, assume a defensive attitude, and admit they do not know definitely just what may be considered good mental qualities. 

The question arises at the outset then: can individual mental qualities be observed with sufficient clearness that they may be set down in print, studied, learned, evaluated, and used as one learns and uses the physical evidences of health or disease in plants or animals? As with many other questions one will have to answer by saying that some persons can learn them and some can not. Or perhaps one should say, that some persons can not learn any of them, some can learn a few, and some can and will learn a goodly number voluntarily.

Any plan or scheme set forth to enable the general reader not the professional psychologist with his intelligence tests-to recognize mental qualities in him-

KNOW THYSELF 

self and other persons must have three things. The rules or observations must be generally true; they should be useful; the plan should be feasible. One should be able to make his observations and apply his knowledge whenever he meets people regardless of place or condition.

It may be stated as a fact that there are a few very good minds and a large number of poor minds in a group of, let us say, one thousand persons. In between these extremes are found our so-called average or mediocre people. A hundred persons having good minds are found to have many similar mental qualities; and the same is true of persons having poor minds. One thing is sure: there is no lack of material for observation at least in the inferior and mediocre groups. 

Comparatively few beginning students of this subject can, without embarrassment, face the facts relative to mental qualities. When the subject comes up, even for impersonal study or discussion, nearly all persons take the defensive attitude and begin to give excusesexcuses for themselves, their near relatives, and a few of their more intimate acquaintances. The excusegiver clings to environmentalism; it is his principal refuge. He tells us that Elmer and Jasper never got anywhere in life because of lack of advantages; he either can not or will not see that Elmer and Jasper have poor mental equipment.

Many lay persons, especially if they are more or less neurotic, can not discuss mental qualities impersonally. 

In beginning this study a person having a non-scientific mind, and more especially a neurotic, is likely to be highly subjective; he gabbles and simpers, talks about himself, gives excuses, produces alibis and gets nowhere. His curiosity does not last long for the subject is distasteful to him and he may dismiss it at once. 

Such persons will be known better by others than they are by themselves.

THE PROBLEM 

11 Any study of mental qualities to be illuminating and helpful must be entirely free of wish-thinking and the defensive attitude. The subject must be undertaken in the same way that a skilful medical diagnostician makes a diagnosis of an obscure disease such as beginning tuberculosis or of pellagra. There are no fool-proof rules for the doctor to follow in diagnosing these diseases. He merely seeks all the evidence in detail and then makes the diagnosis on the evidence he finds. He is not influenced either by his or the patient's wishes or by sentiment.

Before anyone can study mental qualities intelligently he must give up all childish notions about everyone's being born free and equal-an oft-quoted American stupidity. As a matter of fact no two persons are born equal mentally, although identical twins, to the superficial observer, may appear to be, as their name implies, identical. One must avoid stressing the occasional exception to the rule and being blind to the more general truth covered by it. One must also give up the many ifs and buts that come into the mind of the beginner, as well as one's fear and reluctance to see faults. 

There probably are no perfect minds.

In order to make a mental diagnosis on a person one should know and be able to recall, as one knows and recalls the multiplication table, at least a hundred evidences of mental superiority. Some of these evidences may be thought of as talents, some as characteristics, and some as tendencies or traits. Also the factors of quantity and capacity are very important. In the language of the chemist the student must make both qualitative and quantitative tests. One should have enough rules or accepted evidences at his command that he could begin his mental analysis as soon as a person comes into his presence, or as soon as he begins to read a biography. A rose is not judged by one petal but by all its qualities; a human body should not be judged

KNOW THYSELF 

by one organ or part but by all its organs and parts; a library is not judged by one book but by all its books. 

So too no mind should be judged by only one of its qualities either good or bad. No one shows all his mental qualities at one time; but except when asleep, and sometimes even then, everyone shows one or more of his mental qualities at all times when he is awake, especially in voluntary conversation. 

The mind is judged best by its voluntary acts; this fact must not be overlooked at any time. A child that wants to go to school usually has a better mind than a child that must be compelled to go to school. A young man who wants to save his money usually has a better mind than a young man who saves nothing. A man who is voluntarily self-supporting and supports his dependents, if he has any, is of a much higher grade mentally than a parasite or a bill-beater. A person who voluntarily uses good manners and good language is of finer clay than the person who is indifferent to these things. Always in judging the mind it is well to note if the person being judged does worth-while things voluntarily.

If a person is aware that he is being studied he is likely to become embarrassed or confused; or he may throw out a screen of deception. Because of this, mental tests as applied to school students are not so valuable as careful mental observation and accurate evaluation of the findings made without the knowledge of the person being studied. It might, however, take long association with a person to see his voluntary reaction to a hundred situations, conditions, or problems as set forth in this book.

While one is finding the evidences of good mentality one will also find evidences of poor mentality. In predicting the behavior of a person in a given matter, then, it will be necessary to speculate on which mental quality or qualities-good or bad-will be in control. Pre-

THE PROBLEM 

13 diction is therefore more accurate relative to persons with very good minds or with very poor minds. A fool will usually act like a fool and a wise man will usually act with wisdom.

Most persons are a mixture of the sage and the fool but one quality usually predominates. The sage is a fool when he permits his instincts to control him rather than his reasoned judgment; and the mediocre person is a sage when he refrains from doing foolish things. 

It is well to remember, though, that even inferior minds may have some conspicuously good traits, talents, or other qualities; for example, a marked talent for music, or a keen, inflexible sense of justice. Also let it be remembered that minds of genius rank may have a few mental qualities of the moron, or they may be partly insane.

The mind should be judged in the same way that one judges the body. A physician can examine the nude body of an athlete and find scores of evidences of physical superiority together with a few minor defects. 

His conclusion will be that the athlete has a superior physique. So too a mind may be judged as superior even though it has a few defects. Also a mind may be classified as inferior even though it has a few good qualities. What worth-while things a person in his 'teens or an adult has done voluntarily is an important thing to consider in the final judgment of a mind. 

Unless one makes it his business to know the evidences of mental superiority thoroughly and to take sufficient time to discover them in a person his judgment of the person's mind is likely to be only a superficial, ill-founded guess. A physician can sometimes judge the body with considerable accuracy after an hour's examination or even at a glance; so too a layman may recognize an idiot or a maniac at a glance; but a mental analysis, except in extreme cases, may require observation extending over weeks or months of

KNOW THYSELF 

time. On the other hand if a person knows mental qualities thoroughly, he is able to begin their detection in another person as soon as contact is made with him. 

Within a few seconds or a few minutes one is very likely to use the first, second, eighth, and last rules in this study.

Parents are usually unable to judge their children accurately, not only because love is blind but also because their judgment is controlled by wish-thinking and the defensive attitude. Parents often know less about their children than their neighbors and family friends do. Parents' angels may be considered neighborhood devils, in which case the neighbors are usually right. 

If one were to ask John Doe's acquaintances what kind of man he is one would hear a great variety of statements about John's morals, his manners, his physical appearance, his wearing apparel, his occupation, and his social life, together with a few I-knewhim-when stories; but one would hear little or nothing about the most important thing-namely, his mental qualities. If, on the other hand, the question were put to someone who knows mental qualities and also knows John, and the reply was given that John is a man of superior mentality, probably the inquirer would think that John should wear a monocle and appear socially aloof rather than be attired in greasy unionalls and work as a high-grade mechanic. It is not easy for the inexperienced layman to think of the mind, itself, for he has always been used to thinking of the external man. 

Laymen, except those having scientific minds, are prone to have an all-or-none attitude toward theories and facts upon which they pass judgment. They have what may be called the fundamentalist type of mind.

Fundamentalists either believe all or reject all; they do not believe in doubts and reasoned judgment, especially in regard to religious authority. It is necessary to avoid the all-or-none attitude in the study of mental

THE PROBLEM 

15 qualities just as we doctors avoid it in our professional work. Many men and women in the lower mental brackets are prone to praise other persons extravagantly or to condemn them severely because of a single virtue or fault.

Ignorance of mental qualities and mental rights has done much to cause ill feeling, dissension, strife, persecution, and injustice in this world. Also it may be observed that too much of the thinking, planning, and activity directed toward the care and improvement of the inferior-now the gravest problem that confronts America, as well as the rest of the world-has been directed toward their environment, and that there has not been enough thought given to their mental qualities. This particular detail will be mentioned often in this study. It is necessary to do this in order that the layman may know the physician's views in the matter, even though he may not agree with all of them. Ignorance and egotism together make a mental barrier that is almost impenetrable; ignorance and zeal are the basis of fanaticism; wish-thinking, stupidity, and selfcomplacency prevent desire for learning. 

Lust to learn worth-while things is found only in the upper mental brackets. Most persons are able only to follow beaten paths and many of these are often in doubt as to which path to follow. Comparatively few persons can read and fewer still can write. It is a pathetic thing, a poor, weak defensive attitude, for a person to say that he can read but that he does not want to. As a matter of fact persons who can read do read, just as persons who can think do think; and they need no more encouragement to do these things than a duck needs to go a-swimming. Millions of people in the lower mental brackets would not learn about mental qualities if they had an opportunity to do so. 

There will be set forth in this book one hundred evidences of mental superiority together with brief com-

KNOW THYSELF 

ment on them illustrating their application. Most of these rules are easy both to learn and to apply. The fact that the truth-if it is a truth-that is described by the rule was not discovered centuries ago is not strange, if one considers that we physicians are still discovering physical signs of disease that could have been-and should have been-discovered centuries ago. Three of the rules, the first and the last two, have received much study by psychologists, both professional and amateur, for a score or more of years; and yet they remain unknown to most of the so-called reading public. 

Evidences of mental superiority can not be observed in a clearcut, accurate way like the squares on a checkerboard; for all talents, traits, characteristics, tendencies, and whatnot are interrelated if not actually dependent. 

The comparison may appear somewhat far-fetched at first, but selecting mental qualities and fitting them together to form a whole is very much like working out a jig-saw puzzle. Some of the pieces are more important than others; and sometimes one or more pieces of more or less importance are lacking.

The interest as well as the problems involved in the study of mental qualities changes rapidly back and forth from the particular to the general, from the personal to the impersonal, and from the individual to the herd. Very likely most students will have their personal inventory taken within a year from the time they begin the study; and after that is done-and sometimes even before it is done-most of their thought will be directed toward other persons. 

There is much quackery that bears the name psychology given out to the public in these days. Mountebanks and charlatans who call themselves psychologists are found in all cities; and some of them travel from city to city giving courses of lectures at Ritzy hotels and other places to the ignorant and the gullible, promising to teach them how to be healthy, wealthy, and

THE PROBLEM 

17 wise, how to develop personality, sex-appeal, and supersalesmanship-all in six lectures for ten dollars or more.

Then too there is much wordy dogmatizing and academic quibbling among college teachers-the introspectionists, behaviorists, configurationists, British Associationists, and other schools of psychology. Much of this is very unprofitable to read or to hear outside the teacher's class-room.

But there are two things about minds that are always worth while for the layman to know: the first is evidences of mental superiority, and the second is the motives of human behavior. Fortunately the things most important for the layman to know can be learned without the study of what he calls dull, dismal textbooks written in difficult technical language. A person's understanding of human behavior depends very largely upon his knowledge of mental qualities; the first steps then for the student to take are apparent. 

Medical psychology has in recent years come into the limelight and is now read and discussed glibly if not intelligently or profitably from the most remote farm home to Morningside Heights. Most laymen would be better off to suppress their curiosity about complexes, fixations, frustrations, and repressions, and leave the subject alone. Physicians not infrequently see highly suggestible young men and women whose morbid curiosity has led them to read the theories of sex conflicts as taught by Freud and his disciples and who have, as a result of this unwise reading and amateurish speculation and discussion, developed distressing fears and mental kinks about themselves.

If young persons want to know about the physiology and psychology of sex, and the more intelligent ones do, they should consult a physician who is well informed on the subject and learn these things from the viewpoint of embryology, anatomy, and physiology and

KNOW THYSELF 

not from the viewpoint of the moralist, and dealers in filth and the pornographic. It is to a layman's credit that he knows what he should know that is normal about sex. But the less he knows about the abnormal, and the less he talks about it-unless he is personally involved, and then he should talk only to well-informed professional confessors-the better off he will be. Discussing filth for filth's sake is not a characteristic of a good mind.

The mind should be kept free from distressing conflicts. Sex, like sanity and truth, is only a relative thing; there are defects either small or great, due mostly to heredity or to age, in most persons. And since neither heredity nor age can be changed at will, it is useless for one to worry over imperfections caused by them. Physicians who have the type of personality that makes their clients want to confess to them hear sex confessions year after year. Most sex conflicts can be explained away and the patient's mind set at ease unless he is a congenital neuropath, in which case he will have conflicts and anxieties about many things in addition to sex-a fact that Freudians appear to overlook.

Interesting observations have been made by physicians relative to the influence on the mind of the endocrine glands, organic brain disease, poisons formed in the body because of disease or poor organic function, as well as by poisons that enter the body by way of the mouth or by means of the hypodermic needle. Also the influence of the inherent quality of the brain cortexthe external layer of the brain-on mental quality has been studied and speculated about. But this subject is beyond the reach of most laymen; besides, it is well not to forget the old saw that "A little knowledge is a dangerous thing.' 99 And then there is the occult: spiritism, fortune telling, telepathy, voices, visions, dream interpretations,

THE PROBLEM 

19 mind reading, astrology, and other weird things of the ignorant imagination. These will always appeal to the curious, the gullible, and the ignorant; and there will always be mountebanks and charlatans to profit by them. In fact many persons among the inferior and mediocre classes who probably could not be induced to learn mental qualities voluntarily take an interest in psychic fakes and fakery. But knowledge of values is for the few rather than for the many. 

A study of mental qualities may be said to be useful if it furnishes the student some illuminating information that will enable him to know himself better. 

If in addition to this it contains information that will enable him to know his fellow man better, both individually and collectively, it will help him both to avoid and to solve many personal and social problems.

PART TWO THE STUDY 

1. Persons having superior mentality have ability to see and to solve problems.

COMMENT. This is the most frequently found as well as the most important of all evidences of superior mentality. The rule is so inclusive that many of the other evidences listed in this study are only different phases of it. It is somewhat like the Golden Rule in a code of ethics in that if one uses the Golden Rule first in the code many of the other rules appear to be only a different application of it.

Children manifest problem-solving ability early in life. A superior child of eight to ten years of age may be seen doing useful, worth-while things of its own accord that the inferior or mediocre child would do only when several years older and then only when told to do them or even forced to do them. 

Superior boys and girls in their late 'teens not infrequently have a definite, feasible life-plan formulateda plan that they later achieve and in which they take the initiative in solving the problems as they arise from day to day. Inferior persons usually need someone to think for them in worth-while matters. Very often they can not see their problems until they are pointed out to them and sometimes not even then. They are prone to solve their problems poorly or not at all, even when a better informed person supervises them. This indifference and inability to do worth-while things is usually due to a 21

KNOW THYSELF 

lack of inherent intelligence; sometimes, however, it may be due to a lack of interest. 

This last point-a lack of interest-should not be overlooked in judging minds. A child with a good mind may not do a certain thing well and may be thought to be stupid and dull because of it when, as a matter of fact, the child is simply not interested in that particular thing. One may, for example, see ignorant, stupid parents trying to make a musician out of a child that has little or no talent for music. Very naturally the child makes no progress. 

In general it may be said that most of life's important problems have to do either directly or indirectly with the three major instincts: the preservation of self, the perpetuation of the species, and the social instinct. In judging anyone's mind, then, it is well to observe first how he solves or does not solve these three groups of problems.

There is much difference in the importance of problems that require solution from day to day; also there is much difference in the mental qualities that make their solution easy. A young man of twenty-one may know how to dress becomingly-a problem that many young men do not know how to solve; but he may not know how to earn or to save enough money to pay for his wearing appearel-a problem that the more intelligent young man does know how to solve. The lad with the stylish bent probably will not be embarrassed because someone else must pay for his clothes; he is pleased because he knows how to wear them. And the young man who can and does pay for his clothes, but does not know how to wear them stylishly, is not concerned with sartorial niceties and detail, but he is pleased with his integrity and independence. 

Some problems are solved because of talent or a special gift, and some are solved because of high-grade, general intelligence. It is an unfortunate thing that

THE STUDY 

23 many persons who have some special gift, especially artistic or creative talent, are often deficient in general intelligence. 

2. Persons having superior mentality are not often on the defensive; they do not go through life giving excuses for what they have done or for what they have left undone.

COMMENT. Here is one place where suitable environment would help to prevent social irritation and it might even improve personal conduct. If children and adults were taught, trained, and disciplined so they would not give excuses, life would at least be more pleasant for all. For it is less annoying to endure a silence, sullen or otherwise, on the part of someone who has failed in his duty than it is to listen to his inane excuses. In addition to this, if excuse-giving were taboo, most persons would be more likely to use what little sense nature gave them. But since excuse-giving is freely permissible in nearly all places, except in military life and a few others, vast numbers of mediocre and inferior people develop more astuteness, thoroughness, and skill in giving excuses than they do in the performance of their duties. Habitual excuse-giving ought to be recognized as an evidence of mental inferiority and considered a personal and social evil along with lying, theft, and dishonesty.

The inferior mind is not only prone to give excuses, it is also prone to become parasitical, even to the extent of claiming parasitism as a right in such important matters as that of obtaining food, clothing, shelter, and medical service.

The defensive attitude is either wrong or useless about nine times out of ten if one includes all the defending that is done from day to day. Truth needs

KNOW THYSELF 

but little defense; it only needs to be stated. If superior minds know the truth they use it without being urged or compelled to do so. Defending truth for the sake of inferior minds is an unprofitable business. The eternal verities need no defense. Personal faults and failures need to be recognized, properly considered, and then avoided or overcome-not defended! That is the way of the superior mind. Blatant defenders of the common weal are usually hypocrites or mountebanks. 

Fancy Henry VIII as "defender of the faith." 99 As a rule the poorer the mind the more readily and the more often it gives excuses; and the better the mind, the more reluctantly and the less often does it give excuses. It may be said in passing that the "regrets,' "pardons," and "excuse me's" of polite social intercourse are not included for they have little or nothing to do with the serious affairs of life. 

If one wants to select a child or a young adult that is expected to make good in life-to get somewhere in the world-one should carefully avoid selecting the habitual excuse-giver. Such persons by their own efforts rarely rise above mediocrity; indeed, very few of them reach the average in the matter of personal achievement.

There are probably very few persons, aside from prisoners and the insane, who do not have to give excuses occasionally. The really superior person, however, dislikes very much to give excuses and is likely to be intolerant of the habitual excuse-giver. 

Evidences of mental inferiority vary greatly in their conspicuousness. Some-for example, defective integrity-may not appear until after long acquaintance with a person, while others may sometimes be seen during the first few minutes' observation of a person.

Habitual excuse-giving is probably the most frequent and, to persons who are aware of its significance, one of the best leads in recognizing mental inferiority and

THE STUDY 

25 mediocrity. The absense of excuse-giving in a person is not likely to be observed, except by persons who know and who use this rule in judging minds. 

3. Persons having superior mentality are prone to think of life in terms of wholeness and harmony. Because of this they recognize spiritual, social, artistic, and economic values.

COMMENT. Inferior and many mediocre persons appear not to know what is worth while for them to spend time, effort, or money to get and to keep. This causes them to have poor economic conditions, undesirable companions, and purposeless, disappointing lives.

The ability to know values is a gift from the gods. 

Some persons appear to know inherently what is worth while; they do not make the same errors time after time; they have sufficient initiative and judgment to learn from other superior persons.

We who are physicians are often astonished in talking to the inferior persons that we meet professionally to observe how little they have thought about better standards, ways, and means of living. Not knowing what they want to be or to do in order to improve themselves, the problems involved in the changes necessary are either absent or very obscure to them. 

Persons who have had much experience with the inferior note that they are usually without gratitude or vision-they seldom even say "Thank you" for anything done for them-and that they often think they are doing the up-lifter a favor to submit to up-lifting. 

This does not mean that they do not readily accept free food, clothing, shelter, and medical service, but it does mean that many of them are reluctant and may even with resentment refuse to do anything suggested to them for their improvement. Their mental activity

KNOW THYSELF 

reminds one of a parrot sitting on his perch saying the same words and sentences over and over. They recite the same excuses, manifest the same envy, inferiority, and stupidity year after year. They appear to be devoid of worth-while purposes and plans; mentally they are adult infants, controlled by their emotions and the second and third major instincts. Southern people are right in their atttitude toward negroes in that they do not expect very much intelligent action from them. In dealing with the inferior, either white or black, it is well to remember the old profane beatitude: "Blessed is he that expecteth nothing for he shall not be disappointed." And this observation is true whether they have money or not. Let us keep the record straight: all poor people are not mentally inferior, nor are all inferior people poor financially.

People from the mediocre grade on up can learn to appreciate values. The defect in most persons is that they learn values too late; then regret is felt. Every day one hears middle-aged and elderly persons saying they wish they had done differently in their younger years. They did not know values at the right time. 

There is a marked difference between the mental aristocrat and the social pretender. Both are acquisitive, but the former is prone to get and to keep things to please himself, whereas the latter is more interested in a social show. In persons having superior mentality intelligent acquisitiveness is usually manifested early in life. This is a good lead in recognizing the superior mind from about the tenth year on up. 

Happiness is largely the result of what one is and has mentally, rather than of what one has of material things. Because this is true, men of large wealth, who know both phases of the subject, voluntarily give generously, especially to schools, libraries, and museums, in order to help persons of all kinds to improve themselves mentally and socially.

THE STUDY 

27 4. Persons having superior mentality are clearly time-conscious. 

COMMENT. One hears much adverse criticism and complaining because vast numbers of people are almost devoid of foresight. The complaint is well founded. 

Inferior and mediocre persons appear to be unable to visualize themselves as they are likely to be in future years; they live for the day and do not look ahead.

Laboring men, artisans, the white-collar class, and even men in the professions are prone to live in a fool's paradise. They apparently give little or no thought to the fact that they have a limited number of years during which they will have earning capacity. Some of them are probably fatalists but most of them are only dull and stupid in this matter. If they think at all they conclude that society, their children if they have any, or the tax-payers will provide the necessities of life for them during their last years. There is little or no evidence that they have intelligent plans for the future and that they are aware that time, next to their own inherent talent, is the most important thing in carrying out their plans.

But an intelligent view of hindsight is as much neglected as is that of foresight. To the superior mind it seems that men who have got nowhere, have accomplished nothing, at twenty-five, at thirty-five, and are still no better off at forty-five, ought to wake up and recognize the fact; but they do not.

Superior men and women regret the passing of the years because they have accomplished so little, at least so little of what they had hoped to do. Not so with the inferior; except for their waning interest in the second major instinct, the passing of time means very little to them. Having never set up a definite, feasible goal to reach, they are indifferent toward their progress. 

It is true the inferior have childish day-dreams in

KNOW THYSELF 

which they visualize themselves as the leading men and women in a spectacular show. Nearly everyone does that, although the show visualized by the superior mind is usually not so spectacular as that dreamed of by the inferior mind. The superior person does not permit his fancy to be in serious conflict with his daily wellbeing; he knows the value of time; he is very conscious of the progress he has made; he looks both waysbackward as well as forward.

Superior children are keenly, aware of time. They regret sickness or other misfortunes that prevent their being able to finish high school at the average age of members of their class. They are pleased if they are younger than the average when they finish-and most superior boys and girls are younger than the average. 

It is not unusual here in the South to see negroesand white persons too-who often do not know the day of the week, the month, their ages, and the ages of their children if they have any. Time is seemingly a hazy, difficult problem for them and they solve the problem by ignoring it.

People who are lazy or mediocre mentally are frequently heard to say they do not have time to do worthwhile things, in addition to their day's work, that other persons having the same occupation do. This is a threadbare, hackneyed excuse and not the truth in the matter; and like most excuses, it would better be left unmade. 

5. Persons having superior mentality see the necessity for self-denial and they do not rebel against practicing it. 

COMMENT. Inferior and mediocre persons, unless under discipline, are prone to be controlled by transient desires and pleasures. This is due very largely to a

THE STUDY 

29 lack of a worth-while goal, to their not knowing values, and to their not being time-conscious. 

Many persons who have not risen above mediocrity and a very few persons who are richly endowed with mental qualities can and do sneer at self-denial. But the fact remains that about ninety-five persons out of every hundred who have accomplished any outstanding work have seemingly had much experience with this phase of self-discipline. It may be said in passing that self-denial is too often thought of in terms of money, but self-denial has to do more with the conservation of time, the restriction of personal interests, and a rigid control of the social instinct. 

Self-denial, to a person having superior mentality, is not a grievous hardship as is often thought, for this person is usually interested in what he considers an important work or undertaking rather than in transient pleasures. It might be a heart-breaking disappointment to a nit-wit not to be able to go to a movie or to a bridge party, or not to be able to listen to the radio, when a superior person would be bored and annoyed and would think it time wasted if he had to do those things. 

Persons having superior mentality very seldom seek sympathy. Creative minds-such as authors, artists, and inventors are not prone to complain about their hardships unless they are compelled to postpone too many meals. Being conscious of making progress in reaching a goal is of far more importance-to the person who is on his way-than the hardships and selfdenial involved.

Self-denial as an aid to personal improvement may be claimed as a virtue almost entirely by the inferior and the mediocre. They need self-denial to help prevent their being either economic parasites or social nuisances or both.

Self-denial to help somebody else may be a virtue of the first rank or it may be only encouraging dependency

KNOW THYSELF 

in the person being helped. Parents sometimes deny themselves all but the necessities of life in order that their children may be comfortably clothed, properly fed, and sent to school. Young men and women often deny themselves nearly all social pleasures and many of life's comforts in order to take care of their parents or other relatives. And physicians see self-denial and sacrifices made in families where there is chronic illness or invalidism that would melt the heart of the hardest cynic. Self-denial as a religious exercise and self-denial as a form of self-martyrdom-for example, mothers who are slaves to their families-have a different motive from self-denial as an aid to reaching a personal goal.

It is almost useless for parents, parsons, and pedagogs to talk self-denial to children and young adults of inferior or mediocre mentality who do not have a definite, feasible goal in mind. If a goal is set up and kept ever in mind the matter of self-denial will take care of itself. It is necessary in the study of behavior to put first things first and in this matter the goal comes first. 

As with many other intelligent types of behavior, the superior mind uses self-denial voluntarily whereas mediocre and inferior minds must have its merits pointed out to them and then they must be urged or disciplined into using it. 

6. Persons having superior mentality have noticeably good ability to understand even though they may not always be understood.

COMMENT. There is no lack of opportunity to observe in other persons their ability to understand. Inability to understand a new idea may be due solely to ignorance. A person may not understand a thing because it is entirely new to him, that is, the person may

THE STUDY 

31 have no knowledge in his mind of a subject to which and with which he can relate the new information. It is not, for example, discreditable to a person who knows only English not to be able to understand words, phrases, and sentences from foreign languages that careless or affected writers are prone to use too frequently. 

But inability to understand worth-while things where ignorance is not the cause is usually due to mental inferiority rather than to a lack of interest, although the latter must not be overlooked as a possible cause. It is not enough to call a seemingly dull person-child or adult-a "dumb Dora" or a "dull-wit." Stupidity, dullness, inability to understand should be taken seriously, especially in children because of their vocation later in life, and in adults where useful service is expected or demanded of them. The best way to deal with disappointment in other persons is to avoid it; and one of the best ways to avoid it, as well as to prevent it, is to know mental qualities.

Not infrequently a child or an adult is thought to have good understanding because he has a good memory, but such reasoning is not always true. Good understanding depends upon acuteness of perception, facility in integration, and the ability to see relationship, rather than a good memory. A fairly common statement, to the effect that some persons learn slowly but that they learn a thing well, is literally true. Seeing the relationship of ideas or facts is most important, and sometimes the mind grasps relationship slowly.

The person who inherently lacks understanding is often no more interested in what is going on about him than if he were not present. He is satisfied with his own world of thoughts-and a small world it is too. 

In persons in the lower mental brackets, for example one's house servants, one notes the frequency with which they do not understand one another in their conversation; hence the frequent "Huh?" This is due in part,

KNOW THYSELF 

however, to the lack of clearness and completeness of their statements, as well as to lack of interest in what is being said. It may be observed, though, that when the master of the house speaks, their understanding is a great deal better for their interest and attention are at their best.

One can observe daily the lack of interest in understanding manifested by a large number of persons. 

Many persons are seemingly satisfied to know only of or about a thing rather than to know the thing thoroughly. And this is true of things they should knowthings that it would be to their advantage to know well. 

But if one asks them why they do not even want to understand, the subject appears to be as remote to them as interest in conjugating a Greek verb would be to a waitress.

Ability to understand does not have the attention given to it by the layman that it warrants. This is especially true with respect to children who do not understand because of ignorance due to lack of experience, and to adults who do not understand because of a lack of inherent ability. Too often all that is done is to make excuses for the child and to condemn or to ridicule the adult.

Ridicule has no place in personal social contact. It may be clever but it is neither pleasant nor useful. A child of superior mentality does not readily forget a sarcastic statement made about it. Careful instruction and firm discipline will get better results than ridicule in dealing with persons-both children and adultswho do not understand well. 

7. Persons having superior mentality, in order to maintain their own ideals and integrity, readily refuse to follow the ways of the herd.

THE STUDY 

333 COMMENT. Both children and adults who are mental weaklings are unhappy if they can not do as the herd does; they do not want to appear to be different; with them, to conform, to follow, is the path of least resistance. They are somewhat like tiny drops of mercury that unite and lose their identity as soon as they touch a larger quantity of mercury. Inferior and mediocre persons sometimes give more thought to following the herd, to keeping up with the Joneses, than they give to their own welfare.

In general the ethical rules of the herd are right. We should get nowhere without rules for the protection of property rights, life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness. But social customs, habits, ways, and rules are often plainly stupid and are injurious to many of the persons who conform to them. It takes intelligence to be right-even to try to be right; but cattle and sheep can and do form herds; it is their normal social behavior.

Social standards of the herd are often low. Many persons over twenty years of age could keep out of bread-lines and flophouses if they had stuck to oldfashioned ideas of personal economy, industry, and intelligent limitation of their responsibilities instead of yielding to the desire to follow the herd and to have a good time. Many men and women who are back of the bars, looking out, discarded their ideals and integrity to try the easy way. They failed. Many-literally millions of men and women-fail and suffer embarrassment, humiliation, and regret because they were more interested in following the ways of the herd than they were in maintaining the standards taught them by their parents and the clergy.

The three major instincts vary greatly in influencing the behavior of different persons. There are persons of good mentality, as well as millions of them of poor mentality, who are controlled largely by the social in-

KNOW THYSELF 

stinct. They like many people; they like to mix and mingle with people; and they often select occupations in which it is necessary to deal extensively with people. 

Persons of good mentality can be fond of the herd and yet remain uninfluenced by detrimental herd ways.' 

Babbittry and back-slapping are as normal for Babbitts as social exclusiveness is for the intellectuals. The greatest difference between Babbitts and intellectuals is not found so much in the quality of their intellects as in the thickness of their skins. 

The desire to be of the crowd or apart from the crowd is a relative thing. The inferior are usually unhappy if they are not with people, while hermits are more or less unhappy if they are required to be with people. The intelligent adult is inherently selective with respect to companionship and critical of herd ways. 

A superior child is likely to manifest non-conformity to certain ways of the herd by the time it is six years of age, and this feeling grows with the years. By the time mental maturity is reached his individualism may be so strong that the desire to be different is in his mind. daily. 

Does the superior child or adult reject the undesirable ways of the herd because he fears they would harm him, or because he fears that other persons would think of him as being trashy? Possibly both, but probably neither. The superior person knows, or thinks he knows, that his own standard and ideas are best for him; and, having integrity and courage, he adheres to his own ideas and ways. He gives very little thought to the herd's opinion of his behavior. The inferior and mediocre are often admonished to resist evil; the superior are prone to control their desires voluntarily and intelligently.

Men vary greatly in their behavior even when they have had the same environment; this is because nature is more influential than nurture.

THE STUDY 

35 8. Persons having superior mentality are nearly always language-conscious; they try to express themselves with accuracy and effectiveness at all times.

COMMENT. The language more oft proclaims the man than the apparel does. One may see a manual laborer or an artisan who has not had many advantages either at home in his childhood or at school who tries carefully to use good language. Sooner or later he is likely to be foreman of the gang-if he is not at the time; later still he may own the business. 

It is no credit to psychologists, physicians, parents, pedagogs, and parsons, not to be able to pick a winner until after he wins. This suggestion is worth considering: pick the child that is language-conscious. 

A person thinks with words; thoughts are nearly always expressed with words. As the ability to think develops, the vocabularly and the art of speech develop with it. Superior persons usually have an extensive vocabulary and marked facility in using it. The manner of speech may be fluent, flowery, and ornate like that of Edward Bulwer-Lytton or it may be marked by simplicity and accuracy like that of John Stuart Mill.

Social climbers and the newly rich may often be detected by their faulty language. If one has the money with which to do so, one can buy a fashionable wardrobe, a swank motor car, and move into an exclusive apartment building-all in two or three days. But one can not change his language to fit his new surroundings; his bad grammar, poor pronunciation, and worse rhetoric can neither be hidden nor discarded even though he may discontinue inhaling his soup and saucering his coffee.

Persons with inferior or mediocre minds who have had few advantages in their childhood may continue to use such alley-rat expressions as "Huh?" "Uh-huh," and "Huh-uh," throughout their entire lives; they will

KNOW THYSELF 

not change voluntarily. On the other hand enlisted men in the Army and the Navy discontinue the use of such expressions in speaking to commissioned officers as soon as they enter military service. So too student nurses and graduate nurses, unless they are hopelessly dull and stupid, do not use these expressions in speaking to physicians and patients. These two groups are cited to show the effect of rigid discipline. 

Persons with superior mentality use words-not grunts and meaningless noises. If a thing may be said well in six words they use six and not sixteen; they do not repeat, chatter, gabble, and recite a monolog. 

A person who is language-conscious can see improvement in his language from year to year. He may never attain the perfection of a Bierce or a Mencken, a Frank or a Fosdick; but discarding bad usage and acquiring good usage is a constant source of pleasure to him. The improvement of one's language will be recognized by only a very few persons; it is a secret pleasure but nevertheless a real one.

Language-consciousness is the result of a feeling of artistry in which words are the medium of expression. 

Grammatical accuracy, proper pronunciation, the choice of words, and the form of the sentence are only the means to an end. They are the equivalent of the painter's brush, colors, palette, and canvas.

Efforts to improve one's speech are commendable although sometimes amusing. One may in the South hear an aspiring social-climber affecting the pronunciation of the stage, the Bostonian, or the Oxonian-heard at the cinema or over the radio-yet continuing to say "I taken" and "You-all."

The purist, usually a pedagog, may or may not be language-conscious. He may know many language rules after years of teaching, he may be a persistent quibbler, and still be without artistry in his own speech. 

The excuse given by many persons, who think they

THE STUDY 

37 are above the average intellectually, that they have permitted their language to become faulty because of their daily association with the inferior and the illiterate, is just one more foolish excuse. Language-integrity, to persons who are language-conscious, should not be more easily broken than moral, financial, or intellectual integrity. Speaking in an intelligible way to the inferior and the illiterate does not necessitate one's using the bad language of the persons spoken to. 

9. Persons having superior mentality seldom are bored by being alone; they are likely, however, as much as social diplomacy will permit, to manifest intolerance of other persons who are bores. 

COMMENT. A feeling of boredom comes into the conscious mind when it no longer has either interesting or profitable thoughts. The inferior mind usually seeks superficial, unimportant, interesting things-not profitable things. The superior mind seeks profitable things primarily and things merely interesting only as a diversion. 

A person who is neither interesting nor profitable, mentally, is likely to become a bore to the superior mind if intimate association is continued more than a few minutes at a time. On the other hand, persons having superior mentality may be mutually enjoyable as companions for hours at a time because their association is both interesting and profitable.

Superior minds nearly always want to learn something. In the upper mental brackets the lust for learning is apparently insatiable. Even the recreations of the superior are more enjoyable if they have an intellectual phase-either learning or skill. 

Superior persons are intelligently selfish; they are also distinctively time-conscious. The instinct for self-

KNOW THYSELF 

preservation as well as reason bids them avoid timekilling bores. Many persons restrict their social life, refuse to join organizations, or to attend their meetings in case they joined them earlier in life, because they prefer to meet bores one at a time instead of in herds.

Superior persons, especially those having creative minds, must pass much time alone. They usually plan their lives accordingly and carry out the plan. A scientist can pass many hours a day in his laboratory or, if a geologist or a botanist, in fields and forest, alone, and enjoy every minute of the time.

One observes that superior children often like to be alone. They do not seek the company of other children indiscriminately. Other children take the initiative and seek them, for superior children are usually both timid and attractive. Superior children try to protect themselves from bores and other pests just as superior adults do. 

Inferior persons are not well endowed either with ability or will to learn. Their mental activity is largely a matter of feeling; they want to be amused—entertained; they are prone to talk incessantly of what they like or of what they do not like. Most of their likes and dislikes have to do with people. This fact may be readily observed in overhearing the conversation of servants and others of their mental grade.

The inferior mind, not being interested in learning worth-while things, seeks diversion for its feelings. Because of this, inferior persons seek changes, excitement, and amusement. These things they usually find only in association with other persons.

Inferior persons, as well as many of the mediocre grade, can not remain alone without developing a feeling of boredom. That is because their minds can not produce interesting and profitable thoughts either with or without suitable reading matter or other intellectual occupation. Companionship is almost imperative, its

THE STUDY 

39 desire a passion, with a large percentage of inferior and mediocre persons. In fact their desire for companionship is seemingly as strong as is the desire of a superior person to get rid of a conversational bore. 

10. Persons having superior mentality do not often regret that part of their past over which they had control; they contemplate the future with wary optimism. 

COMMENT. Men and women who are failures in life are prone to talk much of things they have left undone that they ought to have done, and of things they have done that they ought not to have done. They have one of the cardinal defects of Episcopalians but probably do not know it.

Superior persons solve their problems so well from year to year that they have comparatively little to regret. Their daydreams of fame, wealth, and powerto be attained in the future-are of less importance to them than the fact that they must live intelligently and wholesomely in the present. Superior persons have a way of dealing with necessary things first. 

Children and adults with childish minds are prone to allow their imaginations to run wild with respect to the future; the past has taught them little or nothing. They build great fortunes, attain fame, glory, and power in the world of make-believe, without using very much reason as to how these things are done in the world of reality. Naturally these daydreams are not realized; then comes regret followed by the usual excuses of the habitual failure.

In judging minds it is well to note that the poor mind usually manifests itself more plainly and more completely than the good mind does; that is, it is easier to recognize a fool than a sage. The person who habitually speaks of a hard-luck past at once brands himself as

KNOW THYSELF 

being mentally inferior; there are but few exceptions to the rule. Pride alone, if nothing else, prevents superior persons from talking indiscriminately about the things in their life that should not have been. 

Persons with superior mentality take stock daily. 

They do not travel the road of life blindly; they know every turn in the road, every hill, every rough place, every pitfall; they do not bore and annoy their acquaintances by telling them over and over about the foolish, childish errors they have made in the past.

Human minds as a whole are somewhat like plantlife as a whole. Most plants reach maturity at a low level; some attain the height and stability of bushes, some become small trees; but there are comparatively few giants of the forest. Superior minds enriched by the past grow as sturdy trees; they are aware of their strength and their stature; they resent the idea of being weeds, bushes, or clinging vines.

The good mind, in looking over its past, finds that it is also somewhat like a tree in its growth and decay. 

A few of the minor branches that a little tree puts out during the first year or two of its life die and drop off early. They served their purpose but are no longer useful. After a tree is five or six years old almost none of its main branches die, but from scores to hundreds of twigs and lesser branches die every year, even though the tree is healthy and well nourished. Good minds have a way of discarding many useless and harmful thoughts and ideas of minor importance. Then in old age, in both men and trees, decay exceeds growth. 

Larger branches die, one by one; and in man senility is likely to accompany physical infirmity. 

All that we have of value comes from the past: our good inherited qualities, the joys of childhood, the pleasures of our school days, recollection of the different persons we have loved, the ripening of our judgment, the development of our appreciation of the best

THE STUDY 

41 in life. No; the intelligent person should be pleased with his past rather than regret it. And at the close of life he should also be able to say with Saint Paul: "I have fought a good fight, I have finished my course, I have kept the faith."

If one asks a score of men of superior mentality, ranging in ages from the late 'teens to fifty years, what their basic idea of their future is, one or the other of the two following answers will be received. The younger ones will usually answer that they intend to make their own future, to control it definitely, to shape their own destiny. The older ones, not so enthusiastic about the matter, are more likely to speak of being ready for the opportunities that come their way. 

11. Persons having superior mentality are careful about speaking with dogmatic finality for they know that appearances are often deceiving, that truth is only relative, and that change is constant.

COMMENT. Small minds are prone to make dogmatic statements that cover a large territory. This practice is due not only to ignorance but also because they think that if a thing is said positively-either with or without the addition of profanity-and exaggerated grossly, it will be believed. Because of this practice small minds are likely to be doubted and distrusted by superior persons.

Superior minds judge a statement by its reasonableness and not by sentiment, emotion, wish-thinking, and prejudice. Persons with inferior or mediocre minds have four fairly common, fairly constant, subjects for conversation: other persons' motives and behavior, economics, politics, and religion. These things they settle with finality every day and sometimes all day; and then the next day and the next, on far into the night, with

KNOW THYSELF 

tireless tongues they settle them again and again. It is apparently a glorious mental state to be in to feel that one's opinions are infallible, if one may judge by the number of persons who feel that way. 

A well-balanced mind appears to be the product of good natural endowment rather than of education, for one sometimes sees laboring men and artisans that have conspicuously good mental balance relative to matters within their ken; that is, they see the truth or falsity in a statement far more readily than many men do who are college graduates. An educated fool remains a fool regardless; this simple fact may be observed every day.

There are persons who are so ignorant, and who know it, that they have very few definite opinions about any worth-while matter; and there are many persons who are so ignorant, and who do not know it, that they dogmatize and speak with finality on nearly any subject that is mentioned. The mentally superior person, however, is prone to recognize his limitations; he knows when he knows and he knows when he does not know; he usually speaks accordingly. A superior person may often say more by keeping silent than a fool can say with hours of talking.

There are few things that mark one so plainly as being mentally inferior and socially stupid as the habit of announcing dogmatic opinions indiscriminately on every subject that arises in conversation. The inferior person unwittingly advertises his inferiority, scores and scores of time daily, in this way.

At the present time one hears much about rethinking traditions, clear thinking, straight thinking, and thinking things through. Clever expressions, these, but of no interest to the inferior and the mediocre. Millions of people talk much but think little. They appear to have a vague idea that a thing can be talked through. 

Inferior and mediocre persons who talk a great deal, and who give opinions freely, use very little discrim-

THE STUDY 

43 ination in judging the value of statements. For example, in medical work we see ignorant persons who put Grandma's statements on an equal footing with the professional statements of the most learned physician of their community. Doctor Whoozis says this, but 

Grandma says that; and the poor patient does not know which one to believe.

The causes or motives that restrain one's advicegiving and opinion-airing desires vary. The superior person voluntarily restrains his desire to talk because he feels he has nothing worthwhile to say on a subject or, that if he had, it would be useless to say it. The mediocre person is prone to restrain his desire to talk because he fears to be thought ignorant or careless socially. And the inferior person restrains his desire to talk only because of fear of reproof or of some other motive having to do with self-preservation. 

12. Persons having superior mentality, except a portion of those having creative minds such as authors, inventors, and artists, voluntarily follow a daily routine closely; in fact they often appear to be slaves to routine. 

They eat, sleep, work, walk, and read with much regularity.

COMMENT. Inferior persons, unless forced by necessity to be regular in their habits, are likely to be very irregular. They go to bed at any time between dusk and dawn, get up when they have to, work when they must, eat when they can, rest when forced to, play when the boss is not looking, and are generally prone to be unreliable, irresponsible, and unsteady. 

According to a newspaper report Einstein was asked to attend his daughter's wedding and he promised to do so only on condition that he would not be kept away from his study more than thirty minutes.

KNOW THYSELF 

Both Bulwer-Lytton and Huxley, even though in public life a great deal, adhered rigidly to a daily routine when circumstances permitted their doing so. 

Carlyle, who had over fifty years of productive life, walked and rode for exercise. He did not enjoy games. 

Being Scotch he did not even waste time. When at home and that was nearly all the time-he too followed a rigid daily routine. 

It is said that Schopenhauer took his daily walk with such punctuality that one could set one's watch accurately by knowing the time the famous philosopher was accustomed to pass a given point. 

Superior persons follow a routine for several reasons, notably: to avoid social pests; to protect their own thinking from unprofitable interruptions; to avoid wasting time; and to permit both body and mind to work by habit as much as possible. 

A person having a creative mind may be very irregular in his habits. There are several reasons for this, the most important of which is that creative work is very exhausting and the mind must have a period of rest. Besides, the Muse is a fickle being that comes and sits on the shoulder only at irregular intervals. Also the creative mind has periods of willful laziness; it can work and produce if forced but resents beginning the task.

Persons with creative minds need much uninterrupted leisure, freedom from responsibility, and appreciative, intelligent companions. Many persons with creative minds need a guardian-someone to protect them from intruders, pests, and bores; someone to make them eat and sleep regularly; someone to make them protect their bodies, for the creative mind is usually hard on the body that houses it.

Fortunately many men who have creative minds have a Guardian Angel, or even several angels of this kind.

It was so with Scott, Carlyle, Goethe, Laennec, Steven-

THE STUDY 

45 son, Doctor Johnson, Edison, Anatole France, and many more. Not so, unfortunately, with Burns, Francis Thompson, Poe, and others.

It is an excellent point in the consideration of a person's mental qualities to know what he does when he is not engaged at his daily work; also it is important to know if he is regular or irregular in his daily habits. 

The facts in the case may be easily learned, for the inferior are usually very talkative about what they do outside of working hours; indeed, they are prone to advertise their inferiority more than is good for them. On the other hand, either by direct questioning or by observation, the superior will be found to be regular in their daily routine. 

13. Persons having superior mentality are interested in and they discuss both abstract and concrete subjects in an impersonal, detached way.

COMMENT. Inferior and mediocre minds are prone to think of themselves, talk of themselves, and thrust their own personal feelings, interests, and prejudices into conversations and discussions of all kinds. This mental and social defect is found in nearly all neurotics regardless of their mental quality, in persons who have had more favorable publicity than they have ability, and in those unfortunate persons of both sexes past thirty years of age commonly recognized as failures.

Excessive subjectiveness is apparently an inherent characteristic rather than an acquired habit; it can not be entirely discontinued for one sees men and women of fair social knowledge who persist in the boresome practice of talking about themselves regardless of ridicule and reproof directed toward their defect. They have an uncontrollable desire-almost a passion in its intensity, to express opinions in which they bring in their per-

KNOW THYSELF 

sonal feelings, interests, or approval, or else the lack of these things. If, for example, one mentions a thing as remote as the French Academy, its origin, or the whys and wherefores of its membership and its activities, the highly subjective person may be expected to thrust himself into the discussion and tell why he approves or does not approve the Forty Immortals.

Highly subjective persons are usually long on personal opinions and short on intelligence; they are oversupplied with egotism and self-complacency and undersupplied with self-knowledge due to self-analysis. 

Really superior persons may sometimes talk excessively and even boresomely about their work because of their zeal and interest in it; but there is a great deal of difference in talking about one's work and in talking about oneself and one's family.

Children and childish adults are interested in things, primarily, that are of interest to them personally; and next to their personal interests very naturally come their family interests. It is not strange, then, that with millions of people in the lower mental brackets, conversation and discussions have to do with personal rather than with impersonal matters. Ninety-nine 

Americans will talk about Boston baked beans, learnedly, foolishly, or jestingly, where there is one who is interested in Boston as a city. Boston, to the provincial, is symbolic of beans, not brains.

Most superior children are seen to have an impersonal, detached interest in many subjects; they can, for example, talk about other children's toys without disparagement or envy. Both children and adults who are abnormally subjective use the words I, me, mine, and my, over and over, scores of times daily, whereas, the superior person consciously avoids the frequent use of these words.

The highly subjective person is an interesting mental study; that is, interesting for a little while. As a com-

THE STUDY 

47 panion he is likely to become an intolerable bore to superior persons, for he is seemingly unaware of his bad mental habit and therefore has no desire to discontinue it.

It is a good lead in discovering the good mind to note if a person talks of worth-while things in an impersonal, detached way and carefully avoids talking about himself in relation to the subject being discussed. 

As with many other evidences of mental superiority this rule is often made more conspicuous by its breach than by its observance. 

14. The scientific mind is richly endowed with curiosity, persistency, a keen sense of observation, and wellbalanced judgment.

COMMENT. The scientific mind wants to know why; it persists in its desire and its efforts to find out why until a satisfactory conclusion is reached or until it considers the subject unknowable at the time. 

The scientific mind is looking for fragments or elements of truth in order that it may understand better a larger truth. The scientist, especially in biology, knows that aside from simple mathematics there are comparatively few fool-proof, infallible rules and conclusions in the world of thinking; he also knows that life in all its forms and manifestations is variable. 

The scientific mind is by its very nature both agnostic and sceptical; it is not a mind that willingly accepts what has been said or taught on a subject merely because the traditional teaching is old. The scientist-for example, a scholarly physician-wants his text-books to be revised, new, and up to date. He is not satisfied to know what the greatest minds in medicine believed, practiced, and taught ten years ago; he wants to know

KNOW THYSELF 

what they believe, practice, and teach at the present time.

The truly scientific mind does not have to be forced to work any more than a person richly endowed with talent for music has to be forced to play or to sing. 

Ability gives an interest that does not cease till long after middle life; all the scientific mind needs is an opportunity to work. Indeed, many scientists are so thoroughly engrossed in their work that they sometimes injure their health by too much work and not enough play.

The scientific mind is prone to guard well its intellectual integrity. This is probably the reason that scientists, except for their idealism, usually are not religious men; also the reason why they discard former beliefs and practices in scientific matters with as much indifference—even joy—as they feel in discarding outworn wearing apparel.

Most laymen are unaware of the element of persistency in the scientific mind. They do not know that scientific progress is a growth made largely by trialand-error method, and that most of the scientific discoveries are the result of months, and in some instances of years, of study, tests, experiments, and observations. 

However, a few valuable scientific facts have been discovered accidentally, for example, the X-ray by Roentgen. It has been said that Coolidge worked six years to make tungsten, an intractable mineral, ductile. His discovery revolutionized the incandescent lamp industry. It is said also that Ehrlich tried 606 times before he found a satisfactory form of arsenic-the product is known as Salvarsan-to use in the treatment of lues, and that he then tried 308 times more to produce Neosalvarsan.

The power of original observation that the truly scientific mind has is a gift. The non-scientific mind can,

THE STUDY 

49 in a measure, be taught to see what others have discovered but it seldom makes original observations. 

There are comparatively few scientific minds of the first rank and, unfortunately, they are not all engaged in scientific work. Not only is this true, but the socalled scientific professions such, for example, as medicine, engineering, and chemistry, contain many men who do not have scientific minds. These unfortunate men are mis-fits, tolerated because of ignorance by the public whom they serve, and piously endured, pitied, and protected by their colleagues through sympathy and the knowledge that it is hard to get out of a profession after one goes into it.

It is unfortunate that high-school pedagogs can not recognize the occasional lad with scientific bent among their many pupils and more unfortunate still that deans of scientific schools can not see the lack of the scientific mind in many of their applicants for admission to the freshman class in time to keep them out of scientific professions. 

15. Persons having superior mentality are prone to have discriminating opinions and judgment—if they have any at all-in regard to Goodness, Beauty, and Truth.

COMMENT. Goodness in this sense has to do with the quality of social behavior. If a person is harmless, that is, if he does no evil thing to his fellow man, his social conduct is said to be good. His being harmless may be due to his lack of age, to old age, to physical or mental incapacity, or to a lack of opportunity to be harmful. 

Being harmless is probably the lowest form of personal goodness.

If a person does something to please another person his behavior is usually said to be good. In the final

KNOW THYSELF 

analysis such behavior is about fifty per cent bad. This is the type of goodness in which one so often finds a sinister motive in the background or on-coming parasitism in the foreground. Goodness with an ax to grind or goodness that encourages dependency does not bear an intelligent label; and of these two evils called goodness, that which encourages dependency is by far the worst. For the encouragement and condoning of parasitism and dependency in small things as well as in important matters, from early childhood to old age, is one of the most harmful of our social practices. 

If a person does something to help someone who is working toward an honorable goal to help himself, he has done the best of good acts.

There is quality in goodness; and it may be observed that the better the mind the better will be its ideas and activities in altruistic matters. Careful observation of superior minds will prove this assertion.
