The appreciation of Beauty is a gift that is not limited to the upper mental brackets. Small children, both boys and girls, appreciate beauty in flowers, ribbons, marbles, doll dresses, and bright-colored pictures.

A group of the most ignorant cotton-field niggers can both create and appreciate good simple music. 

On the other hand Dr. Samuel Johnson never looked at Sir Joshua Reynolds' pictures although he spent many hours, year after year, in the Reynolds home.

Dr. Johnson had no appreciation of mountain scenery and he claimed to have heard music only once that he liked-and that was a funeral march! But Herbert

Spencer, Macaulay, and Goethe were all richly endowed with appreciation of Goodness, Beauty, and Truth.

There is little beauty outside of nature or the imitation of nature, and what there is is usually created by a superior mind; for example, it takes a great mind, a 

Wren or a Cram, to plan a cathedral.

Very few persons are greatly interested in truth for

THE STUDY 

51 truth's sake. Most persons are interested largely in their three major instincts and their daydreams built on them. Very few persons want their present beliefs disturbed; they would rather defend the opinions, ideas, and practices they have, no matter how poor they are, than to try to change them or to submit to anyone else's changing them. There is very little lust for learning in mankind as a whole, and that which is found is in the upper mental brackets.

Truth for truth's sake about things, principles, beliefs, practices, facts, and traditions is the special field of scientists and philosophers; and there are comparatively few scientific and philosophic minds. 

16. There are two basic elements in religion-parasitism or dependency and idealism.

COMMENT. The religion of children-if they may be said to have a religion-of all inferior, and of many mediocre adults is largely, though not entirely, parasitical. Their god is a glorified Santa Claus who is asked and expected to forgive wrongs committed against their fellow man, to heal disease, to feed, clothe and shelter them, to be on the side of their troops in battle, to calm raging storms on land and sea, to punish their enemies, to make them successful in business, to gratify and fulfill their wishes and whims both reasonable and unreasonable, and to solve their many problems that they themselves are unable to solve. The religion of inferior and mediocre persons of today is still very largely a desire for rewards both for the here and now and the hereafter; with some, the element of escape enters into it-also of the here and now and the hereafter.

The inferior mind being unable to solve many of its problems needs to believe in a god that is omnipotent,

KNOW THYSELF 

omniscient, and omnipresent. Not only that but their god needs assistants for small details, hence, in some religions there are saints to pray to for special needsa saint for each need. Simple minds need simple plans. 

Superior minds seemingly know less, at least they talk less definitely, about their god than inferior minds do; in fact as the mind increases in knowledge the 

Creator becomes more indefinite. On the other hand superior minds are prone to have very definite religious ideals which, like Emerson, they guard carefully. 

Among persons having superior mentality religion may be thought of as a feeling of obligation and devotion to an ideal. The ideal conceived and the means for attaining it vary according to the quality of the mind, the age of the person, and, in a measure, to his environment. Idealism is both personal and social. Superior minds think much of social idealism. Inferior minds with their endless don'ts are inclined to stress the matter of being good, whereas the superior mind is prone to accentuate the importance of doing good. 

Since minds vary so greatly any just judgment of religious motives and practices must be based on understanding and appreciation of the varying needs of a great variety of minds. Inferior minds may have as much need-therefore as much right-to Pollyanna, orthodoxy, infallible authority, Fundamentalism, and all their works, as the intellectuals have to Humanism. 

The clash, the controversy, comes when one group denies the other its rights. Holy Rollers and Unitarians are a long way apart in mental endowment and development.

Minds vary as greatly in their religious capabilities and capacities as they do in scientific or musical capabilities and capacities. When adults say they are not religious it may mean that, being able to solve their own problems, they are not interested in a childish, dependent religion; that they are deficient in idealism;

THE STUDY 

53 or that they can not accept the theories and practices in vogue in their locality for attaining the Good Life. 

In the upper mental brackets intellectual difficulties are seemingly more important in keeping men and women out of religious organizations than moral difficulties are. 

17. Persons having superior mentality are prone to examine traditional beliefs and practices intelligently and if they are found to be harmful or useless, they carefully discard them.

COMMENT. Inferior and mediocre persons are prone to discard useless and even harmful beliefs and practices slowly if at all. Regardless of where they live they are often controlled by signs, magic, mysteries, fears, taboos, and irrational ways handed down from one generation to another. This is not due so much to ignorance and lack of opportunity for enlightenment as it is to inherent mental inferiority and either a convenient or a stupid respect for tradition. 

Many farmers know no more about farming at fifty than they did at twenty. Many cooks make as poor biscuits at forty as they did at twenty-and some of them are unbelievably bad! Many persons of both sexes use as poor language at fifty as they did at fifteen. Many men and women with sufficient means to dress becomingly know no more about what to wear or how to wear it at forty than they did at twenty.

Many men past middle life actually boast that they have always voted a straight political ticket-Republior Democratic, it matters not-since they were twenty-one. Surely nothing to boast about in most cases. Many parents fear their children are headed straight to hell if they join a church that caters to more

KNOW THYSELF 

intelligent people than the one does in which they were reared.

Grandpa carried a potato or a buckeye in his pocket to ward off rheumatism because his father did. Grandma believes that a bag of asafoetida worn on a string around a child's neck will prevent its having "them ketchin' diseases." Until a few years ago Aunt Susan wrote to Miss Spinkham for medical advice-and got it too although she died in 1883.

Many men who can hardly provide the necessaries of life for themselves marry and try to justify their act because they have heard many times that it is cheaper to keep two than one. In such cases it frequently happens that more ignorance brings parents and offspring -that should never have been-as an additional load on the charities and tax-payers. Many women marry crude, shiftless, degenerate men and give as their excuse for doing so that they intend to reform them. The divorce court gets some new cash customers. Many 

British still believe in the biologic superiority of royal blood regardless of the fact that numbers of their sovereigns, especially the Hanovers, as well as other members of their royal families were either insane or were plainly mental weaklings.

Superior minds have a mental house-cleaning frequently. Inferior minds seldom clean house; they have neither the will to learn nor the desire to change; they passively accept tradition rather than think independently. The scientific mind, especially, gives up outworn beliefs and practices readily, even gladly. What a pity religious and political leaders could not do the same! But if one twists and deforms a tree during the first ten years of its life it can not overcome all its deformity.

On the other hand there are many persons who change their beliefs and practices to take on something new even though the new is no better than the old.

THE STUDY 

55 

Changes should be for betterment, not for newness; this is the way of the superior mind. A thing is not necessarily bad because it is old, nor is it necessarily better because it is new. 

18. Persons having superior mentality have vision and imagination together with enough resourcefulness to realize in a satisfactory way the needful things they visualized. 

COMMENT. All persons have day-dreams; in fact, day-dreaming is one of life's greatest pleasures. But there is much difference between the day-dreams of the inferior mind and those of the superior mind. As minds increase in quality they have fewer plans and day-dreams; their plans and day-dreams are simpler and more practical, hence, they become correspondingly more attainable.

It is characteristic of many superior persons not to make known their plans until considerable progress has been made in carrying them out. They do not count their chickens before they hatch. On the other hand persons with poor minds are sometimes prone to talk much of things they might do-might do if somebody else does this or does not do that. Always an if-a loophole.

Secrecy on the part of superior persons in making and achieving plans is due more often to the embarrassment that would follow widely known failure than to fear of interference or harm from other persons. As a rule, the better the mind the more seriously it takes personal failure. The school-boy who almost won first place or a prize regrets failure a hundred times more than the poorest student in the class does. Ambition may sometimes cause much unhappiness.

KNOW THYSELF 

Forethought-itself an evidence of mental superiority-makes one aware that a thing should be done, while vision and imagination go farther and determine what may or what can be done; and resourcefulness calls the visualized thing into being. Minds vary greatly in this matter. Millions of people have almost no forethought; other millions of a little higher type have forethought but their vision and imagination are ill-defined. 

Only those in the upper mental brackets have ability both to see and to do the thing or things that should be done. 

It is somewhat depressing to an observer of good judgment and twenty years or more experience to see children grow to physical maturity and to note that so many of them mature mentally at a low level. In the 'teen age a goodly number of children appear to have bright prospects, but as the years pass by more and more of them are seen to make no progress. They seemingly were born to be mediocre and some of them, unfortunately, appear to retrograde so they could not even be classified as average. Heredity, rather than environment, is the dominant factor in this matter as one may readily see by observing the varying amount of progress made by children in the same family where the environment was alike for all. 

To competent observers most children show before reaching adult life whether they have intelligent vision and imagination or whether they are only visionary. 

The observer has only to note carefully if the child voluntarily does well the little, seemingly unimportant duties of the day. That is the essential point in the matter. If a child has sufficient vision to see that a thing should be done and sufficient voluntary interest and determination to do it, one may be reasonably certain that it will voluntarily do bigger and better things in adult life. If, on the other hand, a child can not see that a thing should be done, and if he must be forced

THE STUDY 

57 to do a worth-while thing, it is not likely that discipline born of necessity in later life will change him greatly. 

At best he will probably not rise above mediocrity; at worst he will be an habitual parasite. 

19. Persons having superior mentality are prone to be intelligently both realistic and idealistic; that is, they see things as they are and they see things as they should be. 

COMMENT. A story is being told at this time, perhaps due to the building of the Cathedral Church of St. 

John the Divine in New York or the National Cathedral in Washington, relative to two stone cutters at work. The first man was asked what he was doing and he replied that he was cutting stone for a certain daily wage. The second man was asked the same question and he replied that he was helping to build a great cathedral.

High real-estate taxes, urban life, and hard times are depriving millions of our people of their idealistic rights. Men and women have an inherent right to raise flowers and shrubs, to have a kitchen garden, and to take an active part in producing something beautiful; whether it is a rose, a chrysanthemum, or a cathedral matters not.

We have many men and women in the lower mental brackets who are apparently contented if their physical needs are satisfied. But this is not true of men and women in the upper mental brackets, for with them animal comforts are only incidental; their real happiness is found in a partial realization of their day-dreams -their ideals.

Men and women in the middle brackets who have the necessities and comforts of life-and a raucous radio -appear to be happier than those in the upper mental

KNOW THYSELF 

brackets who can not realize in a satisfactory way some of their ideals. One's sympathy is for the men and women of finer clay. The mentally inferior fail in both. Millions of them are unable to make a comfortable living for themselves; idealism, the flower of personal and social development, buds in a few of them but seldom blooms.

A lack of intelligent idealism is one of the worst defects the mind can have; wrong idealism and an idealism that can not, in a satisfactory measure, be realized are causes of untold sorrow and disappointment. Persons who have no ideals or who have poor ideals are a burden on society; economic parasites and criminals belong to this class. And dreaming idealists who do not take into account the stern realities that confront them make up a long list of failures, ne'er-dowells, pests, mis-fits, and the good but queer who draw on our sympathy every day of the year. 

Superior children manifest intelligent idealism early in life; this may be seen in their interest and ability to do good work in school, their interest in the fine arts, and their attitude toward the best parts of their home life. One may also see finer ethical feeling and conduct manifested voluntarily by superior children than by those in the lower mental brackets. In this respect it is an interesting thing to note that while the superior child, perhaps due to his dramatic bent, may like to play the part of a robber, a pirate, or a scalphunting Indian, yet he will in his personal behavior be free from cruelty to animals and injustice to playmates, and he will carefully avoid unfair behavior with smaller children. Such children differentiate carefully between the dramatic and things that are real, just as they, early in life, begin to differentiate carefully between their day-dreams and reality.

THE STUDY 

59 20. Persons having superior mentality have a keen sense of responsibility.

COMMENT. This is one of the major evidences of a good mind. The person who intelligently avoids unnecessary responsibility has much to his advantage. He is not likely to be an economic parasite; he is likely to retain his honesty and integrity in money matters; he is likely to pay as he goes and if he can not pay he will not go; he does not make promises that he can not fulfill; he does not carelessly jeopardize his future, his honor, his integrity, and his peace of mind; he knows the difference between wishes and facts; he discriminates carefully between what he needs and what he desires; he knows, and therefore keeps within his limitations.

Proper training, rigid discipline, and oft-repeated admonition might help the inferior and the mediocre to avoid unnecessary responsibility. It might. Unfortunately this training is not done and the rabble runs wild with personal, family, and social results that are deplorable.

There are comparatively few persons who are able to assume much responsibility other than that which is necessary. Intelligent, honorable self-preservation should be the first duty of all persons who are old enough to understand what personal responsibility means; and this is all the responsibility that over fifty per cent of our people can handle.

Assuming unnecessary responsibility for the welfare of other persons is often inspired by an unholy hodgepodge of ignorance, sentiment, and hypocrisy. One sometimes sees parents who are oversolicitous about their children; this encourages the children to be helpless, whining dependents who may never get out of the "gimme" class. If or when the parents are no longer

KNOW THYSELF 

able to help them they are likely to become moochers on society. Sometimes religious persons with a bent for helping the lowly and the underprivileged do much more harm at least in many cases-than they do good. 

Sometimes this holy zeal to assume responsibility for the ignorant and the congenitally inferior is only a pretext, a subterfuge, to make a salaried job for a professional up-lifter. Responsibility that rests on reasonborn duty such as that of parent for child, of adult child for aged parent, of all adults toward society, and of society toward all individuals-but more especially toward small children, the aged, and the physically and mentally incompetent-is self-evident and needs no elaboration.

It appears to take native intelligence, insight, and personal experience to enable one to know when he should assume responsibility for the welfare of others and when he should say no in a way that can not be misunderstood. This fact may be observed every day: when we do things for people that they can and should do for themselves, we do them an injury; and when we make individual persons and individual families recognize their own responsibility and then make them solve their own problems, we have made a commendable contribution to society.

As an evidence of mental superiority judge an adult by the way he avoids unnecessary responsibility as well as by the way he fulfills that which is necessary. The inferior fail in both phases of this matter. Trashy, irresponsible men may desert their families or perhaps waste their wages, on drink or gambling, that their families need for the necessities of life. Inferior women may make unnecessary bills here and there, wherever they can get credit, with little thought as to how the wage-earner in the family can pay for them. The inferior marry, live in squalor, and produce swarms of

THE STUDY 

61 children for the tax-payers and charities to support. 

Physicians know all phases of trashiness among trashy people. 

21. Persons having superior mentality usually begin both to choose and to improve their environment as soon as they are fifteen or sixteen years of age.

COMMENT. Of talking and writing about environ ment there is no end. To the person who is in receptive mood the following statement may be useful. What a man does is due largely to environment; but how well he does it, is due largely to his innate ability. Necessity or expediency may, for example, require a man to be a motorcar mechanic; his heredity will determine largely whether he will be a good one or not. 

There is a physical environment, that is, the place or locality where one lives; there is a mental environment in which one comes in contact daily with good minds or poor minds; and there is a social environment that may be either good or bad. It may be stated as a generality that superior persons usually have wholesome ideas and standards relative to all three phases of their environment for they know that a good mind develops best in suitable surroundings. It may also be observed that inferior and mediocre persons, given the means with which to do so, are prone to change their physical environment greatly for the better, but are not prone to change their mental and social environment so noticeably. The reasons for this are apparent. 

Inferior persons do not by their own efforts improve their environment greatly. Many of them have difficulty in adjusting themselves to better surroundings that are provided for them by somebody else or by accidental means. The inferior are not the helpless victims of environment that well-meaning but ignorant

KNOW THYSELF 

up-lifters and sob-sisters think they are. As a rule persons who have both the will and the ability to change their environment are not long in finding ways and means for doing so. 

Except in children under sixteen years of age, and prisoners, victims of poor mental and social environment are usually willing victims. It is usually a case of "birds of a feather."

The mental and social environments are the most difficult to change. Money will enable a person to buy or to lease a good place in which to live in a good neighborhood, but money alone can not enable a fool or an uncouth lout to have intellectual, refined associates. 

The superior person does not rate or measure environmental qualities in terms of money. Thoreau lived happily in a cabin at Walden Pond. The present 

Bishop of London has a palace as a residence; he says he would prefer to live in three rooms. As a rule persons having superior minds desire peace and comfort rather than luxuries and ostentation. 

Superior persons usually change their environment slowly; they retain the best of their past, old friends, old lace, old books, old furniture; they choose the new wisely, not to replace, but to accumulate, and as the years pass by they take much pleasure-and rightfully so-in what they have accumulated. They have to a large extent made their own environment. 

It takes a great deal of optimism or ignorance or blind wish-thinking to expect much improvement in millions of our people in the lower mental brackets; for example, the "po' white trash" of the South, coal miners, textile employees, and negroes. Many of these people lack ideals, a feasible plan to improve, and even the will to improve. Education, religion, professional uplifting, and labor agreements have been of little help in changing the what-is into something better for them.

THE STUDY 

63 Their outlook is indeed dreary to anyone who faces facts.

It is inane and stupid to continue to talk so much in political and economic discussions about the rights of people in the lower mental brackets. These people have all the rights they need. What they lack is intelligence. 

What one has temporarily is not a fair test of his ability to solve his problem of environment. Many a good man has been the victim of bad judgment, bad advice, or unavoidable misfortune in money matters, and has fallen to the bottom. But such men usually collect their wits quickly and begin again—to collect money from other persons who are not intelligent enough to keep it. On the other hand we have millions of adults in the lower mental brackets whose environment is poor, year after year, decade after decade, and even generation after generation. 

22. The mental processes of persons who have superior mentality have a marked tendency to be based on definite premises, to be related, to be consecutive, and to reach definite conclusions.

COMMENT. It was said relative to Coleridge's conversation during his last and senile years that he began with no premises and reached no conclusions. This criticism or description may be applied very often to conversation among inferior and mediocre personsand to conversational bores as well in the upper mental brackets-who have not arrived at the age of senility. 

Superior minds have a tendency to put first things first; that is, they think of causes first and for that reason many of their statements show both cause and effect. Inferior minds are prone to think of effectsof phenomena-first, the cause often appearing to be of little or no importance to them.

KNOW THYSELF 

Inferior and mediocre minds are prone to be erratic. 

Their thoughts are often not related, they are often not consecutive, and their conclusions are either faulty or they are not reached at all. This is one of the reasons why inferior persons are so inaccurate and so unreliable in their statements. Not being able to think and to express themselves accurately they take the next best way-they draw on their imaginations. 

Accuracy in thinking is very largely an innate mental quality, a gift, rather than the result of training or of environment. Long continued, rigid discipline may improve the accuracy of a mediocre person's thinking. 

Very few persons, however, are in position to be disciplined. One may sometimes see two brothers, one of whom will speak accurately or not at all, and the other will be a congenital liar whose statements are seldom entirely true. Persons who speak accurately usually speak comparatively little, and persons who are guilty of much speaking, especially on a great variety of subjects, seldom say much that is worth hearing. 

The manner rather than the matter of speaking is, as far as one's speech indicates it, the best index of one's mental quality. A person who has given much thought and observation to mental qualities has one more good lead in judging a person's mind by observing the accuracy and completeness of his statements on a variety of subjects. Persons who think accurately not only speak accurately but they also complete the thought. A good conversationalist resents the intrusive jackass who interrupts in the middle of a sentence. 

It is embarrassing to a person with a good mind to have some one-even a fool-ask him what he means by a statement made in a conversation unless the subjectmatter or a word used in the statement is unknown to the hearer. One may overhear the conversation of house servants and other persons, either black or white, of inferior mentality and every few minutes one of

THE STUDY 

65 the hearers will ask "Whadda you-all mean?" In such cases the poor, disconnected thought of the speaker, as well as a lack of understanding on the part of the listener, reveal the inferiority and stupidity of both. 

Much of the conversation between two superior persons at any time is based on experimental knowledge; that is, knowledge gained by experience. When knowledge other than that gained by personal experience is used in their conversation it is very often derived from their reading. Their statements, then, are based on rather accurate premises. And when their thoughts are speculative, they frankly state they are speculative. On the other hand the inferior are habitual quoters of hearsay; and anyone who has had much experience with his fellow man knows that statements based on "I heard" or "They say" might, for the most part, be better left unstated. 

23. An adult who has a good mind is constantly thoughtful of the mental limitations of the person or persons to whom he is speaking.

COMMENT. Talleyrand lived in the midst of political, social, and religious intrigue and corruption. It is not strange then that he uttered the brilliant, clever mot that "Language is made to conceal rather than to reveal thought. One would be stupid, indeed, if he did not see every day that language is often used to conceal facts; that point needs no further illumination. 99 But large numbers of people are unable to make themselves understood clearly, not only by the inferior and mediocre, but also by superior persons who are not informed on the particular subject under discussion. It is characteristic of the inferior person, as well as the conversational bore, that he can talk all around a subject without saying what should be said, when a person with

KNOW THYSELF 

a good mind could say everything necessary in from ten to fifty words. 

One hears ignorant, inferior parents talking to their small children with grunts and disconnected phrases or sentences with the result that the children understand almost nothing. One hears the half-educated using technical expressions to other adults and even to children who have no understanding of their meaning. 

One hears adults emitting a very torrent of words, on some simple matter, that causes only confusion instead of enlightenment. And we doctors hear laymen trying to use the technical language of medicine with the result that many of their statements would make excellent contributions to the jokes column in our medical publications.

It has been said many times as a complaint that scientists do not know how to transfer their thoughts and knowledge to laymen. This statement is not generally true. The truth is the layman thinks he should learn in a few minutes what it should normally take him weeks or months to learn. It takes time to learn scientific knowledge just as it takes time to learn a foreign language or to learn to play the piano.

Good language and good sense in its use are the product of a good mind. The ultimate purpose of language is to make oneself understood, hence, to be understood it is necessary to hold in mind at all times the type of mind or minds that are expected to do the understanding. The transference of thought is one of life's most frequent problems; only good minds solve problems well. To talk merely for the sake of exercising one's tongue and airing one's vocabulary is not characteristic of a good mind.

The ability to speak plainly and simply, to say enough but not too much, is apparently a language gift. There is much truth in the complimentary statement sometimes made about a person to the effect that he has the

THE STUDY 

67 gift of speech. But it must not be overlooked that that person also has keen insight into the ability of his hearers to understand.

The good mind is more concerned with its own responsibility in speaking or writing the truth than it is in making an impression on the masses; for making an impression on the masses is often the aim of the mountebank and the charlatan as well as of the prophet. Most men who influence the masses merely stir up their emotions their hates, prejudices, fears, self-esteem, and expectations, rather than their intellects. 

All great thinkers strive to tell the truth as they see it; all want to be understood by certain intellectual grades of people; yet all know that they can be understood only by comparatively few persons. At best, worth-while knowledge is only sifted down from the top through the various mental strata. Little or none of it reaches the lowest stratum. 

24. Persons having superior mentality have a detached attitude and discriminating judgment toward the herd.

COMMENT. This is primarily a phase of self-preservation. The superior person desires first to maintain his integrity, his peace of mind, his daily routine; he does not want to waste time nor does he want to be disturbed. 

The herd is interested in herd activities; the superior mind is interested first, last, and always in its own activities, and it is only when its own activities may be advanced and the welfare of the herd improved that it acts with the herd. The superior person wants to help the herd but he wants to help it in his own way; he does not want the herd to determine how he shall help it. As an illustration: Bentham, Goethe, and Carlyle wanted to help the herd by means of their thinking and writing,

KNOW THYSELF 

68 but they did not let the herd interfere with them while they were at work.

Men who aggressively curry favor with the herd have an ax to grind; there are very few exceptions to the rule. Unselfishness is a beautiful theory that still lingers in the minds of the ignorant and fools. Intelligent selfishness is both normal and approvable; it should not be confused with graft, greed, and avarice. 

Superior persons are not on the defensive giving excuses for their exclusiveness and their aloof manner, for that is their natural bent. As a result of this attitude inferior persons, social climbers, and curiosity seekers who have been repelled by superior persons occasionally vent some small talk because they are not allowed to rush in where fools are not permitted to tread. One could hardly imagine Steinmetz, Edison, Banting, or Burbank belonging to a small-town or suburban Hot-Stove League, although doubtless all members of the league would be glad to welcome them with open arms.

Superior persons have rights just as inferior persons do. One of the rights that many superior persons insist on maintaining is the right to be left alone; hence, if they refuse to attend banquets and bridge parties, to listen to radios, bores, and the local Anvil 

Chorus, they are not exceeding their rights. 

There are times when a lucky spin of the wheel of

Fortune makes an inferior or a mediocre person somewhat dizzy, and for a while he thinks he is flying instead of walking; he affects a haughty, aloof manner; he may become a snob socially; he arouses a feeling of envy in his inferiors and equals, and of amusement in his superiors. But the lucky spin of the wheel did not make him a superior person although he would like to appear to be; it only left him a fool.

There is a difference between a person's being in the herd and of the herd; of currying favor with the herd,

THE STUDY 

69 and of doing something in one's own way for the herd. 

The herd respects a man who respects himself. Few social rules are more profitable for young men and women to know and to heed than the old, old rule that familiarity begets contempt. Many young persons lose their employment and others are considered beyond the pale, socially, because they are coarsely familiar with persons in authority or with their elders. Familiarity on short acquaintance is sometimes permissible with one's equals, but never with one's inferiors. To be familiar but not vulgar is the special province of the mentally superior and of the social artist. A man may be a king, a tyrant, or a boor in his own world of daydreams, but in the world of reality he would do well to be a polished diplomat. 

25. Persons having superior mentality usually recognize and control their three major instincts more than inferior and mediocre persons do.

COMMENT. It is assumed that most readers of this book still use the word instinct relative to human behavior even though some of the psychologists discarded the word years ago; and calling the primary urges "wishes" as other psychologists do is only changing the name. Paley's ideas of instinct will probably survive long after the more extreme views of Behaviorism have been thrown out on the trash-pile of outworn theories. 

Primitive people, inferior people, and children are controlled largely by their instincts and emotions; their acts are not controlled by reason as are the acts of superior adults.

One of the principal problems of society is to get all persons and more especially those in the lower mental brackets-to recognize and to discipline their instincts. 

Primitive people apparently followed their impulses,

KNOW THYSELF 

wishes, and instincts with but little restraint. If a man wanted a thing he got it, provided the person who possessed it was not too strong or too shrewd to relinquish it; and what was true of the individual person was also true of the tribe.

On the whole, self-discipline of the instincts is largely a product of civilization; it is an acquired practice founded on necessity. Superior persons practice selfrestraint for the sake of society as well as for their own personal welfare; as in the case of honesty, they find it to be the best policy.

It is characteristic of persons who are instinct-controlled to act first and to think afterwards-if they think at all. Many of them appear to give but little thought to very serious personal matters-matters that involve society as well as themselves. Nor is there any way to make them think-in time-except by rigid discipline; and very few persons except those in military service and prisoners are under rigid discipline. Life, itself, affords a certain amount of discipline to good minds, but poor minds learn slowly, if at all, by the ordinary experiences of life; they are just as thoughtless, trashy, and irresponsible at forty as they were at twenty. 

Most of our personal fears, troubles, and anxieties have to do with the three major instincts; also most of our personal, family, and social problems as well as a few of our national and international problems are due to lack of control of those instincts. As an illustration of the last-mentioned: nations are prone to go to war when they are overpopulated; for example, Germany in 

1914, and, as a prophesy, Japan within the next twenty years. The deplorable state of China and India due to their overpopulation is well known to all persons informed on this matter. It has been estimated recently that from two to nine millions of people starve to death in China every year.

Any illuminating study of mental qualities must in-

THE STUDY 

71 clude a study of the three major instincts; and any profitable study of an individual person's mental makeup must include definite knowledge as to whether these instincts are weak or are strong in him, and, if strong, whether they control him in a harmful way or whether he controls them. Instincts vary in their intensity from feeling that is scarcely recognized on up to that which may be called an overruling passion. In a civilized medium either extreme is likely to be detrimental to a person and to society as one may readily see by observing the behavior of individual persons or groups from day to day. 

26. Adults having superior mentality are prone to give much intelligent thought and action to the matter of honest, satisfactory self-preservation. 

COMMENT. Except for immediate danger the instinct for self-preservation is not strong in persons of inferior mentality. Their defect in this regard is not entirely a matter of wrong thinking for millions of them apparently do not think at all. The ethics of selfpreservation are seemingly of little concern to them.

Inferior persons are normally dependent; they are accustomed to having superior persons think for them from their earliest recollection, hence, they have no strong inclination to think and to act for themselves independently; they feel sure that somebody else can and will provide for them.

Inferior persons, like the beasts of the field and the fowls of the air, take no thought of the morrow except when forced to do so by physical suffering, hunger, and other disagreeable privations; they wish and dream rather than plan and act. They appear to be almost devoid of intelligent forethought; they have but little intelligence about avoiding unnecessary responsibility,

KNOW THYSELF 

and still less about balancing personal and family budgets.

In the earlier ages of the race the inferior were left to sink or to swim; they sank. But in a civilized medium, religious, altruistic, medical, and philanthropic efforts are in part directed toward saving and uplifting the inferior. They are saved though not noticeably uplifted; and they reproduce others of their kind with a prolificness that is detrimental to society. Among things having life-plants and animals-like produces and reproduces like with only occasional exceptions. 

Mentally inferior parents sometimes may beget a child superior to themselves; also, let it not be overlooked, they just as often beget a child that is inferior to themselves.

Self-preservation on the whole is about seventy-five per cent a personal and family matter; the other twenty-five per cent may be due to general economic conditions, mental or physical incapacity, and accidental causes. A strong, uncontrolled instinct for selfpreservation produces misers, tight-wads, pinch-pennies, and greed in business matters. A strong instinct for self-preservation is usually a virtue; very seldom is it a vice.

Psychologists classify the lower mental grades, beginning at the bottom, as idiots, imbeciles, and morons. 

Idiots and imbeciles are seldom self-supporting; also, fortunately, they seldom reproduce. Morons are more or less self-supporting in so-called good times; unfortunately, most of them reproduce very prolifically. 

Society can, without serious injury to the general good, support only a small amount of parasitism; for when parasitism increases too much it will drag society and general economic conditions downward to a level that will cause many persons who are usually self-supporting to be dependents. Such was the condition after the crash of 'twenty-nine.

THE STUDY 

73 The economic improvement of the lower mental classes by means of charity and governmental aid has very evident limitations if looked at from the viewpoint of fact rather than that of wish-thinking, fancy, and political expediency. It is both humane and right to give temporary relief to all who are in actual want, but an intelligent view of the problem of economic parasitism leads the person who faces facts fearlessly to the conclusion that the problem can be solved permanently and in a socially satisfactory way only by making individual persons and individual families solve their own problems; and this requires definite, intensive teaching and discipline. Millions of our people need disciplinediscipline born of necessity; this is the most potent as well as the most permanent factor in decreasing dependency. Political mountebanks in quest of votes have not the courage to tell their constituents the truth in this matter. Whether a man is honorably self-supporting or not depends almost entirely on what he has above his ears rather than on what is done or left undone in Washington.

In judging mental qualities it is well to note if a person between the ages of sixteen and sixty is or is not self-supporting. And if he is self-supporting it is well to observe his standard of living and the security of his means for maintaining it. Superior minds take the problem of self-preservation seriously; there are very few exceptions to the rule. 

27. Persons having superior mentality are prone to control their sex instinct and activities either for the sake of self-preservation or for the welfare of their offspring if they have any.

COMMENT. It is commonly known that men and women in the uppermost mental brackets are consider-

KNOW THYSELF 

ably below the average in producing offspring. Fools say the intellectuals should have more babies, but fools are noted for saying foolish things. Also the argument, stated or implied by fools, who are on the defensive, that since the upper classes have but few children the lower classes should have many, is utterly foolish. 

The scarcity of children in the upper mental classes is not and has never been a serious social problem; it is very often a serious personal and family problem, however. Physicians know this better than anybody else except childless women. A childless woman who is normal in the physiology of reproduction has a feeling in the matter that only another normal but childless woman understands.

Maternity is a normal woman's natural goal. Parenthood means much more to women than it does to men. 

But there are qualifications in this matter. Defective children are more undesirable than no children. Children of low-grade mentality are both a family and a social problem. To have too many children, that is, more children than the family's income can properly support and provide for, is a social and economic crime -unfair to the children and unfair to the upper classes who are required by taxation as well as by voluntary giving to support them.

The family is the most necessary and the most sacred group in the world. When family life deteriorates society deteriorates. Adults who have low standards relative to family life either should not marry or they should have no children. Children have a right to be born into and reared in a well-ordered home; they have a right to good heredity; they have a right to parents who put the welfare of the family first; they have a right to a good start in life according to the best standards of their time and place.

To the superior person whose thinking is untram-

THE STUDY 

75 meled by ignorance, prejudice, and fear of conventional ideas, sex is, at this time, more of an economic than a religious problem; and its moral phase has to do with the rights and welfare of society rather than with personal disobedience of traditional rules, the breaking of which is of little concern to any one except to those who break them.

The worst, because the most frequent, of sex wrongs and crimes is the production and reproduction of the congenitally unfit-the mentally inferior, the insane, syphilitics, and other physical and mental defectives. 

Yet these wrongs and crimes are committed daily, not only sanctioned by society but also with the blessing of 

Holy Church.

There is on the whole not so much intelligent thought given to the production of quality in children as a Texas ranch owner gives to the production of good cattle or an Iowa farmer gives to the production of good hogs.

Nor would a farmer think of producing calves and pigs, knowing that he had nothing with which to feed them, and depend upon other farmers in his community to take up a collection with which to buy feed for his livestock.

Some day, perhaps, religious folk and moralists will join eugenicists, physicians, and economists in openly condemning reproduction by the mentally inferior, syphilitics, criminals, and habitual parasites. If all persons in the upper mental brackets would reprove and shame beggars, parasites, and criminals for reproducing, there might be developed and put into practice a new rule in social ethics. If a child can not be well born and well cared for after its birth, it should not be conceived. Sex ethics need revision. The right of a child to be well born should be emphasized and the reproduction of trashy stock should be prevented as far as possible.

KNOW THYSELF 

28. The social instinct, that is, the desire to pass one's time with other persons, is weaker as well as more discriminating among superior persons than among inferior persons.

COMMENT. This instinct, like the other two major instincts, varies greatly in intensity. There are very few hermits; so few, indeed, that many persons with wide acquaintance have never even seen one. 

Even among city dwellers there are very few persons, except the famous and the near-famous, who have much difficulty in excluding undesirable and unwanted company and visitors.

Persons who habitually seek companionship merely for the sake of companionship do not have as good mental qualities as do persons whose company is sought. 

Mahomet must go to the mountain. This is a useful observation to make in judging children. The child that habitually leaves its home to be with other children is not likely to rise above mediocrity in adult life; he will be one among millions of his kind who are always going somewhere but never get anywhere. The same observation holds true relative to persons in their late 'teens and early twenties.

Adults who have superior mentality are prone to discriminate carefully in selecting their voluntary associates and companions. Young men and women may be judged mentally, not only by the company they keep, but also by where they keep it; the latter is almost as important as the former. Among adults similar mental qualities attract more strongly than differences in moral practices repel. Superior minds are more intolerant of mental inferiority in involuntary associates than they are of moral inferiority. Superior minds of all ages are prone to seek association with better minds; if

THE STUDY 

77 superior persons are not available they resort to the printed page.

Young men and women who have good mentality do not speak of "settling down" for they have never been any other way than settled down; they have nearly always controlled their social instinct.

In inferior and mediocre persons the social instinct overrules good sense in regard to personal welfare with disappointing frequency. There are probably more bitter family rows due to the efforts of parents to control the social instincts of their children in the late 'teens and early twenties than from any other cause. It usually takes rigid discipline to put an old head on young shoulders. And a young head on young shoulders, especially when the young head is inherently trashy, is sometimes a family and social problem that is not easily solved. 

Persons with superior mentality control their social instinct rather easily; only rarely do such persons need to be admonished by anybody else, and then a hint is usually sufficient. Inferior and mediocre persons have comparatively little self-originated control over this instinct, hence, their frequent conflicts with parents, teachers, employers, and other persons who are trying to take a helpful interest in them. Not infrequently young people of the trashy type give more thought to what they intend doing between seven and ten o'clock at night than they do to the day's work.

There are comparatively few persons who control their instincts completely; even the best may sometimes fail. In the upper social stratum money sometimes marries brains. Money, though seeking the added prestige of reflected intelligence, is likely to be strongly inclined toward the social life; and brains, seeking security and independence, is not socially inclined. A divorce, or in some cases one more divorce, is likely to be one of the results of such ill-considered matrimonial ventures.

KNOW THYSELF 

The physician sees daily the evil effects of uncontrolled social instinct with more detail than the layman does. He not only sees the economic evil that arises from it but he also has first-hand knowledge of the injury to the body as well. He sees there are millions of people who have not enough sense to go to bed at bed time and that many of them pay a penalty because of it; he sees venereal disease and knows its results in all their many forms and ramifications; he sees many persons who are either temporarily or chronically ill who should be at home in bed but whose social instinct causes them to be away from home-often to their detriment-for the most inane and trivial causes.

In the early ages of the race, and even in civilization up to a century ago, the uncontrolled social instinct was not the serious problem that it is today. Uncivilized peoples had no easy means of transportation, except to walk, with the result that they had to remain at home save when they went away in quest of food or other necessity. There were no means of communication except by oral statement; and the population had not collected in large groups. But all of this has been changed in a civilized medium with the result that the uncontrolled social instinct in the inferior and mediocre mental grades has become a problem fully as grave as the uncontrolled sex instinct. 

29. Persons having superior mentality take a superficial though not often an intelligent and persistent interest in self-knowledge.

COMMENT. Although superior men and women have been trying for twenty-three hundred years to follow Socrates' advice to "know thyself" they have, with very few exceptions, made little progress. And yet by a few students and their followers more progress has

THE STUDY 

79 been made during the last century than was made altogether during the twenty-two centuries before. 

The anatomists have dissected the body with so much care and skill that there is little more to be learned about its gross structure although there is still much to be learned about its minute and microscopic details. 

The physiologists have made great progress in determining the function or functions of the various organs and tissues of the body but they still have much to learn.

The psychologists have speculated extensively, if not always intelligently and profitably, about the mind. 

They have had to battle against beliefs, traditions, and superstitions that were firmly fixed in even the best of minds; they have developed and later discarded volumes of theories; and from all the straw they have threshed over and over, they have got but little useful grain for the intelligent layman who has both the ability and will to learn.

A few of the psychologists got rid of the soul long ago; that appeared to simplify matters somewhat for them. Then they threw the mental faculties out in the ash-can along with the soul; more simplification. In more recent years other psychologists discarded the instincts and a few, even, have discarded the mind, itself, and have nothing left but conditioned reflexes manifesting themselves largely by animated activity of the larynx. This, one may hope, is the subject reduced to its simplest terms.

During the last fifty years students of heredity have learned and have taught more useful information than was ever known before on the subject. At this time it looks very much as if heredity will hold the center of the stage in psychology and be in the spotlight for a score or more of years. More and more it is seen that good minds are born and made-and often largely selfmade but that they must first be well born; an in-

KNOW THYSELF 

herently poor mind can not be developed into a good mind. Also it is observed that to be born with a good mind is the greatest asset-the best piece of good luckthat a human being can hope for.

Fortunately most persons can not know a great deal about their family tree. Knowledge of this kind might be discouraging-even depressing-for many family trees, despite careful pruning, have not always borne good fruit. But every intelligent person who can read simple English can learn, slowly but surely, to know himself; this takes both intelligence and time, but it can be done if one has both the ability and the will to learn. 

To know oneself one must be able to see and to think of oneself in a detached way. It is probable that man is the only animal that can do this, that can think of his mind as being good or bad just as he thinks of his heart or of his feet as being good or bad. It is also to a person's credit to know that he has a good mind just as it is to his credit to know that he has a good stomach or good eyes.

There is no reason why a person should be so modest that he need blush with embarrassment to recognize that he has some good mental qualities even though good social sense will not permit him to broadcast the fact daily. Nor is there any reason why a person who has a goodly number of good mental qualities should be so dull, stupid, and self-complacent that he could not recognize that he has some poor mental qualities. 

30. The mind of the poet is specially gifted in seeing, appreciating, and describing Beauty, Goodness, and Truth.

COMMENT. Poetry is an indefinite term. To the child or to the adult with a childish mind a poem is a series of words written in rime, often only a meaning-

THE STUDY 

81 less jingle, that appeals to the sense of rhythm and sound. Millions of small children can recite "Eeny, meeny, miney, moe" by the time they are four years old, and many of them know only very little more about poetry at twenty-four or even at sixty-four. But to the better-grade mind a poem is not, strictly speaking, the words the poet has written but what the words make the reader see, feel, and think; the words of a poem are the equivalent of the notes of a piece of music. 

A good poem inspires; it creates anew.

The poet's mind is like other minds gifted with talent for one of the fine arts in that it is especially alert to see beauty, to appreciate harmony, and to feel goodness. 

Poets always have much sentiment and not infrequently they are controlled by a ruling passion. Sometimes the ruling passion, as in the case of Burns, Byron, Savage,

Wilde, and Verlaine, causes a great deal of trouble. 

The inherited defects in the poet's mind may give rise to social behavior that is strongly disapproved by the "unco gude"-behavior that is injurious to the poet, himself and that causes a feeling of pity and regret in the mind of students of heredity and abnormal psychology.

It is commonly thought that poets have small minds, that they are men having only one kind of ideas and interests, and those of a sort that are of little worth; but such opinion is not true for it is based on the false conception that writers of jingles and rimes are poets.

A real poet is not only an artist, he may also be a prophet, a philosopher, a dramatist, or even a scientist. 

According to all psychologic ratings Goethe had one of the greatest minds that nature has thus far produced. 

It is also interesting to note that while Tennyson lived in a generation of great men, few of them have stood the test of time as well as he has. Poets have by intuition set down almost as many eternal verities as the philosophers have by prolonged study; and many a

KNOW THYSELF 

scientific dictum of today is likely to be in the discard of tomorrow.

The poetical mind, closely akin to the musical mind, very naturally uses meter, rhythm, and rime in its expressions. A feeling of artistry demands that beautiful thoughts and feelings be expressed in beautiful manner. 

The poet's ability to express what he sees and feels is a talent, a thing innate. And like musical talent, it may be improved by practice but it can not be acquired. That poets are born to be poets is an accurate observation that has been made from the time of Homer. There are comparatively few real poets just as there are comparatively few composers of good music; but there are many persons who appreciate poetry although not so many as appreciate music.

The appreciation of a poem depends upon the store of knowledge in the mind of the reader at the time the poem is read. Burns wrote largely of the simple, familiar things-the sights, and thoughts, and sentiment of his native land, and for that reason was thoroughly understood by his people. Not so in the case of Browning as anyone who tries to read him soon finds out.

With poetry, as with pictures, we like the familiar. 

Millions of Americans, who have taken some of their pleasure and many of their weekly baths in the ol' swimmin' hole, like Riley; and a million or so of us who have had a certain experience, still vivid in the mind, can read or recite with considerable feeling McCrae's 

In Flanders Fields. 

31. Persons having superior mentality are usually well supplied with ability to adjust and to adapt themselves to changes in their environment.

COMMENT. This social rule is founded on and is a manifestation of one of the common laws of biology-

THE STUDY 

83 the law of adaptation. Superior persons not only know how to rise intelligently and without ostentation in the scale of living, but they know how to come down gracefully and uncomplainingly when it is necessary. Many thousands of Americans have exhibited one phase of this sterling quality since the financial crash of 'twentynine.

The inferior often can not adapt themselves to better living conditions; they do not know how to take care of a better home if one is provided for them; they have no judgement about money values; and worse than that, they have very little appreciation of social and intellectual values in other persons.

Mediocre persons have more adaptability in rising in the scale of living than they have in coming down after their money has been spent or their investments have become more or less unprofitable. They complain and regret and they exaggerate the glory that was theirs in palmier days; they become social nuisances.

But the superior person knows how to be all things with all men. He does not rise in the social scale like a sky-rocket putting on a show, but he grows like a tree; and like a tree he stands unless destroyed by an unusual storm-an act of God, the lawyers call it or by old age itself.

In war days we saw many men coming from wellordered homes, even from homes of luxury, who became adept at washing their own socks, and they were not altogether bad at washing flannel shirts and underclothing. We saw them eat any kind of food-good, bad, or indifferent that was provided for them; and if none could be obtained, they merely tightened up the belt another inch or two and carried on uncomplainingly. 

And not being professional patriots they have not, since their return to civilian life, used their military experience either for gain or for glory. Having helped to

KNOW THYSELF 

make the world safe for democracy they are willing to let the dictators run it as long as they do it well. More adaptability.

Social, economic, and occupational adaptability is essential for the welfare of an individual who does not have a sufficient and fool-proof income. Mediocre and inferior persons inherently have several or even many bad mental qualities of which the lack of adaptability is one of the worst.

Adaptability is manifested in superior children rather early in life; they are not selfishly exacting in their personal demands; they give and take after the manner of intelligent adults; self-denial resulting in peace and harmony appeals to them more strongly than strife and possession; they learn early to control their desires and learn to be satisfied with necessaries. 

After the necessities and a few comforts of life have been provided for, the intelligent adult sees that happiness depends very largely upon what is within him rather than on what is about him. In all fairness, however, let it be said that adults having superior mentality seldom find it necessary to adapt themselves to extremely bad environment. They usually know how to solve their problems well enough to avoid such situations.

In general it may be observed that superior persons are prone to improve their environment much more than the mediocre and inferior do, and that the quality of their environment does not often vary greatly, either for better or for worse, in a short period of time. In good surroundings the superior appear as if to the manner born; in poor surroundings their adaptability enables them to see and to use the best and to ignore the worst.

THE STUDY 

85 32. Persons having superior mentality frequently are endowed with originality, sometimes to a marked degree.

COMMENT. There is a difference between originality and the ability to solve problems. Old problems usually have old solutions and the person who solves them needs only to follow the beaten path; it is the ability to see and the will to follow that is important. For example, the matter of being honorably self-supporting on the part of a healthy man with fair intelligence requires primarily industry, economy, and the limiting of his pecuniary responsibilities. This plan has been used for centuries; it works. But it fails when any one of the three requirements-and especially the last-is not fulfilled. Most of the good minds that one meets from day to day are only following the beaten path. This is as true of men in the learned professions as it is of the mechanic or of the business man.

But to write a good poem, to compose the music of an opera, to do landscape gardening, or to plan a dwelling house that is both convenient and architecturally beautiful requires originality. There is, however, much truth in the old saying that there is nothing new under the sun. 

For example, in mechanical inventions: after primitive man figured out the wheel, the lever, the screw, and the pulley, inventing machines of various kinds has been only a matter of improvement and adaptation; in the language of the salesman, present-day machines are only "latest models."

Originality is always the result of a special gift; it is the prime characteristic of genius. Nearly always this gift manifests itself early in life. Even before the age of puberty the child with marked originality is seen to want to create, to make, or to improve something. The object of its efforts may be a picture, a toy, a musical

KNOW THYSELF 

instrument, or some device intended to be useful. 

Persons who have originality visualize what they think should be, what may be, and sometimes what can be. They do not, however, always think in terms of feasibility, in which case they are likely to be only dreaming failures. Besides, many thousands of things that are useless have been invented and patented; this is because creative minds are likely to become greatly enamored of their own children. They sometimes lack well-balanced perspective.

That part of the mind that engages itself in original thought usually knows no rest until its work is completed or until its visualized plan is found to be not feasible. Down below the level of consciousness it works on its problems day and night. The best of its work is, from time to time, thrown up into the conscious mind to be considered and perhaps used in the completion of the project. 

The production of an original thing is often called an inspiration by others who do not possess originality, and is thought of as something that comes into the mind spontaneously, suddenly, and mysteriously. But that is not usually true; in fact, it is seldom true. Most original thoughts and things are either the result of a growing, creative mind, as in the case of a musical composition, or else they are the solution of a longconsidered, definite problem. As to the latter it is well to recall the old maxim that "necessity is the mother of invention."

When confronted by a new problem of necessity to be solved, the man without originality almost invariably asks-how do other persons solve this problem?

Their answer is his answer. But the man with originality merely asks-how can this problem be solved? 

And his own originality is brought into use first in its solution. In addition to this the original mind thinks out new problems to be solved-not problems of neces-

THE STUDY 

87 sity, but problems whose solution will bring to it either pleasure or profit. 

33. The superior mind is usually both closely integrated and alert.

COMMENT. In judging a mind one should differentiate carefully between ignorance and stupidity. A child with superior mentality is ignorant because it is not old enough to have learned very much. But millions of adults are ignorant and will remain so because they have inferior minds; it is not a matter of age and environment with them for they do not even want to learn. 

Stupid persons very often can not use what knowledge they have; their minds lacks integration and alertness. 

They may know every detail necessary to do a certain thing but they can not get their details together as a whole. They repeat daily the excuse or explanation— "I did not think about that." No amount of training, discipline, or even punishment can improve very noticeably a stupid person. Ignorance in a good mind may be readily changed to enlightenment, but stupidity is both innate and almost unchangeable. 

Good integration and alertness add much to the usefulness of mediocre and superior minds.

A person having these mental qualities may not be able to solve big problems, after the manner of Macaulay, for they are beyond his ken; but those problems that are within his range of knowledge he can solve with an effectiveness that makes him a valuable person to society. And after all, life is made up largely of small problems. 

Persons who are commonly known to be reliable have good integrity and good mental integration. They have the ability to use their knowledge. They also know their limitations and are reluctant to assume responsibilities beyond their ability to fulfill. Any one who must have

KNOW THYSELF 

work done for him knows the happy feeling, the absence of suspense and worry, due to having a reliable, dependable person doing his work. One often hears the statement made that certain persons do not use what little sense God gave them. Such persons are not necessarily morally bad, but they are mentally bad; they lack, among other things, mental integration and alertness. 

Witty, humorous persons are plus on alertness; they think in time. Many persons think of the clever things they might have said, and even of the good things they might have done, any time from two hours to two weeks too late. Then very often they manifest a stupid egotism by talking of their bright after-thoughts; they would appear less asinine by keeping silent. It is bad enough to be a stupid ass; worse yet to advertise it unnecessarily.

There are good minds, however, that act slowly and yet finally arrive at good conclusions. The old fable of the tortoise and the hare is somewhat descriptive of them. Good minds that act slowly may have the advantage of avoiding the tangential and the erratic. Also it is well to note that good integration and alertness in the mediocre may give rise to a clever, engaging superficiality that is likely to lead to painful disillusionment on the part of persons who are deceived by them. 

When a person who has a closely integrated, alert mind is known to be a failure in life, his failure is usually due either to his having a lack of ethical integrity or to lack of a feasible life plan. This type of person may often be seen in some kind of off-color, shady business. Most charlatans and mountebanks in the business and professional worlds are of this mental make-up. The layman often speaks of them as persons who live by their wits. In addition to those mentioned of a public or semi-public kind, one finds in private life a long list of them from the common bill-beater to the more uncommon blackmailer and kidnapper. These

THE STUDY 

89 people use more judgment in solving problems than they do in selecting the problems to be solved. 

34. Persons having superior mentality usually are engaged in more or less serious conflict throughout much of their adult life.

COMMENT. An imbecile has very few self-originated conflicts and those few have to do largely with obtaining his creature comforts. The higher a person is in the mental scale, the more conflicts he has. Used in this sense the word conflict does not mean a brawl, dissension, or strife with another person, for superior persons are usually very free from those things; it means, among other things, the mental clashes, friction, and obstruction encountered in the solution of life's problems and disagreement and dissatisfaction with the useless and harmful traditional theories and practices of society. 

Doctor William A. White tells us, "Conflict is at the very root and source of life; it is the very stuff out of which life is made, and the necessary pre-condition of progress." Passive persons may be harmless individually but collectively they are a nuisance; they are controlled by ignorance, selfishness, and stupidity; they are, collectively, the principal bar to progress. 

A superior person's conflicts may be largely personal or they may be largely social. Carlyle, like many other persons of superior mentality, was, in early adult life, in conflict with the religious teaching of his childhood; he finally discarded much of its theology and many of its dogmas but carefully retained its ethics. In later life he was in conflict with the political and economic theories and practices of Great Britain. Jesus, Paul, Luther, Servetus, Knox, and Wesley were in conflict with the religious beliefs and practices of their time and place. Voltaire, Rousseau, Paine, Jefferson, Wash-

KNOW THYSELF 

ington, Garibaldi, and Bolivar were in conflict with political conditions in their respective countries. 

There have been and there still are prophets and reformers who are both intelligent and sincere. Many of them lose their zeal after they learn more about the inherent mental qualities, the limitations, of the persons they are trying to improve and to get improvements for. Late in life even some of the most zealous of them ask "What's the use?" and discontinue their efforts directed toward the improvement of people who do not want to be improved.

Millions of inferior people are at peace with the world and are not interested in social betterment so long as they have the comforts of life; their conflicts are mostly personal and are due to a desire to be more parasitical, or to disobedience to the civil law. This is true of most of our negroes in the South and it appears to be true of many millions of Orientals. 

Inferior races passively submit to Nordic uplifters and uplifting but, left to the devices and desires of their own hearts, they do very little to improve one another. 

Educating inferior persons and inferior races is, for the most part, but little more than a noble gesture. An inferior person who can read but will not is but little better off than an entirely illiterate person. 

Conflicts that are largely personal usually have to do with the three major instincts. The Freudians would have us believe that among neurotics the conflicts are largely of sex origin, while others who hear the confessions of adults year after year think the conflicts are fairly evenly divided among the three major instincts. 

During the past few years more persons are worrying about making a living and about maintaining a satisfactory social life for themselves than are worrying about sex. Whatever the conflict may be time will usually solve it, if the rationalizing faculty of the mind fails to do so. Its solution may not meet society's ap-

THE STUDY 

91 proval, but if it meets the approval of the person who has the conflict he is just as well satisfied. On the whole it should be so. 

35. Persons having superior mentality consider well their limitations; they think of their plans and actions both of the present and of the future in terms of their abilities rather than in terms of luck, wishes, and daydreams.

COMMENT. Nearly any person from the moron grade up thinks of his physical limitations frankly, fearlessly, and fairly intelligently. He knows his age and how long, at most, he is likely to be able to carry on his work. His physical condition may not be so good as he thinks it is and it may be better. If he is in doubt about the matter he is likely to consult a scholarly physician annually, or oftener, to get an estimate of his life expectancy. But as to his mental limitations there is often but little definite knowledge, no stock-taking, no knowledge and guidance sought, and none obtained; he trusts to luck based on ignorance, self-complacency, and hope.

Self-confidence is a good trait provided it is a part of a superior mind. In inferior and mediocre persons self-confidence may cause fools to rush in where wise men stay out; this fact may be observed daily. The superior person usually gives a hundred times as much. thought to what he can do, to what he is by mental endowment fitted to do, as the mediocre or inferior person does. Even then he sometimes fails, may fail again, but seldom fails more than twice; he is not an habitual failure.

And as to pecuniary limitations the story is about the same. Millions of people apparently see no inconsistency in assuming responsibilities and obligations far

KNOW THYSELF 

in excess of their income. That somebody else must stand the loss seems to be of little concern to habitual bill-beaters and to the wholly dependent and parasitical class. Ethical standards are low and moral integrity is weak in these people; being found out means nothing to them; bill collectors and monthly statements are no longer embarrassing.

The superior mind grows; it expands its range of activity slowly, carefully, knowingly; it is always aware of its limitations; it does not rush pell-mell into new fields of endeavor. In the superior mind the conscious attainment of ability usually precedes new undertakings.

There are millions of inferior misfits in the world; most of them came from inferior, misfit stock; they never should have been born; they should not, in justice to society as well as to themselves, propagate others of their kind; but, owing to the false propriety, ignorance, and indifference of our upper classes, few persons, except plain-speaking physicians, have sufficient moral and intellectual stamina to tell them so. But they should be told, told over and over, until the dullest of the dull can see that the rights of society should take precedence over an individual person's sex desire combined with sex ignorance.

The fault in this matter lies with the upper classes, for it is the upper classes who make the laws and who must take the lead in teaching the inferior. It looks as if having to provide for twelve million dependents would arouse the upper classes to enter into a campaign of teaching as vigorous and as intelligent as was and still is used to prevent tuberculosis and syphilis. Ultimately the taxpayers and contributors to the charities must turn their attention to the matter of preventing poverty at its source; Dr. Stork must be discouraged, even forbidden, to call on professional business at the homes of the congenitally inferior. Society will get nowhere in this matter by continuing its attitude of

THE STUDY 

93 wishing and waiting. People who do not see their limitations voluntarily, should be made to see them if such thing can be done. 

36. Persons having superior mentality nearly always manifest fine social sensibilities according to the usage of their time and place.

COMMENT. When one observes social behavior intelligently he sees that language oft proclaims the man; that the apparel oft proclaims the man; and that manners oft proclaim the man. These three things are always observed carefully by the observing from the moment personal contact begins on through the minutes, days, months, or years that contact or association is continued. These three things are more than a temporary, artificial social front used for the purpose of making a good impression, although they are frequently used for that purpose. Observed over a long time one or more of these three things will show clearly what the man is as well as what he temporarily pretends to be. 

Inferior persons may use good manners through a sense of fear or because of policy; their employment may depend upon their using good manners to their employers, their administrative superiors, and to the public that they must come in contact with while they are on duty. Off duty, and without the restraining influence of fear or policy, they immediately revert to their natural manner.

Mediocre persons are prone voluntarily to have two grades of manners: a good grade for the few persons who can or who may further their interests and an alleyrat grade for all others. Mediocre persons wilfully and intentionally use more bad manners than inferior persons do; this is a matter of daily observation in the 

South where we have an inferior race.

KNOW THYSELF 

Superior persons rarely deviate from their single standard-good at all times and with all persons; they are controlled, not only by intelligence, but also by a feeling of social artistry. And social artistry is manifested more often by one's manners than by any other means. In judging other persons with whom one has personal relations one may readily excuse their bad language and poor clothing but never their bad manners, whether the contact and association are voluntary or not. Nearly everyone likes to be treated with courtesy and nearly everyone resents personal discourtesy. The social artist knows this and acts accordingly at all times; but the boor and the social barbarian through stupidity and indifference frequently put themselves beyond the social pale.
