One may occasionally meet a superior person-usually of the introvert, highly intellectual type-who has manners like a mule and who is indifferent or even slovenly in his attire, but who is extremely accurate as well as artistic in his language; he may have gravy, egg-yolk, and molasses on his vest day after day, but he would feel socially disgraced were he to split an infinitive or misquote a line from Shakespeare; he is not emilyposted and does not even want to be; he expects to be recognized and properly rated socially and intellectually by his language and the quality of knowledge it conveys. 

Nearly all adults from the moron grade on up find it necessary to reprove or to admonish someone occasionally in a disciplinary way. There is an art, based on justice, in discipline, for discipline in most cases is for the purpose of teaching and correcting rather than for punishment. Browbeating the helpless is the way of the tyrant, not the way of the intelligent. Superior men and women whose duty in life includes the discipline of other persons, not infrequently must speak and act as disciplinarians; they usually fit the language and manner to the needs of the case in hand. Military officers,

THE STUDY 

95 traffic policemen, hospital superintendents, and judges on the bench, are usually not lacking in language that can penetrate the hardest pate, and be understood by the dullest of the dull. 

37. Persons having mental superiority are prone to analyze and to restrain their feeling of importance. 

COMMENT. Usually the less important one is in the world of reality the more important he is in his world of make-believe and fantasy. A feeling of importance is normal from the moron grade of mind on up; it is probably one of the strongest factors that make for personal progress and it is a stimulating factor in social life.

An adult of fair intelligence who has no feeling of importance toward anyone or anything would be a suitable subject for suicide or for the life of a hobo. Persons who have insufficient thyroid secretion often admit that they have a feeling of uselessness, that they are of no importance in the world. But given a suitable amount of thyroid medication for two to six weeks their outlook on life is likely to become normal again. 

A feeling of importance may be due to power, authority, usefulness, or to some special knowledge. A parent feels his importance in his family because of his usefulness; a scholar feels his importance because of his knowledge, and an autocratic ruler because of his power. 

An excessive feeling of importance may make for social annoyance, or, in case the feeling goes to such extremes that one has delusions of grandeur after the manner of paranoiacs, one may become a menace to society. Most persons who feel their oats too much are only harmless, conceited asses; they are prone to bray long and loud, to pontificate at inappropriate times and places, and to fail to discriminate carefully between

KNOW THYSELF 

fact and fancy, between what they are and what they want others to think they are. Except when such persons have authority and power to harm someone who fails to kotow to them they are only subjects for jesting and ridicule.

A tyrant is usually a man of inferior or mediocre mentality who has power that he uses with unfair advantage over others. He may have power because of his birth, as in the case of a king; because of political intrigue, as in case of many of the temporary rulers in Latin-American countries; or as an employer or his representative who has the right to hire and to fire employees. With the rise of democracy in governments and large organizations, tyranny on a big scale is gradually going out; its worst examples are to be found in the more or less remote past. Present-day rifles, pistols, and machine guns have a sobering effect on tyrants and would-be tyrants both great and small. Tyrants, charlatans, mountebanks, and the socially ambitious feed on notoriety. Merely to be publicized or to be applauded gives them a feeling of importance. 

Probably scientists, philosophers, and the intellectuals, generally, have the most intelligent view of their importance in the whole scheme of things. They are usually without authority; it is only what they have produced or thought that matters with them; they are not seeking either converts or followers; they have no ax to grind. If their work and ideas are accepted, they rejoice; if not accepted, they can only wait and hope; for they know that many of the world's great men are greater in death than they were in life. 

In estimating one's own importance in the world, aside from that felt toward one's family or other economic dependents, it is well to note carefully both the praise and the censure of one's equals and superiors; but both the praise and censure of one's inferiors should be greatly discounted. An intelligent view of one's

THE STUDY 

97 importance makes for social sanity, whether one is considering his fellow employees, his professional colleagues, his neighbors, or the public at large. This is a minor, though nevertheless, an important mental quality to possess. 

38. Adults having superior mentality think of duty more often than they think of rights. 

COMMENT. A downy feather will indicate to us which way the wind is blowing and the too frequent use of a single word may sometimes indicate to us the type of mind the user of the word has. Many inferior and mediocre persons are prone to think much and to talk entirely too much about their rights: their personal rights, their family rights, their social rights, their civil rights, their economic rights, and their political rights. 

These people are so exasperating with their complaints -largely a screen for their inferiority—that one might in petulant if not in pious mood say: from all persons who talk much about their rights-Good Lord, deliver us!

It may be observed that inferior children begin to talk about their rights rather early in life. In a family of several children ranging in age from six to sixteen or older, one may note that the child with the poorest mind speaks most often of its rights and that it is likely to complain about being abused and imposed upon. As an adult this type of child will continue to talk about its rights as a partial defense for being a failure.

Superior persons apparently think about their rights very little and talk about them even less. On the other hand they think of their duty very often but talk about it very little.

There are times, however, when superior persons must insist upon having their rights, but such times are

KNOW THYSELF 

very rare. There were times when the masses had to fight for their rights-but not in America after the government was established, for Americans never have been oppressed by priests, royalty, the nobility, and the army. Such oppression as Americans have suffered has been mostly economic and that due largely to the working class not being intelligent enough to avoid unnecessary responsibilities and obligations. For example: in textile towns and mining camps unemployed John

Stefanski who has one wife and seven children claims. that he is not treated right, that he is willing to work if he could get employment, and that society owes him and his family a living. John tells the visiting uplifters that he is oppressed, imposed upon, abused, and that his family is hungry and poorly clad. And some of this is painfully true. But John is too dull to see that society is not responsible for his having a wife and seven children; and that if he had no wife and children -and had never had-that he could make a comfortable living for himself, and that he would not have to endure economic oppresion. John passes the buck to society; and the gimme class damns Capitalism because of John's stupidity instead of damning John. And what is true of John Stefanski in mill town and mining town is true of a few million other dull-witted Johns and Elmers throughout America who are very alert at claiming rights, notably, the right to be supported by the upper classes. 

Much of the talk that one hears from day to day about rights comes from the mentally inferior, the lazy, the stupid, and the improvident. They are the people who profess to be greatly abused-the downtroddenthat political demagogs and mountebanks weep over biennially and quadrenially for the sake of getting votes. 

Duty, as it exists between an individual person and society, is not a one-sided thing. Society is not an inexhaustible Santa Claus who is able to give, give, give

THE STUDY 

99 without limit. Society has rights just as an individual person has rights. Taxes are as real as poverty; both are painful. Intelligent, self-supporting men and women have rights just as the inferior and the improvident have.

There are only two general ways with which to solve the problem of labor. It is necessary either to increase employment or to decrease the number of laborers; the latter way must sooner or later be used. 

39. Persons having superior mentality are usually versatile in their intellectual interests. 

COMMENT. Judging a person's mind by his interest or his lack of interest in a given subject is likely to be inaccurate, unfair, and unjust. Many a child of ten years has been called a dull-wit merely because he was not interested in learning one or more of his gradeschool studies. Later in life some of these children rise above the average and occasionally one becomes distinguished for some intellectual accomplishment.

Interest in a subject depends upon its giving one either pleasure or profit. Children seek pleasure first; childish adults continue to seek pleasure rather than profit. The child having a superior mind seeks profitable interests rather early in life. It has been said that 

Macaulay read English at three and a half years of age and that John Stuart Mill read Greek at four, while

Pascal worked out Euclid's propositions in plane geometry without a text at twelve and wrote a treatise on conic sections at sixteen. 

If a person is intelligent enough or lucky enough to have selected a vocation that he has inherent ability to succeed in, his interest is centered largely in his daily work; he is a fortunate man. Such man will then be able to take an interest in the world that reads and

KNOW THYSELF 

thinks for the sake of pleasure alone. There are, however, a few superior minds of the single-track variety; they can do one thing well but are not interested in general information. Aside from their one talent or ability they are hopelessly dull and uninterested. 

Interest in subjects outside of one's vocation demands both native mental ability and a background of mental experience and accumulated knowledge. Two men aged forty might, for example, discuss Les Miserables interestingly and profitably for an hour; a third man present who had not read the book, and who does not know 

Hugo from Lippmann, would either feel bored or humiliated with inferiority because he lacks a background of reading and association with reading men. 

It is sometimes lack of background rather than lack of ability that makes men of mature years reluctant to associate with others who have an extensive fund of knowledge. Lads of superior mentality in their late 'teens and early twenties are, fortunately, not so sensitive about what they do not know; they take for granted, and rightfully so, that they are not old enough to know very much; they readily, willingly, even eagerly, sit at the feet of Gamaliel.

The good mind both learns and retains worth-while information more readily than the poor mind does. 

To the good mind learning is a pleasure; to the poor mind, a task. The man with a good mind, unless he is a social barbarian, is nearly always received cordially by other persons having good minds; this association not only enables him to exchange ideas and to use what he knows, but it also stimulates him to learn more. This is another instance of-to him that hath shall be given. 

Intellectual versatility does not depend so much upon one's having a great deal of leisure time as the ignorant, who are on the defense, claim. Good minds use their time more profitably than poor minds do. One may see a goodly number of inferior and mediocre persons who,

THE STUDY 

101 because of luck, successful parasitism, inheritance of income, or the wealth of their parents, do not have to give very much thought and time-sometimes none at all-to the matter of making a living, and yet they are just as stupid and ignorant at the end of the year as they were at its beginning; they know very little about the world that thinks, nor do they even want to know. 

40. Persons having superior mentality are prone to consider well the source of a given statement before accepting or rejecting it. 

COMMENT. Small children usually believe whatever is told them-fairy tales and all; in early life children are not incredulous. It appears that their first distrust and disbelief have to do with the preservation of self; they fear injury and pain. A small child is likely to believe its mother's warning that pain may be expected in a new experience but will doubt her statement that pain will not result from a new experience. A physician notes that when it is necessary to do some painless thing to a child for the first time, that even after both he and the child's mother have told it that the thing to be done will not hurt it, the child does not believe either one of them until after the experience is over; even though the child does not cry out or have tantrums the observing physician notes its increased heart rate and its suppressed emotion. And this is observed in children who have intelligent parents as well as in children who have the brainless type who try to discipline or scare a child by threatening to call the doctor and have him hurt it in some way.

Small children at first think of the thing done rather than of the doer. But as they grow older, especially if they have good minds, they begin to think more and more critically of the person who may give them either

KNOW THYSELF 

physical pain or mental discomfort; they doubt, they are skeptical, they think intelligently, not only about physical pain but also about truth, and about other persons' ability and willingness to tell the truth. For truth told or heard at least gives one a starting point for intelligent thinking, while lying done or heard often gives one a sense of mental pain, bewilderment, and insecurity.

The world is so full of liars, lies, half-truths, unproved assertions, traditional beliefs, equivocation, and statements based on wishes and desire instead of knowledge, that the adult of good mentality and mature years usually puts doubt and skepticism before acceptance in the consideration of all statements that are not obviously true or self-evident; this attitude is reached and held for self-protection as well as through a sense of justice toward other persons.

One has not advanced far in the study of motives of human behavior until he finds there is no small amount of wilful, malicious lying on the part of envious, jealous, thwarted, and sadistic persons. These people run all the range from confidential whisperers to blackmailers; their motives are injury, retaliation, financial gain, and, with the sadistic, a perverted sense of pleasure.

A half-truth is one of the most dangerous of all lies; also it is one of the most common in use among persons who lie shrewdly, with malice aforethought. As to unproved assertions, one hears them all day and every day; they are the stock-in-trade of the rattle-brained. 

Traditional beliefs flaunt the false virtue of being intellectually respectable. A statement that is not true passes for truth among the ignorant and unthinking if a million persons repeat it. Many statements and ideas spoken of as generally believed are not true. Outside of simple mathematics, physics, and chemistry there are

THE STUDY 

103 few eternal verities and, except for the Golden Rule, they are neither known nor believed by the masses. 

So-called scientific knowledge, considered by the layman as most accurate of all, is largely guesses, presumption, assumption, unproved assertions, tradition, and bluff. The scientific truth of today may be considered an error tomorrow; this is the way of scientific progress. The true scientist is both fearless and heartless in trying to prove current beliefs and practices wrong. In this respect he differs widely from the religious teacher who fears to controvert religious tradition.

Equivocation is the way of the expert; it is studied deception in speech used often by persons in addressing the public orally or by means of the printed page. It is not used so much in private conversation where the speaker may be forced to explain definitely and clearly what he means to say.

And finally there is the statement based on wish or desire rather than on knowledge and truth. This is the way of the child and of millions of adults who still think and speak after the manner of children. With these people the wish or the desire comes first; truth and accuracy are of less-sometimes of little or noimportance. 

What manner of man is this who speaks? and what is his motive for speaking? are two questions that the superior mind asks over and over daily. Doubt, skepticism, and intelligent, frank denial must often precede the discovery and acceptance of truth. There are comparatively few persons who always judge a statement by its reasonableness, for it is usually easier and more pleasant either to accept or to ignore a statement than it is to prove it true or false. Nevertheless it is both intellectually and socially refreshing to talk with men. and women who know what not to believe, who know

KNOW THYSELF 

the difference between a wish and a fact, men and women who will neither lie nor repeat lies. 

41. Persons having superior mentality are prone to face important realities as they recognize and understand them.

COMMENT. When self-interest is involved children, childish adults, neurotics, and certain types of religious persons, apparently do not want to face disagreeable facts; wilful blindness, self-deception, or excessive optimism-which is a form of overcorrection-will likely be used as a way out of their difficulty. 

Facts may sometimes be the most disconcerting and disagreeable things that one meets in life. In scientific work one newly discovered fact can knock a score of traditional theories and practices into smithereens; medical history, for example, is replete with such happenings. And what is true of medical progress is true of all scientific progress, for scientists are not slaves to tradition.

In the world about us, omitting scientific matters, facts are often neglected or else ignored, especially by persons in the lower mental brackets. Why? Because to meet facts that involve our own interests one must have problem-solving ability, courage, a spirit of adventure, no overpowering dislike for change, freedom from excessive wish-thinking, and freedom from slavish obedience to tradition.

Refusal to face facts runs the whole gamut of life's activities, ranging in importance from a small boy's neglect to wash his hands and face, on up to suicide.

The most important mental defect in a person who refuses to face facts that he is aware of appears to be a lack of integrity. Millions of inferior and mediocre persons know what they should do in life; they know

THE STUDY 

105 the facts involved; they have enough intelligence either to solve the problem or to avoid the problem that they fail in, but they have not enough integrity to control their acts.

If one asks a criminal in prison if he knew the ethical and statutory laws involved in his becoming a prisoner, he frankly admits that he knew them; and he usually admits his lack of integrity in disobeying them. If one asks a panhandler if he knows the few things it is necessary to do in order to be self-supporting he frankly admits that he knows them, and then gives a score of childish, hackneyed excuses to exculpate himself; he too has not faced realities for he lacks integrity. All billbeaters and financial dead-beats know what they are regardless of persistent self-deception; they lack inherent integrity and pride. In this way one might cite scores and scores of life's failures, both great and small, in which the cause is the same-a reluctance or a refusal to face realities.

The good mind puts fact before fancy in serious matters; its excursions into the world of make-believe are carefully limited. A person having a good mind, unless it is his business to do so, would not be likely to attend the theatre or a concert every evening or read a new novel every day-all highly stimulating to the imagination and all useful in moderation; nor would he render himself ineffectual by idle daydreaming. His own inherent good sense would enable him to see that it is necessary to deal first with the things that are, things both agreeable and disagreeable, and that to try to live in a fool's paradise instead of facing realities is the way of fools-and newly-weds-and not the way of intelligent men and women.

It is easy to see why the superior person voluntarily faces important realities as he recognizes and understands them; but not so easy to see why inferior and many mediocre persons can not even be made to do so.

KNOW THYSELF 

It is unfortunate that Athena, the goddess of wisdom, is so sparing with her gifts. 

42. Persons having superior mentality early in life learn many things of importance to their own welfare that mediocre persons do not learn until several or even many years later in life, and that inferior persons never learn at all.

COMMENT. A superior youth is prone to seek personal association with superior adults of mature years. 

If this is not convenient the youth of the intellectual type will make the contact through the printed page; usually he does both. Youth keenly interested in learning and learned adults are attractive to one another. 

When John Stuart Mill was in his 'teens he not only had the advantage of association with his very learned father but he also passed many, many hours with Jeremy 

Bentham, at that time a man in his seventies. Bentham was very fond of the younger Mill.

And what is true of intellectual youth is also true of youth with outstanding talent for the fine arts. For example, a child or a youth highly gifted with talent for music seeks association with the best musicians available in his community or elsewhere. Also it may be noted that many children of ten to fifteen years of age can play the violin or the piano better than a person with only slight musical talent could ever learn to play them. Intellectuality is as much a gift as talent for music is, although this simple fact, either because of wish-thinking or ignorance, is usually overlooked.

It is interesting to a person who knows mental qualities to hear a youth of superior mentality talk on some of life's more serious matters, for not infrequently the youth's observations and comments are as intelligent as those of the average man old enough to be his father

THE STUDY 

107 or even his grandfather. One asks-why? Is it environment that has affected the youth so favorably? 

No; it is inherent ability. His environment has been no better than that of his brothers and sisters and no better than that of hundreds of other youths of his locality whose utterances are either immature or very childish. Besides, there is no record extant that the house servants in the homes of savants ever rise above the mental grade of house servants. Environment without inherent ability is of little influence. 

Superior youths not only learn readily from personal experience and observation, but they are willing, even eager, to learn from older persons who have good minds. 

In this respect they are very different than the common herd. It is well to note, too, that highly intellectual adults are no more likely to take a special interest in dull, stupid youths, unless they are paid to do so, than professional musicians would take in youth without good musical talent. Except as a means to an endthe end being a meal ticket-most superior persons are reluctant to waste their sweetness on the desert air.

Mediocre persons are prone to learn by experience rather than by reason; and this is a good way; in fact, the only way to learn many things. But experiential knowledge has its limitations; it is sometimes harmful, hence, the old saw to the effect that experience is a dear teacher. Mediocre and inferior persons have many undesirable experiences that a superior person's reason and insight enable him to avoid altogether. The superior person appears to be especially alert and canny in avoiding trouble. This observation, however, does not always apply to creative minds as anyone who has read biography widely will readily see. Many persons with creative minds are out of balance; they need a guardian. 

To one who knows mental qualities it is interesting to see mediocre men and women learning at forty or fifty years of age things of importance to them that they

KNOW THYSELF 

ought to have learned at twenty. At the rate at which they learn they would have to be as old as Methuselah before they would be reasonably fool-proof. 

Persons in the lower mental brackets are plainly hopeless. They seem to know very little more of the things they should know for their own welfare at sixty than they did at sixteen. They not only lack the will and the ability to learn, but they are also usually too stupid to use what little they know. 

43. Persons having superior mentality are prone to view with calm, judicial attitude both the faults and rights of others.

COMMENT. A keen, inherent sense of justice, like an unusual talent for music, may be seen in all grades of minds from high-grade morons on up to persons of genius rank. Environment appears to have little to do with one's having or not having the judicial temperament; it is a gift. One may occasionally see a laboring man, product of a laboring man's home, who has a sense of justice as accurate and inflexible on matters within his ken as that of a Supreme Court Justice; and one may also see a man who has had the advantages of good home life, good religious training, and good schooling who is almost devoid of the sense of justice. 

Many men can be just in theory but not in practice; they will discuss the subject in very dogmatic manner, usually condemning somebody else's lack of justice, but in their own personal practices they are as crooked as a barrel of snakes. Their voluntary dogmatizing on the subject is largely a cloak, a screen. Usually persons who are just and fair both in thought and practice do not talk about this personal virtue very much, it being a case where actions speak louder than words. Besides, a man who boasts of his virtues, either real or pre-

THE STUDY 

109 tended, usually has an ax to grind; he should be distrusted.

A man's sense of justice may be unfavorably influenced, even entirely broken down, by envy, jealousy, a desire for retaliation or revenge, and for pecuniary or other gain; it takes a strong character to resist these four things. Many persons will both lie and repeat lies because of envy or jealousy; where revenge is concerned any type of injustice may be used from slander to murder, and it is usually slander; and as to pecuniary gain, only the fear of punishment for perjury-and not one case in a hundred of perjury is punished-restrains blackmailers and the multitude of plaintiffs who are trying to get money from public service corporations, insurance companies, and the government because of disability claims. It is not strange, the habitually offcolored and the cynical say, that every man has his price. The statement is not true, however, for there are many men and women who are controlled at all times in both unimportant and important matters by a sense of justice. Society would go straight to perdition if it were not for this small minority. 

A man with judicial type of mind has a detached point of view; he is not by nature a partisan for he distrusts the opinions, the enthusiasm, and the hysteria of the herd; he withholds snap judgment, whereas the unjust person is eager to give an opinion. The just person frequently refuses to offer an opinion for he knows that he has no right to an opinion. Not so, the unjust person; not having a right to an opinion is a thought that does not enter his mind. The just man knows that safety, honor, and integrity frequently demand that he must not form and express an opinion, that he defer judgment.

The man of judicial temperament takes just as much pride in right judgment as a mathematician does in arriving at a correct mathematical conclusion; both want

KNOW THYSELF 

to be right. But the man who has loose theories and practices in justice is seemingly not embarrassed, or chagrined socially, because of his failure in judgment in ethical or moral matters involving other persons. 

The lack of a sense of justice in individual persons and between individuals and organizations or groups, while involving only a small number of persons, comparatively speaking, costs the taxpayers hundreds of millions of dollars every year. Here again the industrious, just citizen must pay taxes to defray court costs and law-enforcement costs made by unjust, dishonest persons. 

44. Persons having superior mentality, early in life, begin to shape their own destiny. 

COMMENT. In the light of our present-day knowledge the "divinity that shapes our ends, rough-hew them how we will" is our heredity. Either we are or we are not; we have or we have not; we are to be or we are not to be. The person having a superior mind appears to know, or at least to feel, by intuition that he is, that he has, that he is to be; and he proceeds to act accordingly rather early in life.

Inferior minds can learn but little because of sheer lack of ability; mediocre minds have more ability to learn than inferior minds have, but they are deficient in will to learn; superior minds have both ability to learn. and will to learn, ability to do and will to do. 

It is almost useless and nearly always disappointing to send young men and young women to college who have not both ability and will to learn. And yet at least two hundred thousand of this type are in American colleges every year. These inferior students would be as well off at home and the students having good minds in the colleges they attend would be a great deal better

THE STUDY 

111 off without them. If parents want to buy prestige, social veneer, and a little superficial worldly knowledge for their stupid sons and daughters of college age, they would get better results by sending them on a trip around the world; and if one trip did not produce satisfactory results, they could send them again—and again!

Nearly anyone can talk, look, and spend money, but it takes innate intelligence to keep one's mouth shut and listen and learn.

Academic training has been greatly overestimated as a necessary factor in personal development, in shaping one's destiny; and as a means for making a silk purse out of a sow's ear it is a failure. A sow's ear can be pickled but even then it remains on a lower gustatory and gastronomic level than tripe, and tripe has a very limited circle of loyal, steadfast admirers. Superior persons are not prone to boast of the advantages they have had for they discriminate very carefully between means and ends. One frequently, however, hears mediocre men voluntarily mention that they have been schooled here and there, graduated and post-graduated, and wonderfully and fearfully trained. Such boasting may be overlooked in a man under thirty, but after thirty it is time for a man to show what he has done and is doing rather than to continue childish boasting about what has been done for him. At thirty a man who is going to get anywhere in life should be on his way.

Ability to learn and the will to learn are manifested early in life in superior children. The worth-while things they learn will depend upon their interests and their environment. Most superior children who are interested in words and abstract ideas learn to read early; others show mechanical ability, ability in the fine arts, in nature study, or perhaps in the father's occupation.

Very few superior children escape commendation from someone-parents, teachers, or family acquaint-

KNOW THYSELF 

ances. By the time these children are from ten to fifteen years of age they know whether they have unusual ability in some way or not; they relate their daydreams, their life-plans to the abilities they have or that they think they have. In this respect they are very different from the inferior or the mediocre child whose day-dreams are entirely fantastic and who is without a definite life-plan. 

A child with ability and a definite goal works out his own destiny; he knows what he wants to do and why, for he has at least a little self-knowledge; he may not go all the way but he will go far; nothing but ill health can stop him. 

45. Persons having superior mentality are prone to recognize both differences and similarities between ideas as well as between objects. 

COMMENT. A small child may say that an apple is like the moon, but it is not able to say so readily why an apple is not like the moon. Children and adults with childish minds are prone to note resemblances between objects but they are prone to overlook differences. It is the recognition of differences rather than resemblances that often makes for accurate judgment; hence, one of the reasons that all inferior and many mediocre persons have poor judgment is their inability to recognize differences.

In conversation inferior and mediocre persons will usually speak of things being similar, will refer to resemblances, ten times as often as a person with superior mentality does. This is probably due in part to the fact that superior persons are prone not to speak of the obvious, whereas inferior persons have no definite rules about what to say and what to leave unsaid in conversation. But on the other hand superior persons think

THE STUDY 

113 and speak of differences in important matters, for they know that well-balanced judgment requires that this be done.

Superficial, snap judgment gets millions of people into trouble daily. These people are often said to have no more judgment than a child. When one looks into these cases one finds that their poor judgment is often the result of their seeing resemblances and in their not seeing differences. 

Is this singular ability-the ability to see differences -due to environment? The answer must be no; that it is largely a gift. As far as occupation gives extensive experience many men and women can see differences, for example, in flour, cloth, leather, paper, paint, etc.; that is, in the things they work with daily. But aside from the things they test daily their judgment is notoriously bad.

Inferior persons are prone to misquote statements they hear or read. This is not due always to their being intentionally careless about telling the truth; it is due in part to their inability to recognize the whole truth. 

If John Black states that he may go to New Orleans, he is quoted as having said that he will go to New Orleans. And with the inferior and many of the mediocre, what is true of unimportant matters is also true of important matters as far as understanding and quoting are concerned. Shades of meaning, accuracy in speech, telling the whole truth and nothing but the truth, mean little or nothing to them.

It is interesting to note, too, that if an inferior person's attention is called to some matter wherein he has overlooked an important difference in two things or two ideas, that he is not particularly concerned with his oversight. There appears to be something lacking in his mental integrity that prevents his want of exactness or accuracy from embarrassing him. This thing that is lacking-call it a blind spot or whatever one will also

KNOW THYSELF 

prevents intelligent self-criticism; for it may be observed that a child with this mental defect almost invariably retains the defect as an adult. Such persons appear to think that a little truth or a half-truth is as good as the whole truth.

One has a fairly accurate lead, as an evidence of mental superiority, if he finds that a child or an adult observes carefully and in detail the differences in objects and ideas with which he is not familiar, especially in matters that it would be to his advantage to know. 

Such person has what we doctors call distinctive ability in differential diagnosis; his judgment will enable him to know what's what and who's who, and to arrive at accurate conclusions. 

46. Persons having superior mentality recognize and esteem their own individuality.

COMMENT. It is quite as reasonable for a person to think he has a good mind as it is for him to think he has a good body, yet he may be mistaken about both. It is not ill-founded egotism for a person to recognize that he has at least a few good mental qualities and that he knows how to use them. Nearly all adults who solve the fundamental problems of life have a right to be somewhat proud of their ability. If in addition to this adults have mental qualities that enable them voluntarily to take an interest in and to appreciate some of the best thought of the past and the present they are of a higher type still; they live in two worlds, the world of thought as well as the world that works intelligently. 

They manifest choice, and intelligent choice is the most noticeable evidence of individuality. 

Choice in the mental world, in ideas, is the most difficult to exercise and is limited largely to superior minds. Choice in the material world is easier and far

THE STUDY 

115 more common; for example, both women and men, and more especially women, exercise and sometimes stress choice in their wearing apparel; they try to be individualistic; some succeed admirably and some do not.

Individuality in the upper mental brackets is manifested largely by affirmation; that is, the superior person manifests by statement or by act what he likes because he knows what he likes and why he likes it; he has definite ideas about books, magazines, music, drama, wearing apparel, friends, foods, and what-not, as well as about ideas and principles. But this affirmative attitude is not so noticeable in inferior and mediocre persons. They are prone to state what they do not like; their negativeness is largely a defense mechanism, an attempt to belittle the things they are unable to appreciate or to possess. This fact may be observed daily. 

The superior person aspires to be different from the herd. He wants to be able to feel, if not to say, that because of his own personal efforts, honestly and honorably made, he is a man apart. The desire may take the form of fame, scholarship, skill, social usefulness, or the approbation of his fellow man. Inferior and mediocre persons, as well as some of the paranoiacs, sometimes have the same desires but their desires are not realized, even though paranoiacs think they are. 

Superior persons usually take an intelligent view of their mental qualities. They exercise choice in the use and care of these qualities to the end that they appear to the best advantage. They treat their good mental qualities as they do their good physical qualities. Nearly every intelligent adult who has attractive physical qualities makes the most of them by means of choice and care, for the physique is often a distinctive part of one's individuality.

Efforts to change the individuality, especially to improve or to minimize physical defects and deficiencies,

KNOW THYSELF 

are seen in many persons; they are more evident than efforts to improve the mind. The small man, physically, may compensate by a swaggering, blustering manner; the lad with defective foot or leg may want to engage in athletics; the young man of delicate or effeminate type is prone to try to appear coarsely masculine; false teeth are both common and useful, while the use of hair dye runs the range from the grotesque to the becoming. Patrons of beauty parlors and patrons of libraries and art galleries are sometimes very different types of people; one group wants to be seen, the other heard; both are trying to improve their individuality. 

In general it may be said that superior persons want to exhibit their best qualities, both mental and physical, to the best advantage possible. This they usually do carefully and intelligently; they have and manifest individuality. 

47. In the superior mind change is the result of growth and transformation, rather than of sudden conversion and thoughtless acceptance of a new idea and denial of what was formerly believed. 

COMMENT. Children and childish adults often believe one thing today, something contrary tomorrow, and nothing for sure the day after. One can not tell from day to day what they really believe relative to many things they should have definite beliefs about; their opinions are received largely from other persons instead of being produced by their own reason and experience.

Persons who change their minds readily and easily in serious matters usually do not have much mind to change. One may see inferior and mediocre persons who "get religion" quickly and lose it as quickly as they got it; and the same is true of many other of their

THE STUDY 

117 beliefs, principles, and attitudes having to do with matters of considerable importance. 

All good minds are aware of their progress; they know where they are and how they got there; they have grown like a tree; they are not chameleons that change their color to agree with their surroundings. However, one may see many inferior or mediocre persons who have minds like a Christmas tree. They have sometimes collected a goodly number of mental ornaments, gewgaws, and baubles of various kinds-showy things that may be attractive to children and childish adults for a day or a week, but are of little permanent value to them. 

What passes for mental independence is sometimes only ignorance, stupidity, stubbornness, or reluctance to change an opinion. This type of mind is found in many mediocre persons who are dead at the top; they would not know what to do with a new idea if they produced or found one; they appear to think it an element of weakness to change, even for the better. There is more truth than flippancy in the statement frequently heard that many persons congeal mentally; they appear to be unable to take on new, worthwhile ideas if, to do so, requires that they give up an old idea; they have ceased to grow. 

There are many persons who dabble with worthy subjects either through a sense of transient curiosity or in order to affect a knowledge that they do not possess; they are of the Christmas tree type. These people apparently can not understand that thoroughness in any worth-while study is the result of years of thinking, work, and use of one's knowledge. They want to learn in a week or a month what it should normally take them years to learn. These people should be piously endured rather than ridiculed; for while it is true that a little learning is sometimes a dangerous thing-Pope over-

KNOW THYSELF 

stated the matter-it is not always so. Besides, all great masters of knowledge and skill were once beginners; and who is wise enough to recognize unerringly the master of the future? The master in his youth and early adult life feels, hopes, grows, and is silent; a fond mother believes in him; friends, acquaintances, and the public are prone to recognize his merits only when it would be stupidly envious not to do so.

Food for the mind is somewhat like food for the body. The body digests food, then assimilates and uses that which it needs; the food loses its identity. So too the mind gets nourishment from external sourcesfrom reading, from conversation, and through the five special senses; but this nourishment is changed and assimilated in such a way it becomes a part of the mind. 

Thinkers are seldom habitual quoters. Persons who are growing mentally can no more remember their mental food than they can remember their physical food. The mind not only transforms its own ideas from time to time, but it also transforms and uses ideas obtained from other persons.

If one's personal knowledge were thought of as a tree, it may be seen that some of the branches produced early in life die and fall off as do also, later in life, many small branches on the inside that are useless. But of all the branches the tree has, the one having to do with a person's philosophy of life continues to grow to the end. Very old persons who have had good minds and good advantages may have forgotten all their Latin, most of their mathematics and history, much miscellaneous knowledge, and they may no longer give any thoughts to the occupation or profession they followed, but their philosophy of life persists as a vital subject. 

And so it should be; for with a wrong philosophy of life, one really gets nowhere in the world either with or without money; but with a right philosophy of life,

THE STUDY 

119 the good things that one has are more enjoyable, and the defects and deficiencies in one's life are more endurable. 

48. Persons having superior mentality often improve themselves because of their having a sense of artistry or skill in doing some worth-while thing. 

COMMENT. Too often, especially among bookish persons, learning is linked up with books and one is thought to be learning only when he reads and to be learned only after having read widely and wisely. But most of our useful knowledge is not gained from books but from actual experience combined with a keen sense of observation; and this is true of the professional man as well as of the non-professional man. One can not learn to be a good physician, a lawyer, a teacher, an engineer, or a clergyman merely by reading books. 

There are in large machine shops men who have had about the same length of experience, some of whom are poor mechanics, some are good mechanics, and a few are very good mechanics. The poor mechanic is lacking both in ability and will to learn while the very good mechanic has both of these mental qualities. 

The question may be asked-what are the various motives of the very good mechanic that cause him to do his work so well? Does he hope to be promoted to foreman or does he want to earn more money? It may be that both of these motives come into his mind for both are laudable; but the probability is that he is also controlled by a sense of skill in his work. He wants to do and he actually takes pleasure in doing the best work it is possible for him to do, just as a musician in her 'teens who plays the piano well wants to play her very best at all times whether there is anyone listening or

KNOW THYSELF 

not. There is a strong appeal either of skill or of artistry in both instances.

Incidentally it is a matter of common knowledge among careful observers that skilful mechanics, that is, men who are skilful in reality rather than by a union's classification, are a very intelligent class of men and occupy a bracket in the mental scale considerably above manual laborers and a large proportion of the salaried white-collared class. The student of mental qualities would do well to give a great deal of thought to skill, for there is a close relationship between mental ability and physical dexterity.

Except where envy prevents it, both skill and artistry in some form are almost universally appreciated in other persons, even though one may not be noticeably skilful or artistic in any way himself. Note the interest in baseball, football, boat races, and concerts. The skilful workman is just as much pleased by the commendation and approbation of his fellow-workmen and acquaintances as is the ball player who can put the pellet over the fence, or the prima donna who brings her audience to its feet, and receives the applause of thousands. 

Nearly all inferior and many mediocre persons do not have a feeling of skill or artistry in any worth-while thing to stimulate their desire for self-improvement. If someone speaks to them relative to the desirability, even the necessity, of improving themselves, they will usually admit that it should be done and after having admitted it, they dismiss the subject from their minds. Although it has been tried for centuries, persons of this kind, young or old, can not be argued or disciplined into making noticeable improvement. They are the dumb, driven cattle-driven by necessity-that the poet speaks of. Fortunate is the person who has in his mental make-up both the ability and the desire to do some worth-while thing skilfully or artistically.

THE STUDY 

121 49. Adults having superior mentality are intelligently interested in what they have in common with other persons.

COMMENT. This mental quality is a part of the three major instincts, and in the upper mental brackets the element of self-preservation is the principal factor. Our philosophy of life has to do very largely with our relations to other persons; and in all our relations to other persons, what we have in common with them is the most important element to recognize and to deal with intelligently.

The ability to have agreeable relations with all persons that one meets from day to day is far more important than it is to have some beautiful theories, academic theories, taught theories, and traditional theories, about classes, sectional groups in our own nation, and the people of other nations; and yet this important fact is not recognized by millions of inferior and mediocre persons daily. 

Most persons have some very dear social prejudices and hatreds that they like to air in season and out of season. These prejudices and hatreds if real-rather than inane opinions uttered in idle conversation—are due largely to ignorance, envy, and a feeling of inferiority. One has not advanced very far in social practice based on knowledge of mental qualities unless he has learned to treat individual persons according to what they are. It is unwise both to underrate or to overrate anyone with whom social contact is necessary. Neither snobbishness nor fawning are social practices found often in the upper mental brackets, though they may be found fairly often in members of certain social groups who speak of themselves as "society." A prince does

KNOW THYSELF 

not belittle a peasant, nor does a savant ridicule an illiterate man because of his lack of learning. 

In addition to an intelligent recognition of self-interest there is usually present a distinct feeling of humor, sympathy, justice, or affection between individuals whose association is mutually pleasant regardless of mental, moral, social, or economic differences. Selfinterest is nearly always present in some form, even though it is carefully concealed, where social contact is voluntary and continuous; humor is a strong though a transient bond between many persons; human sympathy knows neither natural nor artificial differences in men, and even extends to animals and trees; justice is a feeling of the few; and love may be either a gift from the gods or a mockery from the devil, blind, fickle, and usually fleeting in dissimilar persons. So much for the right way.

As to wrong conditions, it appears to be quite as easy for a person to fail socially by overestimating what he has in common with other persons as by underestimating it. Idle curiosity, ill-considered selfishness, excessive familiarity, misplaced pity, an unwise flare for uplifting and helping the downtrodden, on the part of the superior very often lead to disappointment and disillusionment. On the other hand a bold attempt on the part of the inferior to become parasitical-unintelligent selfishness-usually leads to ill will.

It takes integrity to say no at the right time; and it is often as difficult to say no to oneself as it is to say it to somebody else. An impulse or a desire to help someone is not always right; but ill will manifested toward anybody, either by word or deed, is nearly always wrong except as a protective measure. It takes a well-balanced mind that has learned much, both by experience and reason, to hew to the line, to develop and to practice a satisfactory social philosophy of life.

THE STUDY 

123 50. Persons having superior mentality are prone to differentiate carefully between essentials and nonessentials. 

COMMENT. Small minds are prone to magnify the importance of non-essentials and to fail to recognize or at least to recognize the importance of essentials. 

The student of mental qualities can easily see that this fault has many causes; he should with a little study be able to name some of the more important of them. 

A small child may have tantrums and refuse to eat its breakfast food because it is not served in the bowl it is accustomed to use, while another child of about the same age, even in the same family, is not at all particular about the dish in which its food is served. Parents of the first child will give the excuse that the child is young, that it does not know any better, and that it will outgrow the defect. Unfortunately the child will probably not outgrow it and when it reaches adult life it will retain its faultfinding, captious habit. 

A lad of twelve years may complain and even disobey when he is asked to do some chores around his home at a time when he wants to play. He can not see it is essential that the work be done and that if he does not do it his mother will have to do it. As far as he is concerned playing is more important than working, and unfortunately he is likely to have the same idea in adult life, in which case he will probably lose his job-if he is fortunate enough to have one-for his mother will not be present to do his work for him. Play-boys are found in all economic strata of society; the wealthy ones are envied and the very poor ones are dead-beats or parasites living off legitimate business, relatives, the taxpayers, or the charities.

Men of this type of mind between the ages of twenty and forty, aside from the two extremes just mentioned,

KNOW THYSELF 

are found on every hand. Many of them find suitable places in the world that works. Socially they are tolerated, sometimes resented, by their equals and inferiors, and shunned by their superiors. The superior person inherently dislikes small talk, foolish provincialisms, quibbling, haggling, caviling, pettiness, fussiness in men, and cattiness in women. These are mental faults always but social faults only to persons who do not like them. Social dislike is more often due to mental differences than to moral or economic differences. 

And as to the aged of this mental type there is but little to say, for their day's work is done; their faults should be, and usually are, endured patiently by younger persons.

Adults with childish minds are prone to be continuously involved in non-essentials. In their daily work, in their conversation, in their social, political, and religious discussions and activities, as well as in their thinking when alone, they are often unable to see and to do the essential things. Their penchant for trivialities makes them uninteresting, unpleasant, unprofitable, and sometimes thoroughly intolerable to persons having discriminating minds. They are often nagging faultfinders in their home life or with their fellow-employees; they are penny-wise and pound-foolish; they save at the tap and waste at the bunghole; they make mountains out of molehills. All languages have proverbs and maxims that describe their defects, weaknesses, and inferiority. But they are as numerous now as they were centuries ago for they are born and not made; they have a distinctive type of mind; they can not be changed noticeably by environment, by argument, or even by the discipline of necessity.

If one is seeking a companion, an employee, or a business or professional associate, let not this point be overlooked: avoid the quibbler, the man who does not

THE STUDY 

125 voluntarily discriminate between essentials and nonessentials. 

51. Persons having superior mentality are prone to be exact and to require exactness of others. 

COMMENT. Science and the fine arts are the special domain of this mental quality. Mathematical conclusions must be exact to be useful; chemical analysis requires accuracy; and scientific apparatus, even by the layman, are often spoken of as instruments of precision. 

The scientist may think in terms of analysis or synthesis, in terms of cause or effect, but always he must think with accuracy if his thinking is to lead him to truth.

Persons richly endowed with talent for one of the fine arts feel and appreciate an exactness in their artistic sphere that those of us not so endowed are not, nor can we be made to be, aware of. In fact the most important single element of all art is exactness in detail in order that the whole may be perfect; this is seen in music, painting, sculpture, etching, and the use of language.

The artist is very sensitive about flaws, defects, and inaccuracies in his work. He seeks perfection, and although he rarely finds it or produces it, he continues to try to do so; it is the way of the artistic mind. 

In the world about us, aside from science and art, accuracy in thought, speech, and act is the exception rather than the rule. If a person demands exactness of inferior and mediocre persons he is not likely to get it unless he has disciplinary authority or the right to hire and fire. Inferior and mediocre persons usually resent demands for accuracy from them and speak of their superiors as being exacting, fussy, and hard to get along with.

A soldier may fear company inspection, for his cap-

KNOW THYSELF 

tain is always exacting in his disciplinary power. 

Nurses in hospital service, and more especially student nurses, may fear the superintendent's morning rounds for the same reason that a soldier fears his captain.

Clerks in a large office may fear the chief clerk, and those who handle money and checks may fear an audit of the firm's accounts.

Differences in mental qualities and abilities make for friction, strife, the necessity for discipline, and loss of employment in the world that works. Poor minds are apparently unable to see that essential details must be done right; they take no pride in their work; they only fear being found out, nor do they fear that, unless discipline is in store for them. It is one of the very best leads in determining the quality of the mind, both in larger children and adults, to know how the person being judged voluntarily deals with essential details. 

Over and over in the study of mental qualities it may be observed that good minds do worth-while things voluntarily that mediocre minds will do only when under discipline, and that inferior minds either can not or will not do satisfactorily whether discipline is used or not.

Success or failure in nearly any worth-while work or undertaking from housekeeping to commanding an army in battle depends very largely upon how well the necessary details involved are done. Nothing is more annoying and exasperating to employers than to have employees who, because of ignorance, stupidity, or indifference, fail in essential details; and very few phases of administrative work are more satisfactory to persons who have administrative responsibility than to know that essential details will be done right by employees. Great administrators nearly always have competent advisers and capable assistants; but their hardest problem to solve is to get employees who have intelligence, integrity, and fidelity to do the detail work. A

THE STUDY 

127 major-general and a score of colonels never won a battle even though they usually are given-and modestly accept-the credit for doing so. 

52. The social attitude of the superior mind is altruistic rather than indifferent, parasitic, or misanthropic.

COMMENT. Superior persons are prone to have a detached, judicial attitude toward the social instinct. 

Probably the first intelligent thought that comes into the mind of a superior youth regarding this matter is that he must control his social instinct lest it interfere with his carrying out his life-plan. Later, often several years later, he begins to re-think his taught opinions relative to his duty to society. 

It is normal to want to live with people; it is abnormal to want to live as a hermit; it is normal for good people to want to live with good people. The social medium in which one lives is as important to him as the soil in which a tree lives is to the tree; in fact, trees and people are very much alike in matters of growth. 

All superior persons see that it is, their duty to make some contribution to the maintenance and improvement of the best social advantages, practices, and customs of their locality. The best contributions to society are those that have to do with furnishing a good medium in which superior children may be born and reared. The most valuable contributions to society are made by philosophers, scientists, and prophets-men like Mill, Pasteur, and Phillips Brooks or, of present-day Americans, Dewey, Banting and Fosdick. Society's greatest need is for men who can think right and who can lead other men to think right.

The scientific attitude toward social betterment is to prevent trouble rather than to try to cure it. Up to 1880

KNOW THYSELF 

the best physicians as well as thousands of laymen went about daily dosing malaria patients with quinine; that was the best they knew to do. That year, however, Laveran discovered that the mosquito carried the organisms that cause malaria. The solution of the problem was then clear: get rid of mosquitoes. We still have millions of nice old grandmas of both sexes who are going about with a teaspoon and their favorite nostrum trying to solve the social ills of humanity. But the great amount of poverty and crime that prevails to-day indicates a need of drastic prevention rather than temporary relief by outworn methods.

Social ills and evils can neither be prevented nor cured by pious wishing and hoping any more than insanity, cancer, malaria, or tuberculosis can. Nor can they be prevented by legislation. Noble experiments to make inferior people morally good and economically secure by the enactment of laws are a failure. Society must prevent the production and reproduction of inferior stock-idiots, imbeciles, morons, the insane, epileptics, habitual parasites, and habitual criminals. The load of parasitism in America is heavier than the upper social and economic strata can bear. A boat made to carry four passengers may, in emergency, carry one or two extra for a short distance, but an attempt to carry four extras would probably result in disaster for all the passengers. Or, to change the figure of speech somewhat, the Ship of State ought to put in to dry-dock and have the barnacles removed.

Very few men can make a large and lasting contribution to the welfare of society. There are relatively few philanthropists and few great thinkers. Aside from paying taxes and contributing to charities most persons can do little more than to help maintain the advantages we now have, to avoid anti-social, misanthropic acts and influence, and privately to help a few individual persons who are both willing and able to help themselves. And

THE STUDY 

129 as to the last-mentioned activity one recalls some lines from Wordsworth applicable to millions of intelligent, thoughtful men and women: 

That best portion of a good man's life, His, little, nameless, unremembered acts 

Of kindness and of love. 

53. Persons having superior mentality have a spirit of adventure that causes them to reach out for new experiences in the world of reality. 

COMMENT. Inferior as well as many mediocre persons do not want to leave the beaten path; to do as others have done and are doing is the limit of their aspirations. 

And, taking all things into consideration, this is probably the best way for them to do, lest they prove themselves to be foolhardy in many ways. But superior persons are prone to seek new ideas, new ways, and to do worth-while things that are out of the ordinary; they have originality; they have both the desire and the ability to venture into new fields of endeavor. 

The difference between the superior mind that ventures and succeeds and the mediocre mind that ventures and fails appears to be due largely to three things: inherent ability to solve problems, careful study of the feasibility of the thing to be undertaken, and thorough preparation for the work to be done. 

Mediocre persons sometimes dream dreams and see visions of new ventures, of new things they would like to do; they make many plans but most of their plans for reasons just stated "gang aft a-gley"; they fail.

One may note in observing children that the child who is by nature a leader calls to his playmates—"Let's do, etc."; the others follow the leader. But occasionally one sees a child who does not follow the leader-not be-

KNOW THYSELF 

cause of envy, but because he has originality; he refuses to follow the herd; he wants to do something alone, something different. The adult who showed originality as a child will be very likely to venture and succeed in some worth-while undertaking. Leaders in and of the herd are not often leaders in the world of original thinking. The child or the adult who has originality lives in a world apart; he has neither time nor inclination to mingle with the herd more than social necessity requires; besides, he is likely to have a well-founded distrust for leaders of men, and more especially for leaders who appeal to the ragged rabble, for he knows they are usually mountebanks with an ax to grind.

Adults who have good minds usually have a feeling of dissatisfaction with their own knowledge and their personal achievements. This is especially true of persons having creative or scientific minds. They feel an urge to venture forth in some new undertaking. Usually their venture, if successful, has in it an element of personal gain, as, for example, a business venture that produces money. At other times their venture may be undertaken for the good of society or, perhaps, for fame. Superior persons are usually not so much interested in being as they are in doing. 

But often the controlling motive in undertaking a new venture is not the probability nor the reality of its producing money or fame, but the joy, the satisfaction, that comes from having done some worth-while thing that had not been done so well before by the person doing it or by anyone else known to him. And this brings us back to a former observation on the will to make personal improvement, namely, the pleasure derived from acquiring skill, the joy felt because of doing something well. It is a form of conquering, of overcoming obstacles, of ultimately winning. It is the spirit of adventure.

The spirit of adventure, like so many other good

THE STUDY 

131 mental qualities, is an inherent thing. One either has it or he does not have it; it can not be acquired. Nearly all persons are more or less adventurous in their daydreams and sometimes do great things in their world of phantasy. But in the world of reality there are comparatively few persons who voluntarily leave the beaten path and actually do a new, an original, worth-while thing. 

54. Persons having superior mentality are prone to be intelligently self-critical.

COMMENT. The word criticism connotes discriminating judgment, not hostile judgment as the word commonly implies in popular usage. Much so-called criticism is only ignorant or, perhaps, malicious fault-finding. An intelligent critic sees and commends the good qualities in a thing upon which he passes judgment as well as noting its faults and defects. 

Persons with superior mentality frequently ask themselves the question-have I a right to an opinion in this matter? And the question being answered in the negative causes them to withhold judgment until they become better informed on the subject. But this discretion is not used by inferior and many mediocre persons; they do not often question their right to an opinion, hence, they give opinions on many subjects about which they know little or nothing. 

The right to give an opinion ought to come into one's mind every time the thought of giving an opinion arises. 

Because superior persons are usually reluctant to give opinions in matters on which they are poorly informed, their opinions are usually respected by intelligent persons; it is sometimes said of them that they speak little but say much. On the other hand inferior and many mediocre persons, especially if they are socially stupid

KNOW THYSELF 

and garrulous, broadcast opinions daily on every subject that arises in a conversation or in a group conference. Occasionally one hears one of these opinionated asses say that he has no right to an opinion and then proceed to give one with much positiveness and finality; or else he may reverse the procedure, give the opinion first, and then admit that he has no right to an opinion in the matter. In either case the desire to give an opinion overrules what little social discretion and intellectual integrity he has.

Intelligent self-criticism has much to do with keeping one socially sane and maintaining one's integrity, provided one compares himself to persons having superior qualities rather than inferior. There is no particular good derived from one's believing that he is better than a notorious criminal or intellectually superior to a moron; but there is real worth in one's recognizing that he is not as good in his life or in his work as someone considerably above him. Self-complacency that rests on someone else's failure or inferiority does not stimulate personal growth; noble aspirations come from looking up toward the top of the ladder, not down. The frequently-heard expression from the inferior, from persons who can not solve their problems, from habitual failures, that they have much company of their particular kind, must be very poor consolation. 

Is there such a thing as delusive self-criticism? The question will have to be answered in the affirmative. 

There is an element of truth in the current term inferiority complex. But there is more truth in the wag's jest to the effect that a person who has an inferiority complex merely sees himself as he is. People who can do worth-while things usually have the will to do them; there are but very few exceptions to the rule. And long ago it was observed that where there is a will there is a way.

The other extreme, self-conceit, is so common that it needs no comment here.

THE STUDY 

133 This good thing, too, may be observed about intelligent self-criticism: the more of it one has, the less of hostile criticism he is likely to receive from the herd that surrounds him daily. This last observation is especially worth knowing and using. 

55. A person having superior mentality does worthwhile things of his own free will and accord that a person of mediocre mentality may do because he is required to do them, and that a person of inferior mentality either can not or will not do at all. 

COMMENT. This is one of the most characteristic qualities of a superior mind and may be manifested in all ages from the fifth or sixth year on up, and may be observed in superior persons several times daily. One may, for example, see an eight year old lad voluntarily remove a dead limb of a tree that has fallen across the sidewalk, or he may pick up a piece of glass or a piece of board with a nail through it lying in the street that might injure a motor car tire. A lad with a poor mind would have to be told to do these things. 

But childhood has no monopoly on stupidity. Many adults are as stupid as children and many of the stupid ones do not even take enough interest in worth-while things to ask Why? as a small child often does. A good mind sees that a certain thing should be done and then proceeds to do it voluntarily; the mediocre mind can see the merit in doing a worth-while thing after somebody else has done it or after it has been explained to him; but the inferior person apparently can neither understand nor appreciate many worth-while motives and purposes, acts and deeds, even after having them. explained.

One hears many complaints from superior persons about the lack of initiative in the mediocre and the in-

KNOW THYSELF 

ferior. The mediocre not infrequently have initiative to follow the beaten path, but new thoughts, new ways, and the production of new things voluntarily are not a part of their mental make-up. Their defect is not so much a lack of will to do as it is of inability to see clearly what to do, for one frequently hears them say, apparently with regret, "I didn't think about that." One can not by complaining and scolding make a person have initiative, if he does not have it naturally, any more than one can by complaining, scolding, and wishing, make a person have musical talent when he was not born with it.

People like to do things they can do well; the pleasure derived from skill or artistry is a part of the reward and the thing created is the reward of the creative mind. 

It is as natural for a creative mind to be engaged in creative thought—and creative work, if possible-as it is for a duck to swim. A duck may have its craw well filled with grain and other food and yet it takes to the water for pleasure if not for profit. So too a person with a creative mind may have enough money so that he does not need to work to make a living, yet he wants to create something. A person richly endowed with talent for one of the fine arts wants to use his talent. A person with outstanding mechanical ability also wants to use it; he wants to make something, to make something work or run, to see the wheels go round.

Successful business men have always advised young men starting out in the business world as salaried employes to do all the work assigned them, and then to do something in addition to it. Useless advice. The man with a good mind does this anyway, while the employe with a poor mind does as little as he can to hold his job and watches the clock!

A person's social value depends very largely upon the useful things that he does voluntarily. One is required to pay taxes and one is expected to obey laws and, if

THE STUDY 

135 able, to support local charities; those are customs of the herd. But superior persons have a way of doing a great deal of good of their own accord-things that are not obligatory, ranging in importance from little acts of courtesy in the home life to many acts of helpfulness done for individual persons, the community, and for society at large. No one had to tell Dr. Johnson to compile a dictionary; no one urged Carnegie to build libraries; no one insisted that Lindbergh fly across the Atlantic; no one told Father Damien to spend his life among the lepers. The best of all acts and deeds are done voluntarily.

Persons who have good minds can no more be satisfied with the mental standards and activities of persons who have poor or mediocre minds than an athlete could be satisfied to do as the sick and the physically inferior do. 

56. Persons having superior mentality usually have a strong desire for helpful influence, to be accepted voluntarily, rather than for power that must be submitted to. 

COMMENT. There is apparently much truth in

Nietzsche's statement that "The will to power is the primitive motive force out of which all other motives have been derived." A person who had given but little thought to this statement would probably think that the will to power was concerned entirely with having compelling power over other persons, but that is not the case, at least not at this time. The word power often has an evil import, for power in action frequently means force. No one wants another person to have power over him nor does an intelligent man, outside of his personal or official duty, want power over another. For power over other persons often carries with it a feeling

KNOW THYSELF 

of responsibility for their welfare, and most superior persons want to avoid unnecessary responsibility. The primitive motive force-power-both in the individual and the race is being refined into a gentler influence, an influence to be accepted voluntarily rather than involuntarily submitted to.

Good minds both seek and accept good influence voluntarily. They seek this influence from persons, from books, from works of art, and from nature. To seek good influence voluntarily is an evidence of superior mentality; not to seek it or to reject it when it is available is characteristic of inferior and many mediocre persons. A person with a superior mind will usually do the right thing voluntarily, or because of suggestion, that an inferior person must be forced to do. 

From the viewpoint of mental rating it is not to the credit of millions of our people that they are starving intellectually and spiritually in a land where opportunities and advantages for improvement are to be found on every hand.

Some of these people are blamable and some are not. A person is not blamable for his poor mental heredity any more than he is blamable for poor physical heredity; nor can the inferior be blamed because the superior do not teach and discipline them properly. There is a great deal of determinism in this matter that stupid wish-thinkers either can not or will not see.

Superior persons' will to power is directed very largely to the mastery of their environment; they do not seek power over other persons; they seek security, peace, and the opportunity to develop. Socially they aspire to have a beneficent influence on their fellow men, an influence that is to be accepted voluntarily, however. 

This is the hope of scientists, philosophers, the clergy, artists, and a vast number of influential men and women in private life. Except when official, professional, and parental duty requires it, very few superior persons

THE STUDY 

137 want to be disciplinarians; they inherently dislike to compel obedience to their way of thinking and doing. 

It is a matter of common knowledge that even superior parents of superior children are always glad when their children reach an age that their discipline is only a matter of following the parents' suggestions.

Cruelty is based on power. Many small children are inherently cruel and manifest this feeling toward other children and toward their own or other children's pets -cats, dogs, rabbits, and birds. Power enables many inferior and mediocre persons to have a domineering, rule-or-ruin attitude toward other persons. With power a blackmailer or a kidnaper gets money; power enables a political dictator to oppress many thousands of men and women who are superior to him mentally; because of power Germany overran Belgium in 1914. Except in the higher mental brackets it seems to be easy for many persons to revert to the primitive way of the race and their early childhood feeling in order to get what they want and to punish their real and fancied enemies. 

American people on the whole are getting away from the use of force, rigid discipline, and strict requirements in dealing with children and childish adults, and they are paying the penalty for this stupid, wish-thinking type of social training and conduct.
