We now have millions of inferior and mediocre people who have no respect either for God or man. This is one more illustration of the fact that any virtue carried to the extreme becomes a vice. All persons need discipline; there are no exceptions to the rule. Superior persons usually discipline themselves and require but little discipline from other persons. Inferior persons should be disciplined by their mental superiors from early infancy to old age. Any undisciplined fool is a potential social danger whether he is at the steering wheel of a motor car or not; and many undisciplined fools with a mountebank as a leader may cause a local or national calamity.

KNOW THYSELF 

57. Persons having superior mentality are usually endowed with one or more artistic talents. 

COMMENT. Artistry is a subject that has been much neglected in the study of the mind. This neglect is due in part to the ignorance and sometimes aggressive condemnation of artistic things by the cult of the lowbrow. Too often it is thought that only musicians, painters, and sculptors have artistic talent, but that is not true. It is true that the most richly endowed minds in artistic talent are creative, that they make something new, something original, something that is beautiful and usually useful; for example, an overture, a painting, or a statue. But there is another type of artist, a person who does things artistically rather than makes artistic things: for example, Bernhardt, Duse, Nordica, Caruso. It would be true, however, in dramatic art to say that when Bernhardt played Camille she was Camille -Camille incarnate. When one considers the subject in its entirety it is found that primarily the artistic talent is a desire and the ability to do well certain worth-while things usually having to do with beauty. 

Virtue is its own reward-according to an old bromide. The same in part is true of artistry, for there is a great deal of self-satisfaction in doing well both beautiful things and useful things. But there is in artistry more than self-satisfaction, for artistry is one of the most appealing of the mental qualities. 

A good, artistic public speaker, a good orator, or a good singer not only pleases himself, but he pleases his audience also. A person who has good manners—a very necessary element in social artistry-not only pleases himself, but he pleases his associates also. A person who knows how to dress artistically is the observed of all observers. A person who is an artist socially has all the companions he needs and usually of

THE STUDY 

139 the kind he finds agreeable. A person who uses his language both accurately and artistically is not only pleased with his ability to do so, but he also has a valuable personal and social asset. A person who has artistry in his physical movements, who can walk like a tiger instead of waddling like a duck, who can dance, skate, bow, and nod or salute gracefully, whose gestures, if any, are an addition to his speech rather than the meaningless movements of an awkward imbecile, who can sit down as gracefully as a cat and rise as gracefully as a leopard, has much to his social advantage. 

A person who has daily work in which an element of artistry is used has a reward, a pleasure, in addition. to that of the daily wage. 

Artistry is sometimes a tender plant that develops best when well supported and surrounded by scores of other good mental qualities. It is the orchid of the mental greenhouse. But if it is not well supported it may become a life-long disappointment and troublemaker bewailing its lack of opportunity for expression. 

One sees this last-mentioned condition in many persons richly endowed with talent for one of the fine arts and yet without enough general intelligence to be selfsupporting or even to get along agreeably with their associates in daily life. Artists who are temperamental lack general intelligence. A moron richly endowed with musical talent is still a moron. There is nothing in the possession of artistic talent and artistry, itself, to warrant a person's acting like a fool. 

General intelligence and integrity make for growth, usefulness, and stability in life; but artistry and beauty, aside from the feeling of physical well-being, make for most of life's pleasures. Most persons merely accept and use, and that almost unconsciously, the beauty about them, as they use the air about them. But if they were deprived of the trees, the green lawns, the flowers, the shrubs, the artistic homes, the good-looking human

KNOW THYSELF 

beings, good manners, and other things of beauty that surround them daily, they would feel somewhat as a person would who was deprived of pure air and good food. Life to most superior persons would be intolerable without beauty. 

58. Men and women of superior mentality usually have a keen sense of personal integrity; they try to maintain their standards and ideals of Truth, or Goodness, or Beauty. 

COMMENT. Integrity, like truth, is only a relative thing. Integrity has to do with more things than obedience to the current moral code of one's locality. The good mind knows there are intellectual standards and artistic standards as well as moral standards to be upheld. Too often one thinks of integrity only as applied to morals and more especially to money and to sex.

An artist may have inflexible integrity about his art but be lax about his moral integrity; his greatest interest and knowledge have to do with art, not morals. The highly intellectual person maintains rigid intellectual integrity, may know little and feel less about artistic integrity, and is not greatly interested in morals and moralizing. And persons who feature moral standards, rather than intellectual and artistic standards, are prone to judge the world by their own measuring rod. 

Many of the great artists of the past had unusual integrity in matters of art but their lack of moral integrity was sometimes flagrant; of this type was 

Cellini. Human nature does not change noticeably, for even today the great artists of all kinds are judged, appreciated, and honored because of their art rather than because of their adherence-or in spite of their lack of adherence to conventional moral standards; the faults

THE STUDY 

141 in their private lives are of interest only to morbid gossips of the "did-you-know" type.

Nearly all persons from the moron grade on up try to maintain integrity relative to some particular thing. 

There is said to be honor among thieves. A house servant may boast that she never sweeps the dirt back of a door and leaves it there. An habitual tippler may boast that he never becomes completely soused and down and out. In this way one might mention scores and scores of real or imaginary virtues that are claimed, defended, and adhered to by individual persons. 

The author has known one man who was a congenital liar, yet he would not say an evil thing that would injure anyone; he was a thief; he was dishonest in money matters; there was almost no evidence that he had any morals, using the word morals in the popular sense; he was a drunkard; he was boastful and he was a braggart. And yet he had a feeling of loyalty and helpfulness toward his friends that was sublime. He did not speak of this outstanding virtue that he had; he thought he held it as a secret, and yet his good acts could be seen by many of his acquaintances. 

There are two kinds of personal integrity: one is due to fear and policy, while the other is due to an innate feeling. The former is seen most often in persons of inferior or mediocre mentality and usually has to do with morals, while the latter is usually found in superior persons and has to do with intellectual and artistic matters; the former may be readily broken or discontinued when fear and policy no longer require its maintenance, whereas, the latter, being innate, is not easily broken.

Personal integrity is an enjoyable thing and is often the object of self-esteem. Persons who have strong integrity enjoy having it tested both by personal temptation and by external inducement. They enjoy announcing a thundering, unmistakable No! after which they

KNOW THYSELF 

have a keen sense of satisfaction just as a strong healthy person has after moving or helping to move a piano. 

In the final analysis, then, it may be observed that the superior mind maintains integrity in a greater number of things than mediocre and inferior minds do, and that its integrity is largely inherent rather than being due either to fear or to policy. 

59. Persons having superior mentality usually have an intelligent attitude toward their creative desires and use them for the achievement of worthy ends.

COMMENT. The creative energy of a pair of birds is used in building their nest, physical reproduction, and the care of their offspring. This they repeat every season as long as they live; they do not reason why. 

Procreation is seemingly only an incident, a phase, of creative desire. Small children who know nothing about sex want to create things. Men and women past middle life who have little or no personal interest in sex continue to create if they have creative minds. 

For twenty or thirty years, beginning at puberty, it is instinctive that a part of one's creative energy be used in the perpetuation of the species after the manner of birds. Among primitive people and among those in the lower mental brackets in a civilized people there is apparently but little reason and self-restraint used to control this instinct. In the matter of reproduction superior persons usually have intelligent practices based on praiseworthy standards. They are prone to limit their offspring to the number that they are able to provide for according to approved standards of their time and place; they do not produce a swarm of children to live in poverty or to be supported by the taxpayers and charities.

Civilization makes the curbing of the sex instinct

THE STUDY 

143 imperative largely because of economic reasons, and the sex desires of adults may not with intelligence be gratified to the detriment of society. The fact that this instinct is not controlled intelligently in the lower twenty-five percent of our population is no credit to the upper classes. It will take a great deal more than complaining and a timid, wish-thinking attitude toward the mentally inferior to solve the problem. 

Inferior persons seldom create anything worth while. 

The human race and society as a whole would be a great deal better off without their contribution of children. In the upper mental brackets many persons are both happy and often most useful socially whose creative energy is spent on brain children rather than on physical offspring. The creative activity of Burbank made him one of the most useful men in modern times, and the same may be said of Edison. The brain children of authors-books, essays, poems, and what-not-sometimes die a-borning. A kind but firm editor gives them a lethal dose of a soporific in the form of a rejection slip. 

Other brain children of authors survive in the world of usefulness a year or two, and some a generation; but very few, out of the vast number written, are of much interest even to the kind of reader for whom they were originally intended after they have been printed a century.

Psychologists have written much about restraining, directing, and sublimating the reproductive urge as though it were a thing that could be done by the average person in a way that would transform him into a creative genius; in fact, a few years ago we had an epidemic of sublimating talk among our young intellectuals. Superior persons of the creative type usually control themselves in this respect voluntarily; but no amount of teaching, training, and discipline-sublimating-can change the reproductive energy, desires, and activity of a mediocre person in such way that he

KNOW THYSELF 

will produce the creative work of a genius. No; the theory is beautiful but not true. No one yet has been able to explain originality and how and why it produces new thoughts and new things in any other way than that it is a special gift. A person either has originality and the creative desire or he does not have them; and if he does not have them he would do well to direct his activities into some other field of endeavor. 

60. Adults having superior mentality are prone to judge, to restrain, and to subordinate both their personal and social opinions and actions of emotional origin. 

COMMENT. There are very few persons who are not controlled at times by sentiment and emotion. Human icicles may be both admirable and useful but they are not especially likable. 

Feelings, like reason-produced thoughts, may be either good or bad. The world is cursed by more bad feelings than by bad reason-produced thoughts; malicious gossip is more common than bank-robbing or kidnaping. Babies feel before they reason; in fact, all small children are controlled by feelings for several years before they are controlled to any considerable extent by reason. 

Millions of adults are only children mentally. They are controlled largely by their feelings-their wishes, hates, fears, jealousies, envies, prejudices, and, of course, by their affections. Inferior and many mediocre persons do not want the bright, analytical light of reason thrown on their feelings, for their feelings both good and bad are dear to them. They talk about and greatly enjoy their personal hates, their patriotism, their fervid religious and political enthusiasms, even their griefs in some cases, and their prejudices and ill will toward persons they do not like. Many persons are willing to

THE STUDY 

145 admit they were either wrong or ignorant intellectually on a given point, but few are willing to admit they were or are wrong emotionally.

The intellect through reason and experience can criticize and correct itself far more easily than it can criticize and correct the emotions. In inferior and mediocre persons emotion and sentiment readily overpower the judgment and control the conduct daily both in important as well as in unimportant matters. 

Children's actions and utterances, except those that are the result of habit, are largely of emotional origin and only the exceptional child under twelve years of age is noticeably reason-controlled. In judging the adult it is well to observe carefully to note if, under ordinary conditions, his acts and utterances are produced by his emotions, by somebody else's reasoning that he is using, or by his own reasoning at the time. 

The best type of mind reasons for itself; the mediocre mind is prone to use somebody else's reasoning, that is, he is a follower of the beaten path and he is a quoter; and the inferior mind is likely to be very largely emotion-controlled.

Neither intelligence nor the desire for intelligence is contagious; there are no mass movements to learn useful things. Those of us who have tried for years to teach the public how to avoid or to deal with disease, for example, cancer, tuberculosis, and malaria, find the masses rather apathetic to learning things they should know for their own welfare. But emotions are contagious. It appears to be as easy for hundreds, thousands, even millions of people to hate, to fear, or to rejoice together as it is for two persons. Wars are originated and supported largely by the emotions of the masses; so, too, are religious persecutions. Group hatreds and fears may ultimately destroy civilization. 

It is interesting-as a mental study, only-to hear a man of forty-five manifest and recite the same inane

KNOW THYSELF 

envies, hatreds, prejudices, and stupidities that he had at the age of twenty-five. He seems to have learned nothing; he even boasts that he has always had a malevolent attitude toward certain persons, ideas, principles, and things, and he swears by all the gods and by the long beard of the great gray prophet that he will continue in his cock-sure though perverse way. Of this type there are vast numbers.

Superior persons subject their feelings to the same critical judgment that they give to their intellectual processes; they try carefully to keep their balance; they see the necessity of facing social problems, both large and small, with intelligence and of trying to solve them with reason instead of with force, prejudice, and emotion. 

61. Persons having superior mentality do not fear to make diplomatic concessions to the demands of the herd that are contrary to their own personal desires. 

COMMENT. There are very few persons who live by inflexible rules and who are able to say that always or never they do thus or so. Diplomacy is the way of the wise. Most superior persons are idealists in theory and compromisers in practice. Ideals are goals toward which to travel; one seldom reaches all of them and sometimes none of them. A person has not traveled far on life's journey until he meets barriers, opposition, and conflicts that cause him to develop a give-andtake policy that he finds to be the best way out. The wise man knows when to yield a point, but he also knows that in yielding he must not lower his standards permanently nor lose sight of his ultimate goal; he frankly admits-"This is the best I can do now." He compromises. 

Not to permit diplomacy and expediency to break

THE STUDY 

147 down one's integrity is proof of a strong character. The superior person differentiates carefully between social diplomacy and moral laxity; between intellectual integrity on the part of the man who knows, and intellectual immaturity or inferiority on the part of the person who does not know; and between a highly-developed, inborn artistic feeling of any kind and the absence of this feeling. Long before Saint Paul lived and taught it was known that one must be all things to all men; long before Goldsmith's day it was known that one must sometimes stoop to conquer. All adults stoop to fondle or to be of service to a small child; all altruists stoop to help the fallen and the lowly to rise; and Benjamin 

Franklin admonished us to stoop gracefully. No really big man fears to stoop physically or socially, intellectually or spiritually; only little men have this fear. 

Superior minds are seldom snobbish, supercilious, or disdainful. 

A savant may be seen sitting on the floor playing a simple game with his grandchildren. Grandpa yields to the herd's desire.

His Honor, the mayor, leaves his business to attend the opening of a convention of barbers, butter-and-eggs men, or pedagogs, and to welcome them to his fair city. 

He strives to serve; also being a politician he likes the limelight. 

John Doe, who does not know one tune from another, consents to accompany his musical-or perhaps only ambitious-wife to a concert and endures two hours of torture because he thinks the herd, and not himself, needs and should support good music. Incidentally, and sometimes with much profanity, John dons formal attire still more concession-and sits in state until he goes out to smoke, and forgets to return, after the first half of the concert. 

Henry Roe would like to poison all the neighborhood cats that noisily carry on their amorous activities too

KNOW THYSELF 

near his bedroom window at three o'clock in the morning. He refrains from doing so because the cats are his little neighbors' pets.

The yielding of personal desire, on the part of the superior, as a concession to the demands of the herd is usually right though often a personal sacrifice. On the other hand self-preservation demands that in serious matters the superior person must utter an unequivocal no often enough that he will not be asked to break his integrity, to lower his standards, to waste his time, and to endure an unnecessary boresome experience that is detrimental to him and is without profit to the herd. 

The problem of social contact needs more intelligence than is usually given it. 

To stoop gracefully, to condescend without a patronizing attitude, is the way of the social artist. A good sense of humor often makes association with the inferior a real joy. To laugh with them is more profitable than to feel sorry for them or to want to reform them. Very few persons want to be reformed. People of all mental grades need teaching and discipline, not reforming. It is far better to teach a child to avoid the things in life that should not be than it is to permit or to encourage his inane day-dreams of living in a fool's paradise. There is no reason worth mentioning why the milk of human kindness should be reserved for one's equals and superiors. 

62. Persons having superior mentality are prone to think of defects intelligently. 

COMMENT. What one thinks of defects, that is, the subject as a whole, is of far more importance than what one thinks of spats, dill pickles, or one of Browning's poems. Superior minds often think in terms of ideals, artistry, and perfection; they think of ideals

THE STUDY 

149 in personal and social life; artistry in things of beauty and in social intercourse; and perfection in the products of nature as well as in artificial things. And while the superior person thinks of ideals, artistry, and perfection he is constantly aware of defects and imperfections. 

The superior mind on seeing a defect immediately tries to determine if the defect is remediable, partially remediable, or not remediable. An unsightly mole may be taken off the face, crooked teeth may be straightened in a child, and bad manners and bad language in persons in the upper brackets may be voluntarily improved. 

Burbank could and did improve fruits, vegetables, and flowers; Edison improved and extended the use of electricity; all work in the fine arts can be improved by persons specially gifted in such matters. But the superior person knows that defects and imperfections that can not be cured must simply be endured; there is no other way out.

As to correcting defects, it is interesting to observe that inferior and mediocre persons are more likely to want to make changes in what they have in a material sense than in what they are; that is, they will often try to correct things that can be changed with money but they are reluctant to try to change themselves. A thief, a deadbeat, or a bill-beater will exert himself to get a new motor car to replace an old one, but he will not try to change his defects in matters of honesty. Many men who can afford to do so are too stingy to have their teeth cleaned by a dentist; physical appearance yields to the love of money. One may find many wellbuilt and well-furnished American homes in which the library—at least the reading material of the householdconsists of a few wood-pulp magazines, possibly a Bible that is not read, a cook book that is not used, and a small dictionary of ancient vintage that is never consulted. On the other hand the superior person is prone to think more of what he is than of what he has in a

KNOW THYSELF 

material sense. A young artist will live poorly for years for the sake of improving himself, and many young men and women of superior mentality manage in some way to get through school on a sum of money that is pitifully small.

Inferior, stupid people often can not see defects in a thing unless the defects are pointed out to them. Then they seem not to know nor even to care whether the defects are remediable or not; they are apparently not interested in improving a defective thing. Of this type of people there are several millions in America in the lower mental brackets.

Persons who are not aware of their defects are not embarrassed by them and, of course, make no effort to change them. Millions of people have poor standards of living; they make no intelligent efforts to change their standards, that is, they voluntarily make no prolonged effort in which they use industry, economy, foresight, and rigid avoidance of unnecessary economic responsibility. Millions of inferior and mediocre persons have just as bad language and bad manners at forty as they had at twenty; their ideas relative to money are no better at sixty than they were at sixteen; they apparently have seen no remediable defects in themselves and yet thousands of stupid, inferior economic parasites who live off the taxpayers, the charities, or their relatives pour forth volumes of advice on how to run the fiscal affairs of the universe. 

It is a good lead in judging the minds both of children and of adults to note their reaction to defects. Do they recognize defects or not? Do they think of defects as remediable or not? Are they prone to minimize serious defects, especially in themselves? Are they conscious of their own defects and, if so, what do they voluntarily do about them? Are they more interested in other persons' defects than they are in their own? Do they exaggerate the importance of small defects in things

THE STUDY 

151 and people? Do they waste time and effort over irremediable defects? 

63. Adults having superior mentality assume that happiness and pleasure are normal feelings but that constant care and forethought must be used to prevent pain, discomfort, and trouble.

COMMENT. It is apparent to any intelligent student of mental qualities that there is a constant conflict going on in many persons between the instinct for selfpreservation on one hand and the sex and social instincts on the other. Children usually seek pleasure; so too do most childish adults. In both groups, except in the presence of recognized danger, the social and sex instincts are more potent than the instinct for selfpreservation; and the desire for immediate pleasure rather than the avoidance of pain in the future is the dominant thought in the daily life. Only sheer necessity compels many adults to work.

Down through the ages a war of words has been carried on relative to seeking or not seeking pleasure as the principle motive in life. The hedonists argued for pleasure and the stoics argued against it. Aristotle said the wise man will not seek pleasure but will seek freedom from pain. This is good advice for inferior and mediocre persons to follow. Wise men usually have figured the thing out this way for themselves even though they never heard of Aristotle.

It is a good lead in studying a person's mental qualities to note his attitude and practices relative to the pleasure-pain motive in life. It is well known to a careful observer what children and inferior adults will usually do; they will take the pleasure route as far as they can. If they get into trouble-pain, as Aristotle called it-they appear to have no qualms about it, since

KNOW THYSELF 

a parasitical attitude toward adults and the upper classes is, always has been, and always will be their solution of the problem of pain. 

Intelligent forethought relative to the avoidance of pain, discomfort, and grief, in the more or less remote future, comes to most superior persons before adult life is reached; mediocre minds learn more slowly, but it is characteristic of nearly all intelligent persons past middle life to put the avoidance of pain before the intentional quest of pleasure. Once the thought is implanted in the mind it seldom leaves, and contemplated pleasures of the day that may interfere with one's wellbeing in the future are either rejected entirely or they are entered into with cautious reluctance. 

And yet superior persons rarely speak of this mental quality voluntarily this rule of conduct-for its value is so obvious to the person who recognizes it that he thinks everyone should know the rule and follow it. It is the absence of this mental quality, the breach rather than the observance of the rule, that so often makes for poverty, crime, personal regret, family parasitism, charity, tax burdens to care for the poor, and many other social defects and problems.

Insurance-life, sickness, and accident-is an intelligent effort made to avoid pain, discomfort, and humiliation. Insurance is more than a business; it is a successful effort to keep millions of people out of poverty and to afford the prudent the necessities and comforts of life.

Public health work, preventive medicine we doctors call it, is a more or less successful effort to keep the masses out of physical, economic, and social trouble. 

Most superior adults take consolation in their philosophy of escape. They look upward for inspiration; they think daily of ideals and artistry as goals toward which to travel; they see perfection in nature-perfect flowers, perfect trees, perfect fruit, perfect vegetables,

THE STUDY 

153 even perfect babies at the baby shows, and perfect animals at fat stock shows, dog shows, cat shows, and poultry shows; but secretly they thank the gods that be for their escape from many things. 

64. Persons having superior mentality begin early in life the long endeavor to attain independence. 

COMMENT. Independence is freedom of choice in action; it is, on the whole, only a relative condition, a thing hoped for but rarely attained.

The feeling as well as the fact of dependency is not an uncomfortable, objectionable thing to most children and to vast numbers of adults; this appears to be their normal attitude toward their elders and superiors and toward all persons and institutions that provide for them.

Complete intellectual independence is largely a fictitious thing. Millions of people are still controlled by irrational traditions. Men are still cursed, reviled, and punished for their unorthodox beliefs. The clergy in all churches, with two or three exceptions, still fear heresy trials or some other form of punishment. Conscientious objectors to war, being in the minority, are still thought of as criminals or weaklings by the majority; and some of our patriots-for-pay and patriots-for-pensions would deny them the right of freedom of speech if they could. It must be remembered, though, that intellectual freedom does not give one intellectual ability. Most Americans have all the intellectual freedom they need and more than they use. 

For intellectual guidance, aside from experimental knowledge, children are prone to depend upon their parents, workmen depend upon their foremen, scientists depend upon their so-called authorities, and the President depends upon his brain-trust, his cabinet, and other

KNOW THYSELF 

advisers. It is sometimes interesting as a study in stupidity to hear some long-eared ass bray about his intellectual independence; persons of this type are usually either sophomoric or plainly dense. The best that can be hoped for in matters of intellectual freedom, as far as most persons are concerned, is to have intelligent free choice; and the thing chosen is often something that somebody else has taught or has used. There is comparatively little original, independent thought and action on important matters in the world about us. 

Many persons-and this includes both children and adults are thought to be independent when they are only stupid or obstinate. Independence is based on intelligence and reason and not on prejudice, wishthinking, and obstinacy. An anti-this or an anti-that is quite as likely to be only a blatant fool as he is to be an independent thinker. There is a great deal of difference between not thinking as the herd thinks and not thinking as one's intellectual equals and superiors think.

The superior person readily changes his ideas and opinions when reason and intelligence urge or consent to the change. A person who boasts that he never changes his mind is not independent; he is either obstinate or else is dead at the top. All growing minds are changing minds; there are no exceptions to the rule. 

When a man who has been normal mentally ceases to grow he is in his dotage; what change takes place in his mind then is deterioration-not growth. 

It takes more independence to cast out one false idea that has been believed for years than it does to reject a score of new ideas that come from external sources. 

Independence is a higher mental quality than courage. 

Many persons have courageously defended, even promulgated, the most irrational types of intellectual and moral error and evil. But the combination of independence, intelligence, and courage makes for power,

THE STUDY 

155 usefulness, and sometimes for martyrdom at the hands of the herd.

Social independence is almost unattainable, and except for self-protection is not even desirable. Affection is the strongest of the noble emotions; an affection that is not mutual, therefore dependent, may give rise to anything from anxiety to murder; affection, to be satisfactory, depends upon whether it is reciprocated or not. All satisfactory social relationships are dependent. 

It is, however, very desirable that one may be independent of that is, able to break or to avoid-all undesirable social contacts and relationships. That is only a case of self-preservation.

Economic independence is at least wished for by all persons from the moron grade on up. Most persons only wish about it-wish for a million dollars! It is easier, much easier, to wish than it is to act intelligently in the matter. Wishing and parasitism go together. 

Except when physical or mental incapacity is the cause, most adults who are in poverty are personally responsible for their plight.

A desire for independence is an innate characteristic of the superior mind just as dependency is innate in the inferior mind. The independent mind sets up a definite goal toward which to travel; the fact that very few persons reach the goal is not altogether discreditable for very few persons reach all, or even any, of their idealistic goals. It is better to try and to fail than not to try at all. 

65. Persons having superior mentality are prone to discriminate carefully between their needs and their desires. 

COMMENT. Thoreau considered a man to be rich in proportion to the number of things which he could

KNOW THYSELF 

afford to let alone. This was easy as well as excellent philosophy for Thoreau to teach and to practice because the social instinct was deficient in him; and it is useful philosophy for a lot of us to practice who are more socially inclined. In fact, the more socially inclined we are, the more we need to think carefully, and act accordingly, relative to the many things we can do without. 

There is such a thing as having many unnecessary possessions. One can have too many toilet articles, too many social interests, too many irons in the fire, too much wearing apparel, too many kinsfolk, too large a home, too much real estate, too much of this and too many of that, on and on without end. Many homes contain scores of useless articles, the accumulation of years; they need a fire or a tornado. More than half of all books, even those in professional men's libraries, are only useless junk; more need of bonfires. To possess many unnecessary things finally makes one, against his best interests, a curator of a junk-yard, a custodian of trouble and grief.

There is a reason-and a good one, too-why so many persons of superior mentality hold their acquisitive desires in control, and why they consider giving almost as important as getting. Anyone who has traveled much or traveled far knows that it is nearly always well to travel light. Excess baggage on the journey of life is like excess baggage on a trip to Europe.

But there are difficulties to overcome in giving away many of our useless or surplus possessions. Many persons have a sentimental attachment for their old junk, and many persons know of no intelligent, appreciative person to whom they could give it; for if one is intelligent enough to appreciate a used article as a gift, except perhaps old wearing apparel, he usually has more small possessions than he knows what to do with anyway; so the giver is between the devil and the deep blue sea. As to money, there is of course no difficulty in

THE STUDY 

157 finding persons who will take it. The poor, the inferior, the lazy, as well as the greedy, the grasping, and the dishonest, we have with us always. 

There are men of good mentality who strongly desire a surplus of worldly possessions; the mere fact of possessing things of value is a pleasure to them. Many of these men who have collected and accumulated successfully really intend to begin to give away their surplus some day; but they usually postpone the day-postpone it so long that their heirs, both near and remote, become very impatient with their habit of still being present at their birthday anniversaries.

The deciding factor in superior minds relative to having or not having, keeping or giving, of carrying excess baggage or giving it away, is whether one is more interested in being someone or in possessing something. Many superior persons, and more especially those having scientific, creative, or artistic minds, are more interested in being than they are in having. A scientist would rather be the discoverer or the inventor of a useful thing than to have a hundred times as much money as he needs; to him money is only a means to an end, not the end itself.

Both originality and artistic talent are sometimes very strong, time-consuming, and excluding feelings. Except when necessity compels it, they tolerate no serious interference with the work they have under way or with the artistry they are developing. There is an element of truth in the jokes one reads about absent-minded scientists who are so absorbed in their work that they appear eccentric to other persons; and there is also much truth in the statements one hears about the long hours of work done by inventors and other persons having creative minds. Many men of this kind are not greatly interested in whether they have two shirts or two dozen shirts; possessions are of no great importance to them. 

And aside from material possessions there is a constant

KNOW THYSELF 

conflict going on in the mind between desire and needs. 

The superior mind is prone to dismiss desires that can not be fulfilled, as well as desires for unnecessary things. 

66. Persons having superior mentality are prone to keep clearly in mind the difference between means to an end and the end itself.

COMMENT. In observing persons, either children or adults, who are unable to see the difference clearly between the means to an end and the end itself, one notes several reasons for their failure. With many who are fairly sincere and earnest the thought-goal, the action-goal, or the purpose-goal, appears to be indistinct. 

They are not clear as to where they are going; therefore, they leave the path of progress and wander. Other persons of this mental type appear to have set up a definite goal to be reached but the details involved in reaching it are either so complex or so attractive that they lose themselves in the details and fail to arrive. 

Then there are persons of a still trashier type mentally who are so absorbed in the pleasures and allurements of the day that they have no worthwhile goal; they, of course, get nowhere in life.

Persons who do not see their purpose-goal clearly are found on every hand, for there are millions of them in the inferior and mediocre mental brackets. In the language of the street, a person of this type is unable to see farther than the end of his nose. All intelligent laymen know that a short-sighted person almost never gets anywhere in the world; in fact, the expression, short-sighted, is often used as a term of reproach. 

Superior children begin to show intelligence in this matter rather early in life, and by the time they have finished high school they are seen to jump the hurdles and keep in the race instead of being baffled by the

THE STUDY 

159 hurdles and lured into unprofitable or harmful activities.

During their college years their mental make-up is so manifest that it may be well judged and almost with finality as far as this detail is concerned. 

There are different kinds of conversational bores. 

Some have a pet theme to discuss every day and all day; some are highly subjective and discuss themselves along with every other subject brought up in a conversation; and some are painfully prolix regardless of the subject, time, or place. The last-mentioned type loses himself in details; he becomes so involved in his means that he fails to reach the end. The verbose conversational bore is not language-conscious; he lacks artistry in speech; he seldom says anything well; he was born to be a bore and nothing less than being struck dumb can change him.

But it may be asked-can not the good mind set up a goal for the inferior person to travel toward and then force him by means of rigid discipline to try to reach it? And one must answer the question in the affirmative. The plan has been tried millions of times on children by their parents, and by many superior adults on other inferior and mediocre persons, both children and adults, but without a great deal of success. 

Discipline is not conspicuously present in American life today, with the result that nearly all nit-wits, imbeciles, and morons do very much as they please from early childhood to old age.

The clergy sometimes speak of godless people and probably they speak truth in this matter. One thing is sure to students of mental qualities: we have in the lower mental brackets millions of goalless people. The goalless are more numerous than the godless. 

People who arrive at their goal by unfair or dishonorable means are found to be only a small group when compared to the large number who lose themselves in their means to an end and never arrive at all. The

KNOW THYSELF 

scientist probably has the clearest ideas as well as the most honorable practices having to do with means to ends; it is the natural way of the scientific mind; for example, an engineer building a bridge or a viaduct knows precisely what he is doing and why. 

The better the mind the more definitely goal-related are its thoughts and conclusions; and the poorer the mind the more impulsive, unrelated, and goalless are its activities. There is considerable determinism in this matter, in that a person who is intelligent enough to travel toward a worthy goal usually has one; and if he is not intelligent enough to travel toward a goal voluntarily, he is not intelligent enough to set up a goal voluntarily. It is another case of he is or he is not; he has or he has not. 

67. Persons having superior mentality look upon life and its achievements in terms of well-directed, intelligent growth rather than in terms of luck, chance, or good fortune. 

COMMENT. There are but two satisfactory ways to solve problems or to approach or to reach goals. Either one must do the necessary thing or things himself or else he must be lucky. Luck is the hope of the inferior and the mediocre. Vaguely aware of their lack of inherent ability to do worth-while things by their own intelligent, self-directed effort, they often depend upon luck to reach their goal; and usually luck fails them.

Bad luck is a much-used defense of the person who is a failure. He does not like to admit his lack of intelligence but is prone to set forth at length the childish excuse that luck was against him. 

Luck may be the cause of one's getting money or publicity, of one's winning a ball game, or of one's having dozens of other things of value or of pleasure;

THE STUDY 

161 that is not a debatable point. And luck may be the cause of one's escaping many evils; that, too, is not a debatable point. But luck never made a great scholar, a great musician, a great artist, a good book, or anything else that is the product of a superior mind. Luck may give a person an opportunity to produce some worth-while thing, but without inherent ability and the will-to-do, nothing of value is accomplished. 

When the envious, the jealous, and the failures want to disparage the achievements of persons who have good minds they state the achievements were due to good luck. It would be more truthful as well as more gracious, socially, to admit that intelligent persons are intelligent. This, however, appears to be a difficult thing for small minds to do.

All kinds of adults from the moron grade on up sometimes wish for luck and hope for luck; for good luck is acceptable to all persons. But the important difference between inferior persons and superior persons, as far as luck is concerned, is that superior persons do not depend upon luck; they depend upon intelligent effort, growth, and development. The man with a good mind knows that the best luck that can happen to him -next to having been born with good mentality-is that he may escape injury that he is not responsible for.

His attitude toward luck is the same as his attitude toward the pleasure-pain element or motive in life. He wants to escape bad luck that he is not responsible for just as he wants to escape pain that he is not responsible for. He knows that to seek luck intentionally is as inane and futile as it is to seek pleasure intentionally.

As said before in this study-language proclaims the man more often than apparel does. A man who uses the word luck frequently, who is prone to give bad luck as the cause of his failure and good luck as the cause of a superior person's success, unwittingly classifies himself as inferior or mediocre.

KNOW THYSELF 

Outside of the world of fine arts, general intelligence and a conservative spirit of adventure are the principal factors that make for most of our worth-while achievements. These, like talent for the fine arts, are inherent mental qualities that are capable of growth and improvement. They can not be acquired voluntarily; one either has them or he does not have them.

Superior children manifest their ideas and ways of reaching their goals rather early in life. Their interests and their problem-solving abilities are closely related. 

A child that is interested in mechanics should be encouraged to be a mechanic and not a parson; a child that is interested in abstract thought, that is, bookish, should be a book-worm and not a Babbitt; and a child that is interested in people should select an occupation in which he deals with people. 

68. The philosophic mind is concerned primarily with causes, the ways of nature, and general principles. 

COMMENT. The philosophic mind may or may not have creative ability and it may or may not have outstanding talent for the fine arts; it is usually characterized by general rather than by special intelligence. The philosophic mind wants to know why; its curiosity is directed toward the practical rather than toward the theoretical; it seeks and collects wisdom. Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle knew many useful things for their time; in fact, Socrates at all times taught his followers to gain useful knowledge. 

The doctrinaire, usually shallow, often intolerant, and always impractical, deals with theories. He seems not to know that it requires very little intelligence to talk glibly and dogmatically; he is long on making unsupported assertions and short on thinking a matter through. Not so the philosopher. He thinks much, he

THE STUDY 

163 knows when to speak and when to be silent, and he tries consistently to stick to facts. 

Philosophic ability ranges from zero to-Plato; like the sense of justice or a feeling of integrity, it is found in a measure in nearly all persons from the moron grade on up.

The philosopher is noticeably free from harmful emotions, prejudice, and bigotry. Because of this his reasoned conclusions usually contain much truth; some of them are eternal verities, therefore he sometimes speaks for the ages. Many of the thoughts of Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle are as useful today as they were when they were uttered twenty-three to twenty-four hundred years ago.

As a companion the philosopher is prone to keep the record straight. Not easily perturbed by things that should not be, more especially when he is not responsible for them, his calm, cool judgment in time of trouble is a comfort to other intelligent persons, but he is likely to be a source of annoyance to the flighty, the partisan, and to persons who enjoy the baser emotions. It is not strange that Socrates was very trying to Zantippe, for Zantippe apparently reveled in small talk and hating. 

The philosopher looks askance at many activities of the herd. He is willing to try to help persons who are able and willing to help themselves. His attitude towards the herd was accurately expressed by John 

Stuart Mill: if he goes to the herd at all, he goes only as an apostle; he speaks, teaches, admonishes, and then returns to his tub, his hut, or to a more commodious home. Usually philosophers, unlike prophets, avoid the rabble; they do not go in for mass uplifting. Philosophers teach the teachable, one or a few at a time; they are not prone to waste their sweetness on the desert air; they learn early in life that only a few persons, comparatively speaking, have both the ability and the will to learn.

KNOW THYSELF 

To philosophers, philosophy-rather than money, social class, religious affiliation, or birth-is often the magnet and the tie that binds. For that reason philosophy, like politics, makes strange bedfellows. A philosopher may have wealth by inheritance, education by choice, and he may live, move, and have his being largely within the social class of his family, and yet spend many pleasant hours, in fact his most pleasant hours, with an old tattooed salt down at the pier, a gardener reared in Scotland, a brewmaster reared in Munich, a bookworm from Boston, a miner from the Klondike, or a lowly Uncle Remus. Philosophic minds are attracted by philosophic minds just as musical minds are attracted by other musical minds.

The philosophic person seeks peace and avoids strife; he seeks wisdom rather than useless knowledge; he accepts disciples but does not seek them; he is pleased to be understood and appreciated by the few, and he knows that he can neither be understood nor appreciated by the many; he especially wants to know the motives of human behavior; he wants to know which is the best way to travel the road of life; he feels there is a cosmic purpose, a reason for our being, and he tries to understand his and our part in the whole scheme of things. 

69. Persons having superior mentality are prone to profit by the seemingly detrimental experiences and conditions of both their body and mind. 

COMMENT. Only because of ignorance and very superficial observation is one able to say that there are many perfect bodies and perfect minds; for, as a matter of fact, there are very few, if any, perfect bodies and minds. The untaught layman is prone to think there are more bodily defects than mental defects in

THE STUDY 

165 people, and here again he is wrong. Where there is one person who fails because of physical defect there are at least nine who fail because of poor mentality.

What should be one's attitude toward his personal defects, toward the present personal things that should not be, and toward the things in his past life that should not have been? The manner in which one answers this question both in theory and practice is a fairly good index of his mental make-up.

If one stands aside and looks at his own mind, if he reviews its ways of acting and its final conclusions relative to his defects, and more especially his defective acts, he finds that the mind is first, last, and always, an accomplished liar. The mind's smoothing-over process, its suppressions and repressions, its willful forgetting, its finally figuring out that after all it did not act so thoroughly badly, is called rationalizing. All minds from the moron grade on up rationalize their defects —first, for the sake of peace, and second, for the sake of idealism.

Peace is as essential to the mind as freedom from pain is essential to the body. The good mind is pacifistic; it desires peace at any price, and the price it usually pays to excuse or to condone its own faults and failures is one more lie, told to itself and only to itself. More of our faults and errors are secretly rationalized than are admitted or confessed to others; it should be so. 

It is both logical and desirable that a good mind should try to idealize its past just as it tries to idealize its future. To do this the mind selects the best parts of its past, embellishes them, believes the embellishments as well as the fact that has been embellished, and finally develops a satisfactory, or even a very pleasing, retrospective view. And all of this is usually harmless provided boasting and injury to other persons are carefully avoided-and they usually are carefully avoided by superior minds. One sees a few self-made men who

KNOW THYSELF 

worship their maker and who boast about their having overcome many faults, defects, and obstacles-but only a few. Most self-made men know where they are and how they got there, and most of them know that they started out in life with inherently good minds; that is the secret of their success. A good mind, like a good body, is something to be thankful for, but it is a gift from the gods, not a self-created thing. 

Nearly all persons who have risen above mediocrity, who have traveled the road of life far and with fair satisfaction, have burned a considerable number of bridges behind them; and they have also carefully concealed their deficiencies and defects. Life as a whole is not unlike a physician's professional life. We doctors sometimes make mistakes and, if we are intelligent, we profit by our mistakes; but we do not go out on the street and announce our errors and stupidities to the public.

Secrecy, itself, is an evidence of superior mentality. 

Fools, children, and parrots usually tell everything they know, and a great deal they do not know; but mentally superior persons are almost invariably secretive, especially about their own personal affairs. 

A careful and extensive reading of biography shows that among the great men and women of the world, and more especially among those having creative minds, many had poor bodies due either to disease or to congenital defects. Tuberculosis alone has been a handicap to thousands of them. But in spite of physical defects, or perhaps because of them in a few cases, they carried on and did work that brought them the comforts of life, fame, honor, glory, and satisfaction.

With inferior minds and many of the mediocre the story is very different. They use their defects and deficiencies, their mistakes and their hardships as excuses for failure and as reasons for not doing what they are told to do and are rightfully expected to do.

THE STUDY 

167 Personal mistakes often teach them little or nothing, for they repeat them year after year. 

Certain persons are born to be strong characters; there seems to be no other satisfactory explanation for this fact. Usually between the ages of ten and sixteen a superior child will begin to relate his experiences, his opportunities, and even his heredity, to his future life. 

After a lad of this type begins to recognize favorable and unfavorable factors in reaching his goal he almost invariably minimizes and rationalizes his bad personal experiences and his hardships, not of his own making, and devotes his time and energy to developing the best that is within him. 

70. Persons having superior mentality are rewarded in many ways that are appreciated only by themselves and by other persons of similar mental quality. 

COMMENT. Rewards are usually thought of in terms of money or its equivalent, the approbation, praise, or applause of one's fellow man, and social rank or position; there are very few persons to whom these things are not acceptable whether they have earned them by their own efforts or whether they have them by the accident of birth or as gifts. In the upper mental brackets and in persons having certain talents, however, there are additional rewards that may be derived from personal skill, artistry, creative accomplishment, the acquisitive instinct, and the appreciation of beauty, truth, and goodness.

Life's basic enjoyments are physical-the satisfaction of physiologic demands, and the physical feeling of well-being; but life's rewards are largely mental. 

A person in the lower mental brackets who has a sound body, good organic functions, and the necessary animal comforts, probably enjoys living as much as a dyspeptic

KNOW THYSELF 

savant or a creative genius does; and yet he has but few rewards.

Persons in the upper mental brackets understand and appreciate the enjoyments of persons in the lower mental brackets, for all persons are animals first and always whether they are good healthy animals according to the admonition of Herbert Spencer or not. But persons in the lower mental brackets neither understand nor appreciate many of the rewards of persons in the upper mental brackets.

In games of skill where there is neither money nor prize to be won, the joy that results from skill, alone, is the reward. A musician who plays well is rewarded by his own artistry even though no other person hears him play. A painter gets more reward from painting a picture than any other person who sees it or who may own it. A person who is fond of flowers gets more reward from raising them and seeing them bloom than does any other person who sees them. Authors of all kinds are proud of their brain-children even though other persons may think their intellectual offspring very inferior and useless. A scientist who makes a new discovery is rewarded for his work even though the discovery brings him no money and but little fame. 

Acquisitiveness is an instinct that is primarily concerned with self-preservation, hence, the almost universal appreciation of money or its equivalent as a reward for personal effort. But acquisitiveness sometimes manifests itself in ways that are not directly concerned with the preservation of self; for example, col-. lectors' hobbies. A person may spend years of time, together with money and effort, in collecting stamps, vases, cacti, pipes, pictures, Indian relics, first editions, autographs, colonial furniture, beer mugs, old china, and scores of other things. Persons who are not interested in a collector's hobby are likely to look upon him as one more bore to be avoided; they may look and

THE STUDY 

169 listen a few minutes when shown a collection of old jack-knives, butterflies, or walking-sticks, in order not to appear stupid or socially rude, but they do not become enthusiastic. To the uninterested there is no reward nor can they see any reward for the collector; they look upon the collector as being either eccentric or plainly foolish. 

And then there is intellectual acquisitiveness, a sterile, unprofitable thing in the opinion of persons having inferior or mediocre mentality. They can not see why a lawyer should pass many hours, year after year, reading history; why a woman should belong to a book club; why a physician should learn to read a new language; why a clergyman should be interested in archeology; or why an insurance agent should have astronomy as a hobby.

Rewards vary as minds vary. Inferior persons usually need money and they think of rewards very largely in terms of money; that is the reason they ask such stupid questions about superior persons' hobbies and pastimes. Superior persons, being able to solve their economic problems better than the inferior, are prone to seek rewards that are either intellectually or esthetically pleasurable even though they are unprofitable in terms of money. 

71. Persons having superior mentality are reluctant to indulge in transient pleasures and other activities that may jeopardize their future well-being.

COMMENT. This is a phase of the instinct for selfpreservation. Inferior and mediocre persons are not prone to look ahead; they are prone to live for the day and to take no thought for the morrow. The author of 

Proverbs said, "Where there is no vision the people

KNOW THYSELF 

perish." Where an individual person is without vision he is likely to become a parasite. 

There seems to be no type of mental deficiency, ignorance, and stupidity that is so widespread, so irremediable, and so harmful, both to the individual person and to society in general, as the inability to control one's actions in the present for the sake of one's future welfare. Very few persons either can or even desire to think intelligently or otherwise about immortality; the subject is unthinkable except to a few quoters, and quoters are usually not thinkers. Now it appears that inferior minds are just as reluctant, just as unable, to think voluntarily of what they will do or of what they will be ten or twenty years ahead, as it is for superior minds to grapple with immortality. And if one tries to make an inferior person think seriously of his future years, while here in the flesh, one finds that he is as unconcerned about it as an agnostic is toward a fundamentalist hot-gospeler's warning relative to hell-fire and damnation.

Persons having good minds are prone to think intelligently of their future years here on earth but they are not greatly concerned with what happens after death; whereas persons with inferior minds are prone to be unconcerned about their earthly future, though some of them speak with positiveness and dogmatic finality about heaven, hell, and purgatory.

There has been a great deal of moralizing on this subject in all ages and in all places, but as far as we can see we are no better off now than we were three thousand years ago. People in the lower mental brackets have not improved greatly regardless of our educational advantages and our modern conveniences; for after all our moralizing, hoping, wishing, scolding, teaching, training-yes, and even praying-the inferior remain inferior. After dealing with the inferior for years one becomes somewhat of a fatalist on the subject and

THE STUDY 

171 is likely to look upon much of our efforts at uplifting as utterly futile, and that our pious or sentimental wishthinking is taken advantage of by the lower ten to twenty-five per cent of our people and a horde of salaried job-holders. 

Both external discipline and personal experience have very little influence in causing inferior persons to look ahead. At the time this is written it is estimated that we have about fifteen millions of unemployed wageearners in America, and it is doubtful if more than one in ten of them who are over twenty-five years of age has profited by his bitter experience; the other ninetenths are on the defensive, giving excuses, and blaming somebody else for their sad plight. They want the taxpayers and the charities to provide for them; they may say they are willing to work if they can get employment of a suitable kind, but they are not willing to face the facts of their personal lives from their sixteenth year on up.

Using family experience again as a means of judging this mental characteristic-for children in a family have had the same environment-one may find that in a mediocre family of five children, two will manifest an alert sense of self-restraint and forethought at the age of twenty, two may have the feeling fairly well developed by hard knocks at the age of forty, and one I will remain dull and indifferent toward the matter as long as he lives. And the two having the best minds will probably have to help the one having the poorest mind -and perhaps some of his inferior offspring-throughout their adult lives. Intelligent forethought is as much a special gift as talent for music is.

Inferior persons can, by rigid discipline, be made to be clean physically, to have good manners when speaking to their superiors, and to wear their clothing as it should be worn, as long as the discipline is continued; some of the soldiers in the regular army are a good

KNOW THYSELF 

illustration of this. But little can be done to make inferior persons have forethought and to control their present actions for the sake of their more remote future good. 

72. Persons having superior mentality are prone not to yield quickly, to rebel foolishly, or to become unduly excited when confronted by personal and social problems that require solution. 

COMMENT. Expressions of inability to solve problems, as well as dislike for the problems, range from a studied silence and an air of indifference to violent anger and hysteria. One may observe that children. over ten years of age, and sometimes even younger, who have good minds, solve their problems with the same quiet determination that is seen in superior adults. 

They are not inclined to say that they can not solve them nor do they have a fit of peevishness and anger when they are asked or required to try to solve them. 

Inferior minds solve their routine problems of the day-their daily work-because of habit and necessity. 

They know that it is largely a case of work or else take the consequences, not always pleasant, of not working. 

But any extra duty or a new problem is likely to arouse either their resentment or a frank claim of inability to do the thing asked or required of them.

Inferior adults are often easily distracted by unexpected problems of considerable importance. Their reaction may be tears, whining, hysteria, or a violent outburst of anger. Their behavior is very different from that of the calm, emotionally-controlled superior person. But a childish mind is a childish mind whether it belongs to a child of ten years or to an adult of forty; and yet many persons never learn this simple fact in judging the mental makeup of an adult.

THE STUDY 

173 Both inferior and many mediocre persons lose their goals easily. Superior adults too may lose their goals occasionally for a short time, that is, until they collect their wits and begin again. It is one of the pathetic things in our national and world-wide economic depression that so many persons, and especially those in the middle mental brackets, have lost their goals. Many of them are no better off than the plainly inferior were before the crash; they are confused, muddled, discouraged; many are as blameless for their unfortunate economic condition as though they had been victims of a storm or of a flood; and many will rehabilitate themselves slowly, not because of inherent inability to solve their problems, but because of their being unable to find gainful employment. 

One may note the quickness with which the inferior person often states that he can not or will not even try to solve a problem. Not infrequently he will state a conclusion of this kind after only a few seconds' thought that a superior person would not state until he had considered the subject carefully for a day or a month. 

In fact, inferior persons are noted for saying that a thing can not be done, nor do they appear to be embarrassed when they see someone with a better mind actually do the selfsame thing. Both in children and in adults it appears that this haste in refusing to try to solve a problem is largely the result of a desire to dismiss a disagreeable subject from the mind; back of this is laziness, a lack of close mental integration, and a lack of will to work.

It is a good lead in judging a person's mind to note his manner of considering the solution of a problem that is new to him. The inferior mind usually acts quickly, is prone to reject the problem without considering it carefully, to give excuses when urged to try to solve it, and, if possible, to rule out the problem from his sphere of responsibility. On the other hand the

KNOW THYSELF 

superior mind thinks first of the problem's value, of the feasibility of its solution, and then, if the thing to be done appears to be both useful and feasible, he proceeds to try to solve it.

Superior persons go through life more satisfactorily than the inferior for two reasons. First, because they know how to solve their problems much better than the inferior; and, second, because they know enough early in life to avoid unnecessary responsibility. This last thought can not be repeated too often. 

73. Persons having superior mentality are prone to be courageous but not rash, to be made prudent by fear, but not to be made impotent by cowardice. 

COMMENT. Courage as commonly manifested in the adult is based on intelligence and careful judgment; it is supported either by well-considered probabilities or by facts; it is not the result of impulsive wish-thinking. 

Voluntary courage is distinctively a superior mental quality.

Rashness is the result of impulse and uncontrolled emotion and is usually unrestrained by intelligence, experience, and forethought. Children act rashly because of a lack of experience as well as because of their being emotionally controlled. Childish, inferior adults act rashly because of a lack of inherent intelligence and because experience-both their own and that of other persons has not taught them self-restraint.

Fear is a normal feeling. Normal fear is based on ignorance or an unfavorable experience. It is simply a warning to go slow. Popular expressions to the effect that peace officers, judges of criminal courts, soldiers in battle, and other persons who deal with both known and unknown enemies and dangers are fearless, are in-

THE STUDY 

175 accurate and without foundation. These persons merely accept danger as a part of their official or personal duty. 

Abnormal, irrational fears, what we doctors call phobias, are often of unknown or at least of unremembered origin. For example, the fear caused by looking straight downward from a very high point; or, to take another illustration, the fear of snakes. Very few adults could lie down in a bed and go to sleep if they knew there was a harmless, non-venomous, but uncaged snake in the room. One of them-snake or human beingwould have to vacate. 

Neurotics are easily controlled by anxieties and fears.

Nearly all neurotics have conflicts in which they are unduly disturbed by some real cause or by some imaginary bugaboo. Their conflicts about sex are no more far-fetched than are their conflicts about other things having to do with the three major instincts. They are only more secretive about their sex conflicts and they wilfully hide them, whereas they should voluntarily confess them. A fear confessed to an intelligent, tactful, professional confessor usually ceases to be a fear. 

We doctors hear the neurotics' confessions, absolve them from their sins, and then, like good sinners that they are, they usually look about and find some new bugaboos to be in conflict with. Most neurotics really enjoy their neuroticism; it is a way of escape for them. 

Human beings may have by inherent endowment the courage of a bulldog or the cowardice of a rabbit as far as physical pain and danger concern them. It is the duty of very few persons, however, to have fist fights or to rescue human beings from immediate and extreme danger. Fortunately there are and can be very few heroes of the life-saving type. 

A person's courage is not judged best by his reaction to physical pain and physical danger, however spectacular and heroic his behavior may be, but by the way he meets his ethical, intellectual, and spiritual problems

KNOW THYSELF 

and temptations. And courage to maintain at all times. one's standards relative to Truth, Goodness, and Beauty, that is, one's intellectual, moral, and artistic integrity, is comparatively rare. Many persons are thought to be courageous intellectually who are only ignorant, stupid, or slaves to tradition. These steadfast, loyal bigots are often engaged in political or religious activities. The world of science is comparatively free from them. 

Scientific organizations do not have charges of heresy and heresy trials, for they do not recite creeds either ancient or modern.

It is a good lead in judging a person's mind to determine if he voluntarily and unequivocally says no at the right time and place. It takes more courage to live right and to maintain one's integrity than it does to daydream about doing some heroic thing-and daydreaming is as far as most of us get in heroic matters. 

Yes-men, timid souls, and weak sisters of both sexes are with us always; they lack courage. Most of these weaklings can be recognized in childhood; and regardless of beautiful theories of environmentalists and behaviorists as to what can and should be done to improve their stamina and to stiffen up their backbones, they remain grievous personal, family, and social nuisances. 

74. Persons having superior mentality are prone to recognize and to minimize self-interest in their judgments and criticisms. 

COMMENT. Since there are comparatively few persons whose judgment is not at some time influenced by self-interest in the thing judged, it is necessary to give one's attention to three things in judging somebody else's judgment. The first and most important thing to determine is does the person whose opinion is being judged have a right to an opinion? Then follow the

THE STUDY 

177 questions-is he free of self-interest? and is his judgment warped by self-interest? These three questions should be answered intelligently, first, before anyone's opinion is either accepted or rejected. 

In the small talk of daily life it may be observed that inferior as well as many mediocre persons are prone to think a thing is good or bad according to whether they consider it useful or not useful to themselves. Selfinterest controls probably three-fourths of their judgments not only in minor but also in major matters. 

An ill-behaved child of three years may in anger sweep his plate and bowl of porridge from the table because he wants a cookie to eat instead of porridge.

In this way millions of childish, inferior adults every day condemn, sweep aside, and even try to destroy, as far as words will enable them to do so, scores of thoughts, things, customs, traditions, beliefs, practices, principles, and institutions, merely because they think these things are not useful to them or else they are not to their liking. They are the vast horde of people who do the maximum amount of talking with the minimum amount of thinking.

It may be repeated that the language more often proclaims the man than the apparel does. The oft-used expression, "I don't like, etc." is a good lead in discovering inferior and mediocre minds. There seems to be a never-ending list of things that these people do not like and the freedom and frequency with which they express adverse criticism is an interesting though not always a pleasant study to persons who are well informed on mental qualities. 

Ill-founded, foolish expressions of dissent, condemnation, and hostile criticism are usually a defense mechanism, the result of envy or ignorance, and sometimes of both. Persons who habitually use such expressions vaguely know they are inferior and they try to

KNOW THYSELF 

hide their deficiencies by condemning what they think is not useful to them or what they do not understand. 

A man with a good mind will voluntarily state in a discussion or in a conversation that his judgment and preferences may be influenced by self-interest; such statements are not rare among superior men. Also among superior persons it may be observed that they do not condemn things merely because they have no personal use for them, but readily grant that the things in question may be of use to other persons. 

Small minds deal in small matters. It is not strange then that one hears children and childish adults saying over and over, dozens of times daily, that they do not like this and they do not like that. And while most persons in the upper mental brackets are not likely to pay a great deal of attention to the adverse criticism and the small talk of the inferior, it is well in more serious matters to know that the element of self-interest must be considered at all times and with nearly all persons. There are very few persons who will not find it useful to watch themselves in the matter of opinions controlled by self-interest; the ideal of using intelligent fairness, like all ideals, is difficult of attainment, but the superior mind is prone to try to attain it. 

Improvement in judgment is one of the secret pleasures of the superior mind. When intelligent persons of mature years speak of themselves as liberals it is usually due to improvement in the fairness of their judgment. 

75. Persons having superior mentality are usually well endowed with resolution. 

COMMENT. The lack of resolution, that is, inability to make a decision and to abide by it, is a grievous and frequent defect in the mental equipment of the inferior and in many of the mediocre. Vast numbers of per-

THE STUDY 

179 sons of all ages and of both sexes are as irresolute both in simple and serious matters as Hamlet was in considering suicide as a way out. To be or not to be, to do or not to do, are undecided questions in their minds day after day and even year after year. 

And why? One may well answer in the language of the farmer in speaking of the unreasonable behavior of his mule-it's the nature of the brute. Besides lacking general intelligence such persons are usually lazy, improvident, parasitical, they lack vision, and their lifeplan, if they have one, is not in accord with their inherent abilities.

Resolution has more to do with the present than with the future. If one acts intelligently in the present the future will take care of itself. This was apparent to 

Carlyle when he wrote-"Do the duty which lieth nearest to thee! Thy second duty will already have become clearer."

The good mind is nearly always a decisive mind. 

Lack of experience and forethought may sometimes cause it to decide in the wrong way but nevertheless it decides. Men and women who have good minds do not sit around looking into space waiting, like Micawber, for something to turn up; nor do they discuss their plans with everyone who will listen to them, although they sometimes discuss their problems with someone capable of advising them. 

Inferior persons of the ambitious type sometimes speak of doing big things yet they refuse to do little things. Beginning at the bottom of the ladder does not appeal to them even though they are told that many of the managers, presidents, and directors of large business organizations were once office-boys in the organizations that they manage.

The resolute man is usually not in a hurry to arrive at his social or economic goal, but he is disappointed if he is not making progress toward it. He knows that

KNOW THYSELF 

men walked for millions of years before motor cars were invented. He knows there is no relationship between speed in present-day travel and speed in his mental growth and in his accumulation of wisdom. He knows it takes as long to develop mentally now as it did in the days of Socrates and that much of our knowledge is not wisdom. But the irresolute, wish-thinking types do not see this. They appear to think that because they can travel with speed that economic security and mental maturity should be acquired speedily. 

One of the irremediable defects in altruistic work at all times, and more especially in our modern, overzealous uplifting, is that much time, effort, and money are spent on inferior persons to help them get somewhere in the world of usefulness when they do not know nor even care where they want to go. If a child over fifteen years of age or an adult is so stupid or so irresolute that he does not know what he wants to be or to do, why help him?

The irresolute man usually needs a boss and fortunately he rarely resents having one or more.
