If he is lucky in having a wife or a parent intelligent enough to think for him at home and a patient foreman to think for him where he is employed, he may get through life as a good, harmless ne'er-do-well that one thinks of very much as one does of old Dobbin.

But the irresolute man does not always have such desirable associates. He may marry a woman of his own mental makeup, whereupon it will be apparent that two fools do not make a wise pair; and he may have an employer or a foreman who is intolerant of habitual indecision, in which case he will very likely lose his employment. But that is not all the story nor even the worst of it. These inherently trashy, irresolute people usually leave to posterity a swarm of inferior progeny for the taxpayers and charities to support.

THE STUDY 

181 76. Persons having superior mentality are prone to think for themselves and to express their thoughts in their own language rather than to quote the thoughts of others.

COMMENT. All persons quote at least a little for it is almost impossible to speak or to write without, consciously or unconsciously, using the thought or even the definite statements of other persons. And yet careful observation of a person's quoting is a good lead in determining his mental quality.

In general it may be said that borrowing is due either to poverty or to lack of resourcefulness, and whether the thing borrowed is a thought, a statement, or a material thing such as sugar, coffee, or money, matters not. More persons borrow thoughts than borrow material things. Thought-borrowers are quoters in conversation. Writers who borrow thoughts and neglect giving due credit for them are spoken of by those whom they have copied as plagiarists, thieves, or pirates. Most habitual quoters have poverty of thought. With them it is easier to borrow than it is to produce original, wellexpressed ideas. It is interesting to note that inferior persons usually quote what they have heard, whereas superior persons are prone to quote what they have read.

When superior persons quote the thoughts of other superior persons they do so usually because they like the artistry, the accuracy, or the aptness of the thing quoted. One may occasionally hear a person who has a good mind use quotations in order to avoid appearing dogmatic or to avoid being unpleasantly antagonistic.

In this case the speaker is diplomatically resisting or preventing a possible attack. Not infrequently public speakers must resort to this practice.

Lawyers and the clergy have quoting occupations;

KNOW THYSELF 

they cite authority of the past rather than think in terms of current needs. The judicial system and the church show the effects of this type of thinking-or not thinking; both organizations are in a state of doldrums. 

When the thinkers in these organizations-they have a few thinkers-suggest a housecleaning the reactionary, backward-looking, tradition-controlled quoters have a fit of intellectual jim-jams. There the matter rests year after year. Quoting may be detrimental even to good minds.

Great thinkers are prone to record their thoughts. 

If they do not do this themselves, their followers are likely to record them with more or less accuracy; it was so with Socrates and Jesus. Much mediocre thought has been recorded, more especially during the past seventy-five years, and we are almost submerged daily in a flood of poor, unrecorded thought. 

Using borrowed thought gives a person who can think for himself about the same feeling that wearing a borrowed suit of clothes does to a person well supplied with clothing. The good mind is pleased with its own originality, resourcefulness, and manner of expression; it dislikes borrowing, stealing, or the frequent use of other persons' thoughts even when credit is given. Mental masquerading is seldom seen in superior persons, although it may sometimes be seen in halfeducated, socially-pretentious, mediocre persons who have not yet reached middle life.

If one overhears the conversation of two or more inferior persons, for example, one's house servants, one will note that their conversation sometimes consists largely of statements beginning with "I heard," "She said," "He said," "They say, etc." They appear to have very few original thoughts. Poverty of thought is usually accompanied by poverty of language, hence the frequent use of slang, provincialisms, and colloquialisms.

THE STUDY 

183 Among inferior and mediocre persons it is very seldom that the great American right of freedom of speech is suppressed for more than a few minutes at a time. 

With millions of our people it may be said: the less they think, the more they talk; and the more they talk, the more they quote the inane chatter and gossip of the day. 

77. Persons having superior mentality are usually endowed with much acumen and insight. 

COMMENT. This mental quality, the mind's eye, is as important a factor in the mental makeup as the physical eye is to the body. Used for good purposes it is likely to enable the person who has it to rise considerably above the average both in his occupation and in his social worth. Used for unworthy purposes, especially in relation to understanding the ignorance and defects of other minds, it makes for every kind of crime, misdemeanor, and deviltry that human beings can commit. 

Many criminals, especially those who commit crimes against the person, are of low-grade mentality; this fact appears to be well established now and is a firstrate reason for the sterilization of criminals of this class. 

But there are other evil-doers and criminals, and the range extends from gossips to dishonest bankers, who most surely are not inferior, at least in intelligence, and are very shrewd in dealing with the fears, desires, and weaknesses of individuals and the public; they have an abundance of insight.

Persons who are richly endowed with insight not only are prone to see the relationship between things that are, disconnected though they may be, but they also have a crafty, clever way in seeing what is likely to take place; that is, they can predict human behavior with considerable accuracy.

KNOW THYSELF 

It is the absence of acumen and insight, a condition commonly called stupidity or dulness, that is one of the causes of many of society's most numerous as well as gravest troubles. Many employees do not see clearly the relationship between their particular job and the welfare of the firm or business organization that employs them. Many persons have no idea of helping society voluntarily and intelligently, that is, of doing their part for the common good. To them society is only something to live off, some vague Lady Bountiful to take care of them when they can not or will not take care of themselves. Nor do they show any particular courtesy or gallantry to Lady Bountiful; in fact, many of them are discourteous, ungrateful, and even criminal in their attitude toward her. Political mountebanks and shysters, boldly encouraging parasitism, appeal to the stupid, the gullible, and the improvident and in this way make more work for Lady Bountiful. 

It is well to note carefully, however, whether a person lacks acumen and insight in a matter or whether he is only not interested in it. Life does not hold the same interests to all persons nor even to the same person at different ages. To some persons life is love; to others, life is action and adventure; but to the intellectual type of mind, to live is to learn. To learn, one needs acumen and insight as well as interest. A person has three attitudes toward the things about him. Toward most of the things he is indifferent, and rightly so; toward a few things he is interested; and toward a still smaller number he is either openly or covertly more or less hostile. Back of all this, however, the good mind, because of innate intelligence, improved perhaps by teaching and experience, knows what to be interested in and what to let alone.

But interest without acumen and insight is of short duration. Little can be done to force a person to take an interest in a thing if he lacks acumen and insight in

THE STUDY 

185 the matter. Interest is the result of innate intelligence and talent, and there can be no talent, no special aptitude for a thing, without acumen and insight in that particular thing. As illustrations: one can not make a good musician out of a child who does not have an abundance of talent for music, nor can a person who does not have a scientific mind become a great scientist. 

The former will be only a household-and perhaps a neighborhood-pest for a little while, and the latter will be a failure-one more misfit in scientific work-if he is graduated from a scientific school. 

Children are sometimes keen, accurate observers, and it is a pity that adults do not use as much intelligence in some things as children do. When a group of children who play together day after day say that one among them and they specify which one-"catches on easily" they have said in three words what a verbose psychologist might take a whole chapter in a book to say, and then not say it as well as the children do. The child that catches on easily has acumen and insight; he is likely to go far as an adult. 

78. Persons having superior mentality usually have both inherent desire and ability to shape their own destiny.

COMMENT. For many centuries it was thought that the destiny of a man was controlled by some mysterious, external force-a divine decree, a star, or perhaps a spirit, either good or bad, that led him daily through fields of joy and light or else through swamps and bogs of darkness, difficulty, and despair. But in more recent years it has been learned that a person's destiny is determined largely by the quality of the chromosomesgood, bad, or indifferent, or a mixture of all—that nature used in his embryologic beginning.

KNOW THYSELF 

To be born of good stock is far better than to have been born in Virginia, Boston, or Kentucky; and to have been born of poor stock is worse than to have been born in Arkansas, Nevada, or the Bowery. Either boasting about or regretting the place of one's birth is both inane and asinine-some fairly common practices to the contrary notwithstanding. On the other hand to have sprung from good stock should be cause for gladness if one maintains the quality of the family tree. 

But for an inferior person who has never been able to get to first base by his own efforts to boast of his family tree is carrying social stupidity beyond the limits of forbearance. 

Years ago, probably due to Lincoln's childhood experience, it was thought to be to one's credit to have been born in a log cabin or in a two-room shack with a lean-to. Political mountebanks were especially prone to boast of their humble origin. At this time, however, that field is no longer fertile and the politicians find it more profitable to buy votes with the taxpayers' money. 

So far, uplifters, environmentalists, and other disbelievers in the influence of heredity have not been able to point with pride to any great or near-great person who was born of trashy stock in a charity hospital. 

This is a field that political mountebanks might profitably till.

But since no one is responsible either for the place of his birth or for his heredity, and since nearly everyone is responsible for making the best use possible of the mental qualities that nature gave him, it is creditable and commendable in persons born in socially and economically humble surroundings to rise above such surroundings. Yes, commendable, but not a thing to boast about. Every man who talks much of the disagreeable or unfavorable things of his past life has an ax to grind. He is seeking sympathy, money, praise, or some other thing he can use.

THE STUDY 

187 The superior person, possibly as a result of intuition, usually feels before reaching adult life that he is to have a favorable destiny. He feels that he is to be, whereas, the inferior person only wishes that he might be. There are two ways open to the superior person to shape, to achieve, his destiny. He may decide on a definite lifeplan extending far into the future and then carry it out. 

Of this type one sees a few in the military service, in law, medicine, and in the ministry-men who have followed in the footsteps of their fathers. But more likely he will simply do each day's duty well and sail his little barque under sealed orders given him by a beneficent fate. As proof of the latter it is a matter of common observation that a large percentage of persons who have risen above mediocrity in the world's work did not know at twenty what they would do or even could do to win satisfaction, glory, or fame at forty or sixty.

Some lads in their 'teens follow the occupation selected for them by their parents; but unless the parents are well informed relative to their children's mental qualities their judgment is no better than that of their children, and in some cases not even as good. It was observed accurately long ago what happens when the blind lead the blind. A superior lad of fifteen to twenty at least knows what worth-while thing he is interested in; he may or may not tell his parents what it is, for many superior persons are very secretive. On the other hand parents are more likely to think of their child's occupation in terms of economic advantage and social prestige, not knowing that interest, acumen, insight, and ability are necessary before any person can do any worth-while thing well. Very few parents know the difference between a wish and a fact in judging their own children. 

But what of the mis-directed youth? Usually if he is of the right quality he will rebel against the advice and plans of his parents and work out his own destiny

KNOW THYSELF 

in accordance with his interests and his ability. It was so with Carlyle, Huxley, James Mill, Spencer, Emerson --and millions of others not so well known. 

79. Persons having superior mentality usually feel a keen intellectual satisfaction in knowing they are right. 

COMMENT. There is a feeling of mental well-being just as there is a feeling of physical well-being. At times this feeling may reach a state of exhilaration, for example when a college student passes his examinations well. Knowing that one is right intellectually is a secret pleasure not unlike that of being free from physical pain; for being wrong in a matter, unless it is a very trivial thing, gives the good mind a feeling of discomfort or pain. This is especially noticeable when one is proved to be wrong in a conflict.

Very few persons question their knowledge-or what passes for knowledge with them-except when they are in the presence of their intellectual equals or superiors.

Then there is likely to be intellectual conflict, and conflict leads to accuracy and learning. Much of our so-called knowledge is only a matter of memory, but the best of our knowledge-our wisdom-is usually gained by experience and by definite conflict. To be right in conflict gives the good mind assurance, strength, and encouragement; but to be proved wrong in conflict frequently would be dispiriting, depressing, and discouraging.

The superior person is prone to keep within his range of knowledge. If he does not know, or if he is uncertain about a point, he is frank to say so; therefore, he is not often humiliated by being proved utterly wrong. 

But this is not the case with inferior and many mediocre persons. They are more prone to bluff, to lie, or to try to deceive in some way with the result that they are

THE STUDY 

189 often proved to be wrong-but this appears not to humiliate most of them noticeably. 

A person with a good mind appreciates and welcomes information that makes for intellectual rightness, whereas the inferior person is prone to manifest indifference to intellectual rightness. If one observes carefully the conversation of superior persons, both children and adults, one will see that many of their questions as well as many of their voluntary statements have to do with getting right or being right intellectually. 

But this is not true of the inferior and many of the mediocre, for they are more prone to discuss moral rightness, especially somebody else's morals, and to remain indifferent toward intellectual accuracy. 99 Mental growth and mental stability, as well as the feeling of mental well-being, depend very largely on the rightness of our knowledge. For that reason the superior person frequently has to "look things up.' It is a common experience with him. He must look up his pronunciations, his grammar, correct or preferred usage of words, historical and scientific data, and scores and scores of other things about which his knowledge is insufficient, inaccurate, or hazy. Inferior and mediocre persons rarely, and sometimes never, do these things voluntarily. They are not interested in being right merely for the sake of being right; and many of them even dislike to change their ideas from wrong to right after being shown they are wrong. Probably fifty per cent of American adults do not consult a dictionary once a year; and if someone were to say before a large mixed audience that George Washington was born in New York City in 1832 the statement would probably go unchallenged by at least a fourth of the persons present. Even if they had a hazy doubt as to the accuracy of the statement they would not take the trouble to clear up the doubt.

Voluntary interest in intellectual rightness is largely

KNOW THYSELF 

an inherent mental quality-a matter of heredity. And like other superior mental qualities one either has it or he does not have it. By means of rigid discipline both inferior and mediocre children and adults can be made to give some thought to intellectual accuracy; but as soon as the discipline is relaxed or removed they lose interest in the matter.

In general it may be said that inferior persons are not interested in intellectual accuracy nor do they feel noticeably embarrassed to be proved wrong; that mediocre persons are more likely to try to bluff and to deceive than are the inferior, and that they often resent being shown that their statements and beliefs are inaccurate; and that superior persons not only voluntarily seek to know what is right, but they also accept information gladly. 

80. Persons having superior mentality are prone to base their social attitudes on reason rather than on sentiment, prejudice, or traditional beliefs. 

COMMENT. Probably every person has a conscious attitude toward every other person with whom he comes in close contact. A superior person may have any one of four reasoned attitudes toward other persons: a feeling of indifference, of equality, of superiority, or of inferiority. Toward a large proportion of the persons he meets daily the superior person is indifferent; this is especially true of persons living in large cities. But when the contact is more intimate and prolonged the attitude is more likely to change and to become more distinct. Then it is that a feeling of equality, superiority, or inferiority comes into the mind. These attitudes are not necessarily controlled by one's affections, for one man may consider another man superior mentally and yet dislike him personally; or a servant may

THE STUDY 

191 be considered rather inferior mentally, yet be thoroughly liked by his employer.

The more one knows of mental qualities the sooner he will reach a definite, intelligent attitude toward another person. A superior person understands an inferior, either child or adult, much more readily than he understands his equal or his superior, and his attitude is formed accordingly. But when a superior person meets another superior person both become cautious, somewhat like ambassadors and foreign ministers, as to what they say and do. Each is likely to assume an attitude of watchful waiting for a while. 

The superior person knows why he has a certain. attitude, either favorable or unfavorable, toward another person. But this is not the case with many of the inferior and the mediocre; they are prone to echo the opinion of the herd. There are probably two or three millions of adults in the South who will say that Jefferson was a great man, and yet they do not know a single thing that he did other than that he was Presi- Ident of the United States at one time-and that does not necessarily qualify one as great. And millions of 

Americans sing the praises of Franklin who know nothing of what he did or said. 

Coming down to persons of the present time one finds that the inferior and many of the mediocre only recite the opinions of the herd about men and women in the upper classes as well as about those persons, both high and low, whose doings and goings-on make the news and gossip of the day. If they have a personal opinion it is based largely on prejudice or sentimentrarely on facts.

Many inferior persons seemingly enjoy their personal dislikes and they air them whenever they have an opportunity to do so. The number of persons they dislike, hate, and try to defame is astonishing. One might well conclude that some of them pass through

KNOW THYSELF 

life engaged at all times in hating someone. Fortunately their opinions are not taken seriously by the more intelligent classes.

It is not unusual to hear a superior person say of another person that he likes him but does not admire him; or, perhaps, that he admires and respects someone, but does not like him personally. In the latter case reason-not sentiment-controls his opinion. Inferior and mediocre persons seldom make such observations. They usually say that they either like or dislike a person but they are not prone to mention the more admirable mental qualities of the person under fire.

Superior persons are prone to judge other persons by their mental qualities, whereas the inferior and mediocre are prone to judge others by money-standards, herd opinions, and their personal likes and dislikes. It takes a good mind to know a good mind. A good mind readily understands a poor mind but a poor mind can not appreciate a good mind except in a very superficial way.

Man's attitude toward his fellow-man has changed greatly during the time of recorded thought on this matter. Primitive man was motivated apparently largely by the three major instincts and his desires were achieved largely by force; the physically strong and the crafty survived. Then came helpful influences in the way of religious idealism, charity, political and economic beliefs and practices-some of which were good and some were bad-the foolish idea that all men are born free and equal, wholesale uplifting, philanthropy, and legalized methods of compelling the upper classes to provide for the lower. Finally, because of the knowledge and teaching of psychologists, men are beginning to judge their fellow-men by their inherent mental qualities. It should be so.

THE STUDY 

193 

81. Persons having superior mentality seldom fear the obstacles that lie in the path of intelligent personal progress.

COMMENT. Life, to the good mind, is a continuous series of problems to be solved. Persons who can solve problems like to solve problems; in fact, if there were no problems to solve life would be intolerable to them, for their daily existence would be on the intellectual level of one of the so-called lower animals that was sufficiently supplied with creature comforts.

One of the outstanding characteristics of the good mind relative to making personal progress is that it keeps its aspirations in accord with its abilities. Persons with good minds sometimes live in a world of phantasy just as do persons with poor or mediocre minds; but in making plans to carry out they are prone to differentiate carefully between what is feasible and what is not feasible. Because of this intelligent insight and judgment their progress appears to be-and really is a normal development of growth. Very few persons who rise above mediocrity do so as a result of accident or good luck. 

To win a race a horse must not be overburdened.

Jockeys are not giants. Only the good mind is intelligent enough to see this very important point early in life. Many more men could win if they knew enough at the right time to avoid the burden of unnecessary responsibility. Mediocre men usually see this point too late and inferior men apparently never see the point at all. Failure to avoid unnecessary responsibility from the late 'teens on up makes parasites out of millions of our inferior people. They do not worry much about their condition, however, for they are permitted, even encouraged, to think that society owes them and their swarms of inferior offspring a living. The upper

KNOW THYSELF 

classes the men and women who are sufficiently intelligent to make a living for themselves-must feed, clothe, and shelter the inferior. When these parasites are sick or injured they flock to free clinics and taxsupported hospitals to get free medical service. More charity, work, and annoyance for doctors! 

Fear of obstacles is usually an admission that one is either unable to cope with them or to avoid them. 

Superior persons circumvent obstacles more often than they surmount them. On the whole, superior minds do more avoiding and circumventing than they do surmounting. They know both by reason and by experience that keeping out of trouble is preferable to getting out of trouble.

People of all mental grades from the highest types down to low-grade morons sometimes set up goals to reach that are neither feasible nor intelligent. Not even the brain-trust is always trustworthy. It is well, then, that obstacles prevent the achievement of such ill-considered plans. As a matter of fact, obstacles, by thwarting the ambitions of the inferior and the mediocre -as well as a goodly number of persons who are thought to be superior-keep society from going to the demnition bow-wows.

As far as vast numbers of people are concerned, inability to do some of the things they want to do is of more importance than their ability to do honorable things. Obstacles prevent progress but they also prevent individual persons and groups from injuring society. 

Self-pity is an admission of failure-an emotional refuge for the weak and the inferior. Men and women who are making satisfactory progress do not bemoan either the real or the fancied hardships of their past or the obstacles of the future. To the good mind the evils of the past only teach and warn; they neither destroy hope nor prevent happiness; not infrequently they stimulate the mind to more intelligent effort.

THE STUDY 

195 In judging a person's mental make-up it is well to note what he does to solve the problem of obstacles, or what he says, that is, what excuses he gives, for not being able to cope with obstacles. 

82. Superior persons of the meditative type are usually sensitive to noise and more especially to unnecessary noise.

COMMENT. The story is told that Thomas Carlyle asked a next-door neighbor to dispose of a cock that crowed at five o'clock in the morning. The neighbor complied with the request. Visitors to the Carlyle home in Chelsea are shown the sage's study on the top floor of the house. Its walls were so built that the room was almost noiseproof. 

Schopenhauer complained that the cracking of whips by teamsters in Nuremberg-Schopenhauer's homedisturbed him, whereupon, the mayor and city council passed an ordinance forbidding teamsters to crack whips.

Both Carlyle and Schopenhauer were famous at the time the public began to protect them from noise, disturbances, and distractions. But there are millions of men and women of superior mentality who are sensitive to noise that are not protected except as they protect themselves. They must daily endure, not the crowing of cocks and the cracking of whips, but raucous radios, motorcycles, ambulance sirens, unserviced motor cars and trucks, brainless horn honkers, telephones, and saxophones.

Persons who are dull and stupid mentally are correspondingly insensitive to noise; in fact, many of them appear to enjoy it. In the South, negroes and many of the white persons in the lower mental brackets have their radios running with such force that they

KNOW THYSELF 

may be heard a square or two away. Negroes are especially insensitive to noise; they do not like silence. 

Many of them either sing or talk aloud when alone, and they are prone to talk much or to sing together when in groups. They enjoy the "rousements" of their church services, at which times their vocal exuberance may sometimes be heard for a distance of a hundred yards.

As civilization advances, noise-making machines, devices, and activities increase, especially in cities. Little is done to prevent unnecessary street noise except in the immediate vicinity of hospitals. And even there the rules are not always obeyed, for physicians can sometimes hear an ignorant, thick-skinned moron honking his horn in front of a hospital to inform a hospital visitor-for whom he is acting as chauffeur-that he is tired of waiting and that he wants to move on. 

The contemplative mind resents disturbances and distractions, hence, disturbing noise is likely to arouse a temporary feeling of anger or else a desire to escape, in a person who is engaged in serious mental work. Many professional men must do their reading after other members of the household have retired for the night. 

Many persons having creative minds must retire to some quiet country place to write or to produce other original work. Generally speaking the superior person finds that escape is preferable to anger and his attempts to play the role of a policeman. With many city-dwellers, however, escape is not always possible. In some way or other they pay the penalty. 

Superior children of the introvert, meditative type usually dislike noise and usually are not noisy. They do not care for rattles, drums, and other noise-making devices. Howling, whooping, noisy children may, as grown-ups, be good truck-drivers, but they are not likely to be distinguished for high-grade thinking. As a rule persons who are conspicuous for the unnecessary noise

THE STUDY 

197 they make, either as children or adults, are not conspicuous as good thinkers, for noise and intelligent thinking are incompatible. 

83. The good creative mind is endowed with fertile imagination, resourcefulness, and keen ability to criticise its own creations. 

COMMENT. Creative talent varies greatly. A little girl in a home of poverty can make a satisfactory rag doll out of a pitiful supply of material. Her mind is highly imaginative, she is resourceful, but not critical. 

The same type of imagination and resourcefulnessand lack of criticism-is found in little boys at play. 

What a great variety of knives and guns and soldiers and Indians and generals and forts they can create with a few sticks and stones!

In the 'teen age, general intelligence having increased, the critical phase of the creative mind has also increased. 

One may see persons of this age producing fairly acceptable music, poems, short stories, and, more rarely, novels. In middle life some of these youthful effusions may embarrass, but they will more likely only amuse, their creators. Most good creations, however, save a few produced in the world of fine arts, come from mature minds. Youth must wait until its technical knowledge, its worldly wisdom, and its critical faculty develop. 

In the best of creative minds the desire to create is often an absorbing passion. Once a definite work is begun the mind, consciously or unconsciously, is prone to continue with it both day and night until the work is finished. One reads occasionally of creative minds that have two or more subjects gestating at the same time. But twins, triplets, quadruplets, and quintuplets, born of the genus homo, are usually not so vigorous and are not so likely to survive as single babies are. So

KNOW THYSELF 

too with brain-children; one at a time is the safe rule. 

The creative mind usually works with the material at hand; it is both resourceful and adaptable. Robins build their nests with whatever material they can find; those built in Texas are probably very different in material from those built in Wisconsin. A novelist's best books are about persons and conditions with which he is most familiar. It was so with Dickens, Hugo, Hawthorne, and Sinclair Lewis. Burbank knew his material; so too did Edison and Steinmetz. Whether one is painting, writing novels, engraving, or making a vase, it is necessary that he know thoroughly the materials he is using in his creative work; otherwise the thing produced will be noticeably faulty.

Inability to criticise one's own creations intelligently is probably the most frequently found fault in creative work. If men love their brain-children as they do their physical progeny-and they are prone to do so-they may see no fault in them. But brain-children are like human children as far as the judgment of the world is concerned. Because their progenitors think they are perfect is no reason for the public's thinking so. The discriminating public brands a lot of creative work as trash and in the course of time throws it out on the rubbish heap or into the garbage can where it belongs. 

Very few books, poems, pictures, musical compositions, machines, devices, gadgets, and preparations of various kinds can survive the test of usefulness for more than a few years.

And this is as it should be. If our creative work serves a useful purpose for a few years or, more rarely, for a generation or two, what more could be asked?

Very few men have spoken the last word on any subject although many have thought they had done so. There is more creative work going on in the world now than ever before, for there is more need of it; besides, there

THE STUDY 

199 is more and better material to work with than ever before.

Doubtless desire for enduring fame, a kind of worldly immortality, is present in the minds of many men who do creative work. But the pleasure of creating is the usual reward, and often the only reward. What others think at the time and what posterity will think of one's creation are, after all, things of less importance. 

Creative ability and desire are manifested early in life. The wish to create is almost as common as the appreciation of beauty-the fine-arts talent. But only a very few of the persons who can imagine or visualize a thing to be created have resourcefulness enough to make it a reality. And of this latter group only a small percentage, again, have enough critical judgment to produce a worth-while product. Therefore, one must conclude that creative minds of the first rank are very, very rare. 

84. Persons having superior mentality do not use ignoble means to attain their ends. 

COMMENT. Deviations from this standard are cited more often than the more general truth. It is frequently said that "all is fair in love and war"; that "the end justifies the means"; and that "every man has his price". These and similar statements are made by the ignorant, the envious, the suspicious, and by persons who are on the defensive. Mud-slingers always have dirty hands; as a rule the ignorant are more malicious than the intelligent; the envious in the middle class are the bane of society; and the suspicious, trying to appear shrewd, sometimes give utterance to their suspicions at the expense of honor and social good taste.

KNOW THYSELF 

If a superior person does ninety-nine honorable things and one thing that appears to be of doubtful honor, some of his acquaintances, and even persons who only know of him, will recite, over and over, and often with gross exaggeration what they consider his one misdeed. They expect perfection in someone else.

Because this is true many superior persons are careful to avoid even the appearance of evil. 

There are three or four good reasons worth considering why superior persons do not follow the practice of doing things by hook or by crook for personal gain. 

To begin with, persons of good mentality set up intelligent, feasible goals; they know how to solve their problems; therefore, they do not have to lower their ethical standards to attain their ends.

Persons having superior mentality usually have a goodly amount of personal and family pride to maintain, and pride, though silent, is an unyielding influence. 

There is a difference between ambition and aspiration. Ambitious persons-usually middle-class, mentally-sometimes have a way of ignoring the difference between mine and thine. Their dishonest pecuniary practices, due largely to their unwise ambition, make copy daily and often screaming headlines in the newspapers. It is this group and this glaring defect that makes the ignorant and others who use money as a measuring-rod express their distrust of humanity in general and the upper financial classes and political mountebanks in particular. Superior persons are prone to curb unwise personal ambition, not only in regard to money, but in other things as well. 

Society gets its ethics normally from nature's aristocracy and not from the social or financial aristocracy. 

Society gets some of its current etiquette from the social aristocracy and it is sometimes prone to ape the vices but not the virtues of the financial aristocracy.

THE STUDY STUDY 

201 In any community the way nature's aristocracy on the whole solves its ethical problems will be the unwritten ethical code of that community. This may be illustrated more definitely by observing that medical ethics and legal ethics are established by the natural aristocracy, that is, the best minds in these two professions. And what is true of these two small groups is true of society on the whole. Superior men recognize, understand, and appreciate honor in other superior men. They seldom talk about their ethics but they are always careful to observe the ethical proprieties of their class. 

There are many men and women-millions of them -who will do no personal harm to anyone to attain their goal; they appear to have been born to be honest; their personal integrity is no superficial, showy pretense to be seen of men; they are nature's aristocracy. On the other hand, honesty and fair dealing that are the result of fear or policy are not to be despised. For ethical veneer is somewhat like social veneer, in that whatever may be its faults, it is usually better than no veneer. 

Some persons manifest voluntary ethical integrity early in life-even when children from six to ten years of age; others learn later and some, too late. But there are many who are honest only through fear or policy; they have to be watched at all times.

All good social conduct having to do with personal honor is the result of one or the other of two things. 

It is due either to fear or policy, or else it is due to intelligent idealism supported by inflexible integrity. 

Voluntary honesty, whether found in savant or in manual laborer, is more constant and more reliable than honesty due to fear or to policy. For several reasons, some of which have been mentioned in this comment, it is easier for a superior person to be honest than it is for an inferior.

KNOW THYSELF 

85. Persons having superior mentality are prone to take philosophically both the world's censure and the world's praise.

COMMENT. Both children and adults with childish minds are usually very sensitive in respect to what is said to them and about them. Because of this, considerable ill feeling and hatred exist between persons in the lower mental and social strata due to what someone has said. The unruly tongue either by direct action or through the medium of news-carriers or gossips keeps many persons in a state of resentment, anger, or fear.

Among defamers and gossips, competition is sometimes keen to see who can produce the most damaging utterance or insinuation. Hostile criticism in certain social circles is one of the greatest of the indoor sports and even outranks salacious stories as a means of keeping in the spotlight. The person who knows and tells the dirtiest gossip can nearly always find attentive listeners.

Human beings have always been and probably always will be the most interesting things in the world. What people say about other persons, as well as how they say it, is a good index of what they, the speakers, are. 

Superior persons usually talk about the good qualities of other superior persons, but they are prone to be silent with respect to their defects unless definitely asked an opinion about them. Inferior and mediocre persons are prone to speak voluntarily of other persons' defects, but are not so prone to speak of other persons' good qualities voluntarily unless they can gain by it. 

In the final analysis, then, it appears that all people talk much about other people-what they have said, what they have done, and what they are. In the upper mental brackets men and women are prone to talk of

THE STUDY 

203 the persons, both of the past and the present, they have read about, while in the lower mental brackets men and women, except for their discussion of crime and other news as reported in the daily press, talk about local persons they have heard about. One group reads and talks and the other group listens and talks. In the lower mental brackets conversation, on the whole, is largely about individual persons; but people in the upper mental brackets have much to talk about besides other people.

Nearly all persons who rise above mediocrity are the subjects both of extravagant praise and envious or even malicious criticism. There is no way to avoid these things. Usually, however, as one rises above the common level he accumulates enough worldly wisdom to keep his balance in spite of the praise and censure he receives. Praise will inflate an inferior or a mediocre person, but some news-carrying person with some barbs of criticism will usually come along soon and deflate him. Praise will please and stimulate a superior person, more especially praise from his mental equals or superiors. There are persons, especially in large cities, who have sunk so low they no longer care what is said of them. 

The praise-or-censure problem is one of the most common, and is occasionally a serious problem, in social life. This is especially true of the censure phase of it. 

Sometimes censure ends only in small talk and ill feeling. But a strong feeling of retaliation on the part of the defamed person may lead to trial in court for slander or perjury; or the feeling may lead to fistfights between men, to hair-pulling between women, to duelling in some countries, or even to homicide. 

It is not surprising that from the beginning of recorded thought man has been admonished to control his temper and to hold his tongue. In spite of this admonition, however, calumny is the most common

KNOW THYSELF 

form of retaliation and of all attempts to injure or to destroy.

Early in life most superior persons formulate the rule to say nothing about a person they could not with good form say to him. Many superior persons never deviate from this rule, while others break the rule occasionally but are very careful as to what they say as well as to whom they say it. But this is not the case with many of the inferior and the mediocre; for many of them use little or no discretion as to what, when, why, or to whom they say evil things of other persons. 

Superior persons are careful to avoid causing ill feelings. They know both by observation and experience that persons who speak well of others, or else keep silent, are likely to be treated the same way, at least by their equals and superiors; and it is the opinion of one's equals and superiors that is of most importance to superior persons. What the rabble think is of less importance. The Great Dane that ignores the barking of a fiste is a social Bourbon. 

That the fear of censure prevents many persons from doing things they would like to do can not be doubted. 

These things range from a desire to pick one's teeth in company on down to serious crime. Censure is not all bad, although it is mostly bad except when it is a part of one's duty. And praise is not always good, although it is mostly good. Judge an adult's mentality, in part at least, by his practices relative to praise and censure. 

86. Superior persons begin to learn early in life that the mind grows on what it feeds on. 

COMMENT. Let it be stated at once that a youth does not use the language of the psychologist, the pedagog, or the physician. If a superior youth reads good books

THE STUDY 

205 voluntarily or has good associates voluntarily he will only say that he likes them just as he' would say that he likes watermelon or fried chicken. He is no more prone to argue or to dogmatize on mental food values than he is to argue or to dogmatize on calories, mineral salts, fruit acids, and elimination.

Inferior persons have very little sense of choice relative to mental food values; they are not likely to read useful books voluntarily and their personal selection of companions and associates is noticeably poor. They are not, however, greatly to blame for this as any wellinformed person can readily see. For regardless of our pious wishes in the matter these unfortunate people are so inherently deficient in ability that but little mental growth may be expected of them.

Mentally mediocre persons have more voluntary choice as well as more appreciation of choice made for them in mental food values. They may sometimes be encouraged to select, to obtain, and to act. Many of them stop at selecting, while others act only as dabblers; they do not carry through to the end, for thoroughness is not a part of their mental make-up. Their success is no better in trying to obtain and to keep companions of superior mentality than it is in their reading. For the superior person is not likely to tolerate uninteresting bores as intimate associates if the association can be discontinued-and it usually can be. Many mediocre persons, if not too old, are prone to have temporary aspirations and ambitions to do great things; but they soon peter out and something else is taken up for a brief time. So it goes on up to middle life, after which age most of their time and thought are devoted to making a living and to providing for their dependents if they have any.

The superior mind is a learning mind. If it is of the introvert type it is likely to be bookish and either philosophic or scientific; but if it is of the extrovert type

KNOW THYSELF 

it goes in for action and adventure. From early childhood on throughout life the superior person has many advantages and opportunities that the inferior or mediocre person does not have. Learning minds like learning minds. Mutual interests make for pleasant and often for long-continued companionship in which difference in age is no barrier. The pedagog is especially interested in the child, the youth, the young adult who learns readily. And who can blame the pedagog! So it is too with other superior adults. Superior persons like superior persons. It was observed many years ago that birds of a feather flock together. As far as human beings are concerned, likes are not only prone to flock together but they are also prone to feed on similar mental foods, just as birds that flock together feed on the same physical foods. 

Inferior persons have become almost static, mentally, by the time they reach physical maturity. What little growth they make after reaching adult life has to do largely with their struggle for self-preservation; and millions of them make no noticeable growth even in this matter, for they live as poorly at fifty as they did at twenty. Many of them are seemingly no more interested in mental growth and mental food than a cow is. 

The primary hungers in animal life are for food and sex gratification. So it is too in human beings in the lower mental brackets; they live close to the earth; they are largely instinct-controlled. Their interest in, their hunger for, and their knowledge of Truth, Goodness, and Beauty are very slight. And what little knowledge they have in these matters is obtained largely in childhood because of discipline rather than as the result of voluntary effort. When the discipline is relaxed or discontinued they almost entirely cease to learn. 

But this is not the case with superior minds. They have a strong, voluntary desire to learn from childhood on past middle life and even in old age in some persons.

THE STUDY 

207 And along with this desire to learn comes the element of choice, the feeling of interest in definite subjects, and the conscious knowledge, gained through experience, that the mind grows on what it feeds. Most persons have good food for the body because of the dietary interests and practices of our people. Comparatively few Americans eat bad food even though the quantity is deficient for many of them at this time. 

But as to mental food the condition is very different. 

The quality used varies from bad to worse among a large proportion of our people who are not sufficiently intelligent to choose for themselves; nor have they sufficient will-power to follow the choice that betterinformed persons make for them. 

87. Persons having superior mentality are prone to control their aspirations and ambitions by a careful consideration of their ability to achieve them. 

COMMENT. Many adults are reluctant to take stock of their mental qualities. This is probably due to wishthinking and wilful blindness as well as to the fact that there is a dearth of illuminating books on the subject written in layman's language. Many adults refuse to face the fact of their being physically defective—more wish-thinking; some, however, are glad to know the facts and try to live accordingly; while others-notably some of the neurotics-are in constant quest of a new disease, defect, or ailment. Many neurotics are not happy without a disease to talk about; they have a mental defect, problem, or conflict that they unconsciously try to change into a physical defect. 

When contemplating a new and difficult problem to be solved superior persons are prone to ask themselves -what have I to work with in order to solve this problem? The answer to the question should contain,

KNOW THYSELF 

as nearly as possible, an accurate estimate of one's mental capital as well as knowledge of one's physical and pecuniary capital. People who expect to get somewhere in the world, whose mental capital consists of wishes, hopes, and enthusiasm, very seldom arrive; many millions of them have tried it and have failed. 

Superior persons of the introvert type are prone to look far ahead. They are not looking for quick returns. 

They expect to carry, not only their working capital but also their investments, in their heads. Of this type are many professional men, pedagogs in the upper brackets, and other so-called brain workers. 

Superior persons of the extrovert type who go in for action and adventure are prone to study well the field to be tilled. They may go in for quick returns or for the long haul-or for both. They may sell the masses five- and ten-cent articles or play for big money on the stock exchange. They get a thrill out of action and adventure; they sometimes have their ups and downs on a big scale; they are prone to live in the grand manner; their doings and goings-on are sometimes aped by innocent and ignorant lambs who later wished they had not been so ambitious as to leave the safe and simple surroundings they were accustomed to. However, many lambs out in the hinter-land are sheared at home-and not by the stock exchange.

There are two common ways by which a person's ability to do worth-while things is judged. The first and most common is by whether one likes or dislikes -the person being judged. Most of us like a goodly number of persons who are incompetent mentallypersons who are unable to solve important problems or to carry on any kind of work that requires more intelligence than that which a twelve-year-old child has. 

To consider a person mentally competent for certain work merely because we like him, or because we wish him well, is no more intelligent than it is to consider

THE STUDY 

209 man with a bad heart competent for a foot-race, merely because we like him.

The second most common way to judge a person's mentality is by what he has done. There is much merit in this method of judgment, more especially if one goes into an individual case thoroughly. Stupid persons, however, are unable to recognize an intelligent climber until he is near the top of the ladder; or, to change the figure of speech, they are unable to recognize a winner until after he wins in a big way.

The third way-as yet only in the new-born stateis to learn the evidences of superior mentality, one by one, and then use them-also, one by one-in making a mental diagnosis of oneself or of another person, just as a skilful, scholarly doctor looks for evidences of physical normality and abnormality in the persons he examines.

It is the most childish kind of wish-thinking for an adult to aspire to do a thing that he is not mentally equipped to do. This is the most frequent of all causes of failure. The superior person is prone to take stock, to know his mental equipment, first, and to avoid illconsidered plans that he lacks mental ability to carry out. 

88. Persons having superior mentality are prone to feel socially apart from the herd.

COMMENT. We who hear the intimate confessions of adults are sometimes astonished to meet superior persons who think their feeling of social aloofness a fault rather than a normal feeling. These people need more accurate self-knowledge as well as more extensive knowledge of the mental qualities of the herd. For they have been unduly influenced by herd claims of equality and by the rantings and ravings of political

KNOW THYSELF 

mountebanks, reformers, and ignorant religious teachers who want to level humanity downward, despite the fact that people can not be made equal by laws, by reforming, or by taking on any particular type of religion. And while superior persons know that the population becomes progressively sparser as the upper mental stratum is approached, yet some of them are apparently unaware that its sparseness affects their social interests by decreasing the number of persons who are interesting to them.

Social agreeability depends primarily on mutual interests. A moron and a Doctor of Philosophy might go a-fishing together and on the way there they might buy a watermelon by the roadside and eat it together, and so, together, they might enjoy the day. But as a steady companion the scholar would not, for he could not, endure the moron. Such intimate companionship continued for more than a day would be too much of a strain on the scholar's adaptability. Nearly anyone in the upper mental brackets can have brief association with nearly anyone in the lower mental brackets, butit must be brief. 

Most Americans fear being considered undemocratic; and even though they are social Bourbons in their feelings, they fear to be spoken of as uppish, aloof, high-hat, or snooty. Superior persons are not so much uppish or aloof as they are apart; and it is mostly a difference in their intellectual interests that causes their feeling of exclusiveness.

Well-developed artistic talent of any kind is also prone to cause social exclusiveness. A philosopher or a scientist can tolerate a fool and his foolish ideas about philosophy and science, but the artist is usually both sensitive and intolerant about things that jar his artistic soul. As illustrations: a first-class musician that had to remain in a room an hour while a lad of four or five years of age amused himself by picking at a piano

THE STUDY 

211 keyboard would probably be ready for a strait-jacket at the end of the hour. Soup and coffee inhalers do not offend others of their kind; but an expert inhaler -one who inhales with zest and gusto-can almost break up a dinner party where the other diners go in for more social artistry and less for noisy enjoyment. 

Bad manners are always offensive to persons with good manners whether they are at table or elsewhere. Good dancers shun poor dancers. The use of bad grammar by persons who should know better gives the person who avoids that particular language-fault a feeling not unlike that which a bad musical tone gives to a musician.

The possession of any kind of artistry, for example, in music, manners, language, painting, dancing, or whatnot, makes its possessor sensitive and gives him a desire to exclude those who offend. Artistry of some kind is found in a greater number of people than is marked intelligence; therefore, faults in artistry make for social exclusion more often than do a lack of intellectual interests.

Millions of people are inherently and boldly parasitical. They are always alert to try to get something for nothing. Men who are thought to have a little money in excess of their needs must avoid beggarsbeggars for personal needs, beggars for noble causes, and beggars for causes principally to maintain some salaried job-holders. Professional men, too, must avoid free-information and free-service beggars. They call us physicians on the phone, they visit us in our offices if we will admit them, they lie in wait for us on the street, at social gatherings, at church, and tell us about their indigestion, about Grandma's kidneys, and the baby's nettle-rash.

Social aloofness and social exclusion are, for the most part, only protective measures. If a person says he does not need to protect himself, it is a good indica-

KNOW THYSELF 

tion that he has little or nothing to protect. There are people of that kind; they are in the lower mental brackets. 

89. The superior mind has a clear appreciation of the element of continuity in mental growth and accomplishment. 

COMMENT. Emerson said, "If Minerva offered me a gift and an option, I would say give me continuity. 

I am tired of scraps."

There are people-millions of them-who appear to be wandering aimlessly from day to day picking up unimportant, unrelated scraps. Such persons lack purpose, therefore, they lack choice. They know many things that are not true; they know a few half-truths; they do not know how to select and to integrate their scraps of knowledge, therefore, they lack wisdom. 

Among physicians to take a so-called scholarly group are those who pass more time reading about sports than they do reading medical journals and new textbooks; they know batting averages and golf scores better than they know the diagnosis and treatment of disease. Their clients judge them and like them because of their personality, their professional assurance, and their promises rather than because of their scholarship, ability, and professional integrity. And what is true of men in the medical profession is true of men in many other occupations and professions; they fail to put first things first.

A man is at any time what his inheritance and his past experience make him. This fact should be selfevident and yet very few persons appear to be aware of it. Most persons, save a few in the upper mental brackets, appear to think they can do whatever they wish to do, merely by wishing it. More childish wish-

THE STUDY 

213 thinking. But one can not become an intelligent soldier merely by donning a soldier's uniform; nor can one become a scientist merely by equipping and owning a laboratory or by passing a few days in somebody else's laboratory. It appears that many of our people still believe in magic and luck rather than good inheritance plus a lot of hard work to make a somebody out of a nobody.

If one wants to know what kind of work a man can do well, or even learn to do well, let him first find out what the man has done and how well he did it. If a man has done nothing well by the time he is twenty-five or thirty years of age, the probability is he will never do anything well. There are but few exceptions to the rule, for ability to do things well is manifested early in life. Most poor workmen are expert wishers.

Dabblers, of whom the world has millions, lack continuity. They appear to do nothing well enough to please themselves or anybody else. At best they are satisfied with low standards of accomplishment; they are not masters of their craft. It is not strange, then, that their interest and enthusiasm soon peter out. Dabbling is to be expected with respect to pastimes and avocations, for these are not especially important matters; but occupational dabblers make up a considerable proportion of our fifteen millions or more of wage earners that are now supported by the taxpayers and charities.

The superior mind, whether introvert or extrovert, manifests purpose, choice, and continuity. A person's interests in life must be controlled by purpose lest he waste his time and dissipate his energy and get nowhere. The man who is Jack-of-all-trades and master of none has been recognized as a pathetic failure for many centuries.

Emerson had a good mind and he knew how to use it. If his statement, here quoted, was an admission.

KNOW THYSELF 

of fact, it is interesting to note what he considered, at the age of fifty years, his worst mental defect. 

Possibly he thought he had frittered away too much time on unimportant matters-most people have done more or less of this-but in all probability he wrote the statement about the most desirable boon that he could receive from the goddess of wisdom largely to teach his readers, in a striking way, the necessity of continuity. 

90. Persons having superior mentality are usually endowed with a strong feeling of self-respect. 

COMMENT. The lexicographer tells us that selfrespect is such a regard for one's own character as will restrain one from unworthy action. The question arises at once is a strong feeling of self-respect an evidence of inherent mental superiority or is it only an artificial thing, an accident of environment, like one's belief in democracy, the Divine Right of Kings, or the impropriety of eating pie with a knife? If one looks at the subject carefully, it appears that the feeling of selfrespect is closely related to the instinct for self-preservation and more especially to that of social self-preservation; hence, it is primarily inherent rather than an artificial thing. Also if one looks about carefully he will see that those persons who solve their life-problems best have the most exacting and inflexible self-respect; while those persons who solve their life-problems poorly, or not at all, have very little self-respect, and what they have is easily bent. A person's self-respect is a fairly accurate index of his problem-solving ability; and his problem-solving ability is the surest index known of his mental quality, on the whole. 

There is probably no feeling that the superior person is more sensitive about than his feeling of self-

THE STUDY 

215 respect. Injury to it by someone else may be forgiven but it is not easily forgotten. One may speak unfavorably of a man's political beliefs, of his religion, of his scientific knowledge, of his judgment of books, or of his skill at bridge, and time will soon heal the injury; but when one injures another man's self-respect he is likely to have made an enemy of him for years, and perhaps even for life.

Self-respect and a feeling that one is important to someone else are the last noble feelings of supporting character to remain in persons who are gradually but surely descending to Avernus. When these feelings are lost, all is lost.

Self-respect varies as mental qualities vary. It appears that the basis of self-respect is integrity based on intelligence. Among persons in the lower mental brackets where both intelligence and integrity are lacking, the feeling of self-respect is not strong. Selfrespect is a protective feeling. If one has nothing to protect, it is not likely that he will have a strong feeling of self-respect. And the converse is obviously true, namely, that if one has much integrity to protect, he will have a strong feeling of self-respect. 

In the study of crime and parasitism it is clearly apparent that both criminals and parasites are greatly deficient in integrity and self-respect. This is especially true of persons who commit premeditated crimes and of persons who are habitually parasitical. The man who kills another man in anger usually has more selfrespect than the hold-up man who kills to get and then to get away. The unemployed man who is forced to beg for food may have much more self-respect than the habitual bill-beater who lives well. Most bill-beaters have very little self-respect, but they have very thick skins. There are obviously two kinds of self-protection.

Self-respect appears to be a special mental gift some-

KNOW THYSELF 

what like the sense of justice; in fact, it is closely akin to justice. One may sometimes see an admirable manifestation of self-respect in working men, both manual laborers and artisans. And one may also see a deplorable lack of self-respect in men in the upper economic and social brackets. Except for the ability to be honorably self-supporting, money is a poor measuring-rod with which to judge one's fellow man.

The quality of the mind determines the voluntary quality of one's ethical and moral conduct. Obedience to the demands of self-respect is a voluntary thing. 

Conduct of this type is more admirable than is obedience to the ethical and moral rules of the herd because of fear of being punished for disobedience; that is, a man who is voluntarily honorable is of a higher type, mentally, than a man who refrains from dishonorable conduct only because of fear. 

91. Persons having superior mentality are not prone to overestimate themselves, their importance, or their influence, in any way. 

COMMENT. Nearly all adults think of themselves in their day-dreams either as playing the role of some very important person or else of being very important themselves. Every man is king in his own air-castle; that is the reason he builds it. It is only a step from controlled phantasy to uncontrolled delusions of grandeur. Sometimes a little too much whiskey will cause one to take the step. The difference between the inferior person and the superior person in this matter is that the inferior person is prone to get his phantasy confused with his facts, whereas the superior person nearly always keeps them entirely separate. 

Comparatively few persons know how to exercise authority over others. Adults in the lower and middle

THE STUDY 

217 mental brackets are likely either to relax their authority to the point where they have none or else go to the other extreme and become tyrannical. This is seen with respect to parental authority in millions of American homes. It is not strange that millions of children. have bad manners and bad conduct. They would have to be born with the mental make-up of a Macaulay not to have.

There are several reasons why superior persons do not take themselves too seriously. Persons with good minds usually have at least a few associates that also have good minds. Meeting one's equals and superiors daily is not conducive to inflation of the ego. Persons with good minds are usually readers of good books; and writers of good books are usually far better informed on the subject about which they write than their readers are, hence the reader meets his superior by means of the printed page. Any adult who has a superior mind knows there are vast fields of knowledge that he can not even enter to say nothing of cultivating them extensively. He knows his limitations both as to ability and as to time. He knows that the most he can do is to till a very small field with only occasional wanderings, lead by curiosity, into the fields of others.

The judgment of one's equals and superiors is the most accurate and their commendation and praise are the most highly appreciated. The inferior are prone to attack the superior because of envy or else to overestimate them because of ignorance. Knowing this, superior persons usually ignore all the unfavorable as well as much of the favorable criticism that comes from the inferior.

An ignorant person might think that men and women who receive a great deal of favorable publicity, either because of their official positions or because of some worth-while things they have done, must necessarily develop an exaggerated feeling of self-importance; but

KNOW THYSELF 

to think this would only be an unwitting confession of what he would do if he were in their place. As a matter of fact most of the adverse criticism of the superior person by the inferior is due either to ignorance or to envy-and sometimes to both. General 

Lee, the idol of the South, was a very simple, unaffected man. And Colonel Lindbergh, probably the most loved persons of modern times, avoids publicity and insists on being left alone. So it has been and is with nearly all persons who have been the object of much heroworship.

Success is sometimes a heady wine to the inferior and mediocre. But all intoxications from that cause are only temporary. Such persons usually become sober after they blow off a little steam and do a little public strutting. 

One hears and reads much of men in authority, usually politicians or ecclesiastics, who took or take their positions too seriously. Because of the power they had or have, they have received much publicity, and they are remembered by the things they should have left undone. The activities, both public and private, of tyrants and dictators make interesting reading for students of abnormal psychology as well as for students of government, sociology, and organized religion. 

Very few men wield an influence, even for good, beyond their time and place; and, in some ways, it is well that this is so. The world would make but little progress if men of the present gave blind obedience to traditional beliefs and practices. It must be remembered that what is considered good influence today might not be good fifty years from now. Many superior men would like to immortalize themselves in some way, but fortunately they can not do it. 

No man who has a good sense of humor overestimates himself. Any man who is thought by the public to have risen above mediocrity, and is accordingly placed

THE STUDY 

219 on a pedestal, can, with intelligence, do only one or the other of two things about it: he either can remain embarrassed by the unreality of his position, or he can laugh. He usually laughs. 

92. Persons having superior mentality have a strong tendency to accumulate wisdom. 

COMMENT. The question comes at once to the mind of the reader-do not all persons want to collect wisdom? But after one observes persons carefully with respect to this mental quality for months and years, one must answer-no; not in the lower mental brackets. The lack of wisdom among these people is sufficient proof for the statement.

Wisdom, according to the lexicographer, implies the highest and noblest exercise of all the faculties of the moral nature as well as the intellectual. In less ponderous language one might say that the essence of wisdom appears to be to know those things necessary for one's particular calling and station in life. Wisdom is primarily concerned with the welfare of self. 

Many persons have considerable knowledge, that is, they can remember and repeat things they have read or they have heard, and yet they have very little wisdom. 

But what does it profit a man to be versatile, even ornamental, if he is not useful?

Wisdom has to do largely with self-preservation and the attainment of satisfactory social relations with both individual persons and the herd. Inferior persons are conspicuously deficient in wisdom at twenty, at forty, at sixty. Life, itself-even the discipline of necessity -seems to teach the inferior very little. 

Literally millions of adults know how to run the national, state, and local governments, their neighbors' business, schools, corporations, churches, and anything

KNOW THYSELF 

else that is mentioned in their presence, and yet they can not make an honest living for themselves. Among other grievous defects, they lack wisdom.

When one speaks to the inferior about knowing and doing the necessary things to be able to live honestly and honorably in the sight of God and man, he sees many of them manifest the same indifference to the subject that most of us would manifest if someone suggested to us that we learn Sanskrit. They are plainly not interested.

Next to the generally inferior the most interesting group to study, because of their lack of wisdom, is made up of persons who are specially gifted with one talent. 

Among these are many persons richly endowed with a fine-arts talent, creative talent, and even an occasional person who is plus in scientific ability. Many of these people need a guardian. They can sing, or play, or paint; they can write novels, short stories, or poems; they can do good work in a laboratory; but they can scarcely make a living for themselves. And socially they are failures.

The one-talent mind is not a superior mind, interesting and even useful though it may be. Usually such persons are morons, except for their one special gift; hence, wisdom-the product of what students of the mind call general intelligence—is lacking in them.

A person is useful, not because he knows many things, but because he knows the right thing at the right time. Inferior persons lack an intelligent sense of choice in learning. They appear not to know or even to care-what is desirable and necessary to learn for their own welfare. And here comes up again the voluntary element in learning and doing. The superior mind takes on wisdom voluntarily-almost instinctively. This appears to be its natural bent. The mediocre mind accumulates wisdom slowly and often needs to be urged to do so. Not infrequently one sees a person

THE STUDY 

221 of forty who has just learned some simple thing that he, himself, admits he should have learned at twenty. 

The inferior are worse still, for they are usually quite as ignorant and indifferent at sixty as they were at twenty.

The superior mind is conscious of its growth. It is also pleased with its growth-pleased with the increase of its fund of general knowledge, as well as with its conscious accumulation of wisdom. For a while the good mind puts useful knowledge first, especially between the ages of about fifteen to fifty; it is nevertheless aware of the fact that for social reasons it must also have a store of the ornamental. A mind that devoted itself solely to Truth and utility to the neglect of Goodness and Beauty would not be socially acceptable to many persons. 

93. With respect to group activities, persons having superior mentality are prone to be critical observers or leaders rather than followers.

COMMENT. The masses, that is, the inferior and many of the mediocre, are followers. They have much faith in political leaders, labor-union leaders, strikebreakers' leaders, leaders of this, and leaders of that.

Being unable to solve some of their more serious problems by their own personal efforts, large numbers of people follow the rule that in union there is strength. 

Besides, it is easier to follow than it is to think. 

As soon as it is known that a considerable number of persons are forming a group for their mutual betterment, a leader-sometimes honest, sometimes intelligent, and sometimes both-will appear to make a speech, carry the flag, and make for himself a salaried job. That is the best phase of this matter. The worst phase of it has to do with the mountebanks and charla-

KNOW THYSELF 

tans who are self-appointed leaders of the rabble. They are interested in graft, glory, and power; they strive earnestly, shrewdly, and sometimes successfully to get what they want until some other mountebank comes along and supplants them.

The extrovert type of mind is very likely to be a leader in action and adventure. He usually has a loud voice, a thick skin, and a come-on-boys attitude toward his followers. He shines with special brightness in luncheon clubs, chambers of commerce, and other groups who want to put something across, go over the top, or make a big public splash. The extrovert who aspires to lead is prone to splash both publicly and privately. 

The leader having the introvert type of mind often does not know that he is a leader. His followers may not know one another. He does not go in for organizations, noise, publicity, and splashing. He attracts and influences people usually by means of the printed page rather than by the microphone and the megaphone. He is not heard so much as he is read and quoted. One could hardly imagine Macaulay, Mill, Carlyle, or Emerson going before a large group of men and saying- "Come on now, boys; let's put this thing over in a big way!"

Most superior persons both of the introvert and extrovert type of mind, however, neither aspire to be leaders nor do they want to be led. They can think for themselves; they can solve their own problems, and they prefer to do so. Many of them are suspicious of leaders and, therefore, they merely observe carefully trends and movements of groups both large and small. They have a wait-and-see attitude toward much of the organized hokum that appears on the scene from time to time. 

Persons who have good minds are willing to study and to consider the best thought available on subjects that are profitable for them to know about; for example,

THE STUDY 

223 good professional men read their so-called current authorities. But they are reluctant to admit they are uncritical followers of anyone. Sheep and inferior persons follow; mediocre persons copy, ape, and imitate; superior persons have originality, selective judgment, and they think for themselves.

It is not wrong, in fact, it is right to follow, to copy, to ape, and to imitate, provided one selects the right models and standards. But there's the rub! If one can not think for himself, the best thing he can do is either to get some superior person to think for him or else follow the example of someone of ability who has had experience of that particular kind. Most persons do this in various ways regardless of their mental qualities. It is, in fact, an unfailing evidence either of inferiority or of envious mediocrity never to be willing to recognize and to say voluntarily of someone that he is a master of his craft. 

94. Persons having superior mentality are prone to deal with unavoidable misfortunes-past, present, and future-with intelligence and reason rather than with emotion and prolonged regret. 

COMMENT. Probably no phase of adult infantilism is more annoying to bear with than hard-luck stories; at least, it is so with physicians. Inferior as well as many mediocre persons are prone to make much of their losses and hardships due to misfortune and bad luck. 

And while most of these losses and hardships are due to lack of intelligence, yet that part of the story is carefully omitted by persons who feature misfortune as an excuse for failure.

Not infrequently habitual failures and misfits tell over and over their well-embellished stories of misfortunes they suffered from two to twenty years before.

KNOW THYSELF 

Such excuses are nearly always an evidence of mental inferiority and are unwitting admissions, on the part of the persons who use them, that they were, and still are, unable to solve their problems.
