In 1950 millions of inferior and mediocre persons will still be reciting stories of how the economic depression that began during the reign of Herbert the Great ruined them financially for all time, even though at the time the depression began they had little or nothing to ruin. 

Fiction sometimes sounds better than fact. 

Excuse-giving is as unnatural and as humiliating to the superior mind as it is natural and convenient to the inferior mind. Superior persons are not prone to regret their past, more especially that part of it over which they had control. Besides, they know that if one has been a fool or an ass that it only makes matters worse to tell other persons about it. And as to real misfortunes, they hold to the same rule: the less said about them the better. The folklore of any people contains several maxims setting forth this attitude toward harmful or disagreeable experiences and conditions that could not be avoided. In America, for example, we say that "What can't be cured must be endured;" and that "There's no use in crying over spilled milk."

It can not be brought to the mind of the student too often that the best single lead in judging the good mind is to know the number and importance of the worth-while things a person has done voluntarily. This may, for obvious reasons, take considerable time to learn. On the other hand, in order not to be controlled by sentiment and wish-thinking, it is well to be warned that one is dealing with a poor mind when the contact is first made. Such knowledge may prevent one's employing or giving or lending money to an habitual failure. Misfits and failures are prone to exhibit their poor mental makeup on very short acquaintance, es-

THE STUDY 

225 pecially by their unwise, tactless telling of their misfortunes and hard luck. Nearly all moochers and hardened parasites have a hard-luck story. People who have too much to explain are usually either constitutionally inferior or else they are impulsively foolishand one fault is as bad as the other. 

Loss of anything valuable causes mental pain. Children and childish adults are not prone to suffer in silence whether the suffering is due to physical or mental causes. It would be quite natural for them to talk about their losses at the time they were sustained. But to continue to bewail misfortunes and bad luck is not due entirely to the loss sustained, for even the most inferior of them know that the thing lost can not be regained by talking about it. The bewailing and repining and regretting are the result of different causes: first, inability to solve the problems necessary to form new plans and to execute them; and second, to justify and excuse parasitism. They are defense mechanisms.

Persons with good minds grow, for growth is a fundamental desire or passion with them. They would like to have a steady growth and a steady accumulation of those material things that make for comfort and security. But owing to the vicissitudes of life there are unavoidable checks, accidents, and destructive forces that prevent their growing according to their conceived plans. Good minds are prone to handle these obstructions to growth uncomplainingly and effectively. 

95. The acquisitive desires of the mentally superior person, aside from money, are strongly individualistic rather than herd-controlled.

COMMENT. In a civilized medium self-preservation is attained largely by having money or its equivalent; hence, all persons from the imbecile grade on up are

KNOW THYSELF 

willing to accept money, although many of them are not willing to earn it and save it. That part of the matter may be admitted first and then dismissed. 

But even before the superior person has obtained economic security one notes that his interests and tastes with respect to acquisitiveness differ greatly from those of the herd. The meditative or introvert type of person, unless he has access to a good library and reading room, is prone to accumulate books and other reading matter, bookish companions, and book knowledge. In addition to these things he endeavors to acquire economic security, peace, physical comfort, and leisure. 

Then, if he has a good pipe and a can of tobacco, all's well.

However, that is not all the individualism that contemplative persons manifest, for they are prone to go in quite generally for things that are different: different types of personal articles both useful and ornamental; a different type of social life; a different type of home, whether it be a cabin like Thoreau's or one more commodious. The superior person of the contemplative type takes pleasure in having something different from that which the herd has rather than in conforming to herd ways.

The superior person of the extrovert type-the man who goes in for action and adventure-usually manifests his desire for difference in acquisitiveness in wanting to have something that is bigger and better. 

He wants a bigger and better motor car, house, apartment, show, place of business, political office, business office, swimming pool, or dog kennel. In fact, he gets a great deal of pleasure out of planning or actually acquiring something that is bigger and better. He aspires to be seen and heard and to have his possessions seen by the admiring public and his envious competitors. Bigger and better is his goal. He would have the herd pass by and look at his possessions, for he

THE STUDY 

227 really enjoys a public exhibition of his possession that is bigger and better. But the contemplative man goes in more for a private exhibition, if any, limited to a few persons with more appreciation.

Nearly all children and childish adults imitate in their acquisitiveness; they ape the herd. What the herd has, they want too. They have a slight sense of choice, especially as between things of the same kind. For example, a child will select which he thinks is the best of three apples offered him. But such persons have little sense of rejection. One may note this in little boys who will take home anything they find on the street or elsewhere ranging in value from a lost puppy to a discarded bathing suit. As intelligence improves imitation in acquisitiveness decreases and choice increases.

Since acquisitiveness is one of the most constant of the primary urges it is well for the student to know his own acquisitive interests. Does he want something different, something bigger and better, or does he want what the herd has? The student will also find in observing other persons that knowledge of their acquisitive interests is useful in forming a conclusion relative to their mental make-up.

Choice in things desired may be seen in superior children rather early in life. And in adults, choice in things desired and obtained is a good index of their mental quality. 

96. Persons having superior mentality discriminate carefully between what they know and what they merely think or surmise.

COMMENT. The conscience sits in judgment on our acts and ideas having to do with Truth, Goodness, and

KNOW THYSELF 

Beauty. In many persons the intellectual conscience, that is, judgment having to do with truth, is both alert and strong and does not permit the careless use of the assertion, I know. This may be seen daily, not only in the usual accuracy of these persons' speech, but also in occasional changes in their statements from I know to I think. Intellectual accuracy of this kind is characteristic of a good mind. It may be observed, too, that this feeling is just as keen when a wrong, a gain, or a loss is not likely to result from an inaccurate statement. 

The intellectual conscience demands accuracy for accuracy's sake; in this respect it is like the esthetic or artistic conscience. 

Usually persons who resort to profanity and bluster, in order to force or encourage others to believe their statements, are wilful liars. The more profanity and bluster, the less truth.

In the small talk of the day among the inferior and the mediocre, when malice and envy are absent and when there is neither loss nor gain involved, there is much inaccuracy in statements due, apparently, to dullness or lack of ability to discern the truth. Persons who lie in this way-seemingly without bad motive-are like unmusical persons who do not discern small faults in music. They seem to have a blind spot for truth.

If a superior person makes a statement and someone who doubts it asks him if he really knows what he is talking about, the superior person is likely to feel offended; but if one asks an inferior person the same question, there seems to be no offense felt. Why is this? It appears that the superior person has integrity relative to telling the truth; he recognizes both truth and error more readily than the inferior person does; he is accurate in his speech and does not say that he knows a thing when he only thinks it or surmises it; he expects to be believed, and he resents being doubted. On the other

THE STUDY 

229 hand the inferior person has been doubted and told so, so often, that he is no longer sensitive about it. 

The inferior not only think inaccurately and express themselves carelessly, but they are also notoriously careless quoters. That is the reason that stories grow and grow. The inferior are prone to tell a thing the way they want it to sound; they are not greatly interested in the truth in the matter.

A person's accuracy in recognizing and expressing truth is an interesting mental study always, and a moral problem sometimes. Knowledge of a person's mental qualities makes for better understanding of the moral phase of the matter.

Among every one hundred unselected persons one will find a certain number of the argumentative type and another considerable number of the opinionated type. 

These incurable sophomores, as a rule, are not interested in truth and its accurate expression so much as they are interested in arguing and broadcasting unproved and even unprovable assertions. They also like to hear themselves talk. To the superior person they are rather egotistical, unreliable pests.

Many superior persons, early in life, learn to listen to small talk in about the same way they listen to street noise or the sound of an electric fan. They are wise. 

Also a man is wise if he knows enough to judge a statement largely by its plausibility and its source. A man of worldly wisdom knows that nearly all persons will deceive in some way for self-protection, and that a considerable part of the whole will deceive for gain. He also knows that it his duty to know what to believe and what not to believe of the things he hears and reads. 

Credulous persons need a guardian.

It is a good lead in judging a man's mental quality to observe his use of the expressions-I know, I think, and I surmise; for, let it be repeated, the language oft proclaims the man.

KNOW THYSELF 

97. Persons having superior mentality are very conscious of the necessity for obedience.

COMMENT. There are three common motives in obedience: to win reward, to avoid punishment, and to uphold certain standards, ideals, and principles. The first two are observed very often in children and in childish adults, while all three may be observed in the conduct of superior persons.

Obedience to external authority is due largely to habit, with reward or the avoidance of punishment as secondary considerations. The military service is a good example of this, for nearly all soldiers who have passed beyond the rookie stage have the habit of obedience. In civilian life obedience to traffic signals in cities is a good illustration of this habit. 

Obedience to self-originated and self-imposed rules is, on the whole, a far more complex problem than is obedience to external authority. Many persons living in cities are hardly aware of obedience to external authority, other than traffic rules and postal regulations, day after day, and sometimes, even, week after week.

And with our farmers and others living in remote places there must be still less contact with external authority except that which is incident to family life and the day's work. But obedience to self-imposed rules is a problem that is with us always, regardless of where we live or how we live. 

To the psychologist the reward of obedience to selfimposed rules is self-approbation and the approbation of one's fellow man. What the psychologist calls obedience to self, the religious person may call obedience to God; and Carlyle called it obedience to Good. Probably all are right.

In matters of obedience the superior mind finds the hardest taskmaster to be the enlightened conscience in

THE STUDY 

231 all three of its phases-intellectual, esthetic, and moral. 

If someone else tells a man he is wrong, the man may honestly doubt it or deny it. But when his own enlightened conscience tells him he is wrong, he will have to do one of three things about it: immediately make amends to the person or persons involved, try to rationalize himself out of trouble, or trust to time and repression to put the wrong committed out of his memory. 

Some of the extreme environmentalists would have us believe that obedience is only a matter of training. 

And here they are wrong again—as usual. Obedience to external authority is sometimes largely a matter of training and discipline; this is especially true when punishment for disobedience is almost sure. But other things being equal, the better the mind, the better the obedience. Army officers have much less disciplinary trouble with enlisted men who have average or good mentality than they have with soldiers who have poor mentality; yet all have the same discipline. And in a family of five children the one having the best mind is almost invariably the most obedient, although all have had the same environment.

The superior person has a strong desire to maintain his integrity. To do this he must obey his inner feelings -his conscience, his sense of duty, his sense of responsibility, and his code of ethics. To refrain from breaking the social or civil law, in order to maintain one's principles, is a very different motive than refraining from breaking the law because of fear of being discovered and punished. The former is characteristic of the superior mind; the latter, of the inferior. 

Nearly all superior adults are distinctly obedienceconscious. So much so, in fact, that if they forget to do a thing they should have done, they are usually greatly embarrassed because of it. On the other hand one may see many inferior adults and children, with so little sense of obedience in worth-while matters, that

KNOW THYSELF 

they are almost without dependability-nor do they have any desire to do better.

Then there is another type of mind farther down the scale, usually mediocre or of still lower grade, that wilfully features disobedience. Depending upon their ages and opportunities, people of this type range from the so-called spoiled child to bandits. Their perverse conduct brings humiliation and sorrow to their parents and much trouble to juvenile and criminal courts. 

The foolish idea, now fairly prevalent in America, that people-even children-will do the right thing if left alone, is worse than inane wish-thinking and should be discarded. People of all ages and of all mental grades need discipline; they should be forced to obey. In the upper mental brackets discipline is nearly always selfimposed and is largely a matter of self-restraint; that is, the person does the thing he should do, rather than some other thing he would like to do. In the middle and lower mental brackets self-discipline becomes increasingly less and there is a corresponding need for more external discipline. Lack of obedience is more than a matter of pedagogical and parental speculation and quibbling, for it is an important factor in producing the great amount of parasitism, poverty, crime, and bad manners that we now have. 

98. In their personal philosophy of life the mentally superior are prone to be happy because of escape from the world's ills rather than to be unhappy because of envy and ill-founded desire.

COMMENT. Self-preservation depends upon two things: acquiring something and escaping something; the intelligent adult will recognize the importance of each. In man the acquisitive instinct is manifested earlier in life than is the instinct of escape. Also in

THE STUDY 

233 man the instinct of escape is not so strong as it is in the lower animals; for example, quail and chickens only a few days old have a highly developed instinct to escape danger. Human beings above the age of three or four years, unless they are idiots, have instinct and intelligence to escape immediate danger, but a large part of them have little forethought about escaping hardships and the generally undesirable conditions in life that are more remote.

All persons are more or less subjective, and compare their condition and what they have or have not with other persons above them and below them. Happiness often results from comparing one's condition with that of persons in worse condition; regret and envy result from comparing one's condition with that of persons who have more of desirable things. One sees daily on the street persons that are in deplorable condition, physically, mentally, morally, and economically. One may feel and express sorrow for them, but one also feels happy-although he usually does not express it-because he is not in their condition.

To be consoled by a feeling of escape is far more intelligent than to be disappointed and vexed by unattained ambition. If a man who is economically secure says voluntarily that he has never had very much but that he has escaped a lot, he has spoken as a philosopher of the first rank.

Many of the mentally inferior have very little, except good health, to give them pleasure. They are unhappy because they desire things they have not enough intelligence to get and to keep. They have very little upon which to base a philosophy of escape. 

Many of the mentally mediocre are looking the wrong way to be happy. Their ill-founded ambition makes them compare themselves with the wrong social group or financial stratum. Ambition, without intelligence to achieve it, destroys contentment.

KNOW THYSELF 

Persons in the upper mental brackets, except when physically incapacitated for work, nearly always have enough problem-solving ability to obtain the necessaries and comforts of life. Knowledge of what they have escaped and are escaping makes them happy; and they usually curb their ambition to the extent that they are not made unhappy by regret and envy. They answer intelligently the questoin-what's the use of wanting a thing there is no likelihood of one's getting? Millions of the inferior and mediocre can not, or at least do not, answer this question intelligently; for, among other things, they do not discriminate between things they need and things they desire.

The philosophy of escape appears to be an inherent, self-evolved thing. It seemingly can not be acquired by many, for one sees both men and women who, regardless of their association with superior persons, are as devoid of it at forty or sixty as they were at twenty. 

Such persons do not discriminate carefully between facts and fancy or between needs and desires; nor do they fully understand that after the necessaries and comforts of life are provided for, that escape from pain is more important than the direct pursuit of pleasure. One notes in our present time of widespread economic distress that many persons are conscious of a philosophy of escape, who did not have it in times of prosperity. Men and women who formerly scorned the idea of having only enough to live on comfortably are now very glad to have enough. 

99. Persons having superior mentality usually have large capacity either for general or for specialized learning, and occasionally for both.

COMMENT. It is now quite generally believed among students of mental qualities that, as Thorndike states,

THE STUDY 

235 difference in original capacity plays a large part in producing the differences that are actually found between one human being and another. If one is on the defensive-for example, as parents usually are in making excuses for their children, or if one is merely wishing for the welfare of persons in the lower mental bracketsthis belief seems harsh and deterministic. If, on the other hand, one holds in mind the very evident fact that all progress is made by following mental leaders—men and women who have large capacity for learning-one is truly glad that nature produces a few persons with mental capacity far in excess of that of the inferior and average minds with which one comes in contact daily. 

The lack of suitable environment and advantages for learning are excuses that are greatly overworked in America. There are very few American boys that have not had as good opportunities as Lincoln, Carnegie,

Marshall Field, and Edison had; there are millions of 

American boys and young men who have had far better opportunities than Huxley, Burbank, Walter Williams, and Conrad had. Very few persons have failed because of lack of advantages compared to the millions who have failed because of lack of capacity to learn. One can not put a gallon of water into a pint cup; and a shallow pate can contain but little worthwhile learning.

Let it be granted, though, that the persons named in the previous paragraph reached a point in development too high for general consideration-that fame is for the very few. The standard of mental efficiency that everyone above the imbecile grade should aspire to, and that failure to reach and to maintain should classify a person as an undesirable citizen, is: to be honestly and honorably self-supporting, to support one's offspring if one has any, and to live agreeably with one's fellow man. 

And yet in America we have millions of people who

KNOW THYSELF 

have insufficient mental capacity to meet these simple requirements.

The amount of formal school work one has done is not a good test of one's mental capacity; let that be admitted at once. A far better test is to judge a person by what he has done after his school days are over. 

But what the school test lacks in accuracy it makes up in frequency, for nearly all persons in America have had fairly good school advantages even though the results are disappointing. Millions of grade pupils do not even want to enter high school; a large proportion of high-school matriculants do not finish their work; and many of those who finish high school do not even want to go to college. Only about one-third to two-fifths of the matriculants in the freshman college classes are graduated four years later; and their so-called Commencement-for many of them, at least-is not the beginning of a scholarly life, but the beginning of intellectual lethargy and decay. From kindergarten to postgraduate and professional school there is an appalling lack of mental capacity to carry on even averge school work. And all the curriculum changes and improvements in the world will not enable a teacher to make a scholar out of a student that lacks mental capacity. 

Lack of capacity to learn, rather than lack of opportunity, is the cause of most ignorance. And this is true, not only of the imbecile who vacantly stares into space, but also of the enthusiastic dabbler who begins to learn many things but learns none of them well. 

But, ask the environmentalists and wishful parentscouldn't little Willie, of moronic mental make-up, be made to learn and to expand his pint-size mental capacity into one of barrel-size? And one must answer, no.

And even if this were so, the discouraging thing to be faced is-who's going to make little Willie learn?

Little Willie with a pint-size mind, but little filled at seven, will probably be dull, stupid Bill with a pint-size

THE STUDY 

237 mind almost filled at twenty. Human beings appear to increase but little in mental depth after reaching physical maturity. After this age their growth is lateral rather than vertical. If a person does not clearly manifest problem-solving ability, insight, persistent voluntary interest in worth-while things, and conscious effort to maintain his integrity at twenty, he is not likely to change noticeably for the better later in life, regardless of parental wishes and environment.

Most students of mental qualities have an abundance of mediocre and inferior clinical material to study daily. 

But often one must resort to biography to get details of minds of great capacity. For this purpose the biographies of Macaulay, William Morris, Goethe, and Leonardo da Vinci are very illuminating. All of these men had great capacity for both general and special knowledge. 

And Macaulay's memory has been the subject of much comment and wonderment since he was a lad in his 'teens. His mind was truly encyclopedic. 

100. The good mind has both will and ability to learn. 

In addition to these the great mind has great capacity for learning.

COMMENT. The words can and will are often used in discussing or speculating on someone's mental ability. 

It may, for example, be said of an inferior or a mediocre person who is being discussed by name, that he can learn a certain thing if he wills to do it. Perhaps so; but the insurmountable defect may be that he can not will.

Superior minds are learning minds; this fact can not be stated too often. They not only have ability to learn but they also have the will to learn. The mediocre mind appears to have more ability to learn than it has will to learn and interest in learning; because of this it must often be urged or even forced to learn. The inferior

KNOW THYSELF 

mind is noticeably deficient in both will and ability to learn as well as in interest in learning. Inferior persons are usually recognized and rated with fair accuracy by everyone except their parents; they continue to wish, as well as to be blind to facts.

Once in a long while one sees an adult who wills to learn a worth-while thing, but who has very little ability to learn. His efforts are commendable but pathetic. 

Many mediocre persons know their mental limitations, but they are blest with strong determination to use what sense they have, or, as some of them modestly say what little sense they have. Such persons are often spoken of as the salt of the earth, the backbone of society, or the hub of the wheel. Regardless of how they are described, they rank next to the superior minds in social value.

The mediocre mind is prone to limit its curiosity and its efforts to learning what was and what is; that is, it follows the beaten path. The superior mind, however, is not content to be a follower or an imitator, but, after having learned what was and what is, it has enough curiosity and spirit of adventure to seek new knowledge. 

This is especially characteristic of the scientific mind. 

The superior mind takes more interest in doing something new or different than it does in excelling in the use of that which is old. A good scientist is not likely to be a good Latinist.

The superior mind is not a slave to tradition nor is it wedded to its views and beliefs. In fact the superior mind appears to take delight in rethinking traditions and casting them out if they are harmful, as well as in discarding beliefs and practices once held to be of value. 

Sophomoric agnosticism and iconoclasm are usually only the first clumsy efforts of a mental house-cleaning. 

Young adults of superior mentality, after second thought, are more prone to revalue and rearrange some

THE STUDY 

239 of their mental furnishings than they are to throw them out on the scrap pile.

Unless a superior person is socially objectionable to his equals and superiors-which is seldom the case-he is likely to have a variety of intellectual companions. He may talk science with one, biography with another, art with the third, and literature or current events with the fourth. For, next to the joy of learning something new, the greatest pleasure of the learning mind is to talk over its newly-acquired knowledge with a kindred spirit.

There is always freshness in the learning mind. That is the reason companionship based on intellectual attraction lasts over the years, while companionship based on emotion or physical attraction is likely to be of short duration.

Theoretically any talent or mental faculty may be improved by exercise or use; actually this is hard to prove relative to will-to-learn. The best proof that comes to the physician's mind—and it is rare-is seen in persons who have suffered a severe physical incapacity for work due either to accident or to disease. Some of these unfortunates will to learn a new occupation, and some of them actually succeed in doing something worth while. 

In such cases the discipline, born of necessity, is much more rigid than that which is imposed on normally healthy persons. Generally speaking, voluntary will-tolearn changes but little from late childhood to middle life. That amount-either much or little-of will-tolearn that an older child has, it will have in adult life. up to the time that senility begins.

Freedom of the will has been the subject of much unprofitable talk, discussion, and writing. It is the size or the amount of the will that is important and not its freedom. Will, like interest, varies from zero to that of the headstrong tyrant.

KNOW THYSELF 

Ability to learn is largely the result of talent, insight, close mental integration, and interest. Interest in a subject may vary from entire absence on up to a controlling passion. Also voluntary interest in a subject varies from time to time, but the other three factors, being inherent, vary but little.

PART THREE PROGRESS 

The best that can be hoped for in the study of mental qualities is to learn a little more about them from year to year. Without any special study of the subject the average person should know more about it at forty than he did at twenty, and he should know more about it at sixty, than he did at forty. But with one year of special study and observation, one should be able to know as much about mental qualities at twenty-one as one would know without the special study at sixty; provided, of course, the subject is studied intelligently and thoroughly.

The advantages of knowing mental qualities, more especially in early adult life, need no special explanation or argument; for if a person could not see that point clearly, himself, he would not be likely to read a book of this kind.

The easiest thing to observe about a person's mental quality is, fortunately, the most profitable thing to learn -namely, how he solves or does not solve the daily problems of his life, and more especially those having to do with his three major instincts. It looks as if it would be easy to observe if a man is able to make an honest, honorable living for himself and his dependents, if he has any, and if he controls his sex and social instincts, or if they control him in a way and to an extent that are injurious to him.

The defensive attitude is one of the most difficult things for the new student to overcome, for he is likely to be prolific with excuses for himself, and perhaps for 241

KNOW THYSELF 

members of his immediate family and for his intimate friends. He may argue that both he and they were victims of bad environment, bad luck, and unavoidable misfortunes; he does not want to face the facts. But he is not so likely to have a defensive attitude for mere acquaintances and strangers, therefore his judgment of them will be more accurate. One has made considerable progress, then, in the study of mental qualities, when the defensive, excuse-giving attitude has been discontinued. 

Perhaps the second most common and most difficult problem for the student to deal with is the exception to the rule. For some reason-and he does not know just what it is-he thinks a mind should be either all good or all bad. It is seemingly difficult for him to understand that a person may be mostly inferior mentally and yet be richly endowed with talent for, let us say, music; or that one may be of genius rank in creative ability and have a few mental qualities of the moron grade.

The student may exaggerate the exceptions so grossly that he does not even believe the rule. This is usually due to his not having had enough experience in using the rule to warrant his having an opinion in the matter; or, perhaps, it may be a plain case of wish-thinking involving himself. For example, the student may find, or he may only think he has found, a person of superior mentality who is not sensitive to unnecessary noise. 

Perhaps it is his first test of the rule-his first observation. But if he will continue his inquiry on at least a hundred superior persons, he will see the value of the rule; for he will find that at least four-fifths of them are sensitive, even hypersensitive, to unnecessary noise. 

The student of mental qualities must have the same attitude toward exceptions to rules and toward defects that a physician has toward the organs of the body and their functions. There are scores of separate organs

PROGRESS 

243 in the body, any one of which may be defective either in structure or in function. One might, for example, find an athlete that is either partially or entirely deaf in one or both ears, his body being in other ways seemingly perfect. So too one may find a person richly endowed with superior mental qualities, yet lacking in one or more good qualities, the absence of which is as noticeable to the experienced mental student as partial deafness is to the average layman. If, then, the student is intelligent enough, if he has enough of the scientific type of mind, not to reject the rule because he finds an exception to it, he is making progress. 

The student should prove the rule. Some of the rules set down in this study are so obvious that they are almost self-evident; for example, the first and the last. Others are less obvious. The student should therefore test each of them on enough persons whom he thinks have superior mentality-his personal acquaintances or subjects of biography-so that he may know the truth or falsity of the rule itself. The scientific mind has a way of proving a rule or a statement of fact as true or untrue, when possible, rather than taking somebody else's opinion in the matter. After the student has the rule fixed in his mind, and after he has proved it true to his own satisfaction, he has something of value with which to judge both himself and other persons. 

He has made more progress. 

The rule should be inclusive and should apply to a general truth rather than to a particular detail. It may be said, for example, that persons having superior mentality are usually manners-conscious, and that they are prone to conform to the best rules of society of their time and place. This rule is very different from saying that persons having superior mentality do not gurgle their soup nor remove their shoes before they enter a home, for both of these practices are good form in certain nations. There is a difference between good man-

KNOW THYSELF 

ners and etiquette. The former are both natural and voluntary and have to do with the social rights of other persons; whereas, the latter has to do with more or less unimportant, artificial, and arbitrary practices like fashions or styles in wearing apparel, and are usually only a detail of personal display. 

Good manners, in the more general sense, are closely related to the three major instincts. A person has had very little worldly experience if he does not know that good manners may be the means of preventing personal altercations, quarrels, fights, bodily injury, and even death. Or to take a more common illustration: if an employee uses good manners toward, and in the presence of, his employer, he is more likely to retain his employment-another aid to self-preservation.

As to the second major instinct-sex. Surely any adult from the moron grade up knows that good manners increase sex appeal, and that bad manners diminish or even destroy sex appeal. 

And as to the social instinct, good manners do more to make for personal attractiveness and tolerance than nearly anything else, especially where there is marked difference in the mental qualities and experience of the persons in social contact. This rule applies both to voluntary and to involuntary association. These details of the last four paragraphs are given to show the student that the rule should be thought through; also they may aid him in studying the motives of human behavior.

Most students want to arrive too soon. They feel as if they should know as much about a subject after reading a book once as the author of the book has learned in several years of careful observation and thinking. Memorizing the rule, that is, knowing it as words, and knowing how to recognize the fact that the rule covers, are very different things. But after one has observed the fact that calls forth the rule in a dozen

PROGRESS 

245 or more different persons, the rule and its application should be a part of one's mental equipment. If, for example, one can recite the rule about the scientific mind and then come in contact for a few days with a real scientist, one will see why the rule was formulated. And if, in addition to this, the student could have contact with eleven more scientists, the rule would be as firmly fixed in his mind as six times seven in the multiplication table.

Learning the rules in this book and learning to apply them is much more difficult than learning the first year's work in high school algebra. This book may be read through in twelve hours. Surely a student would not expect to learn the first year's work in algebra in twelve hours. The element of time, then, should be recognized early and the student should be fully aware of the fact that the study of mental qualities covers all the rest of his life after the study is begun. It would take a great deal of ignorance or of self-conceit for one to think he could learn mental qualities in hours instead of years.

It is characteristic of the novice in studying nearly any subject to skip, to skim, to dabble with it, and not to know anything well; students of mental qualities may also be of this kind. If there are a hundred rules to be learned the student may learn two or three, or even a dozen of them, and begin at once to spread his wings and air his new knowledge. The young intelligentsia will then have a new addition and society will have a new pest.

The evidences of mental superiority-one hundred of them, at least should be as well known to the student as the multiplication table is. This statement is not intended to mean that the student should be able to recite the rules from memory as he can the multiplication table, for the author, himself, can not do that; but he should be able to recall and apply them at the right time. This

KNOW THYSELF 

is imperative if he is to be proficient in his work of judging other persons. A physician begins to judge a person physically as soon as the person comes into his presence; and if the physician knows mental qualities, he also begins to judge the mental make-up of the per- The layman who is a student of mental qualities should have his knowledge at his fingertips so that he, too, may begin to judge the mental qualities of the person or persons in his presence.

After the student has learned his rules and his evidences thoroughly and has learned to apply them intelligently, he should be able to make fairly accurate observations. He should observe carefully, think much, and talk but little. He is ready then, but not till then, to see that he is making progress in his right to judge minds, and that he is not a superficial dabbler.

As the months and years pass by the student will develop the same attitude toward his knowledge, his observations, and the persons he observes and has observed, that a physician has toward his patients, past and present. He may even adopt the language of the physician and speak of a case or of cases that he knows. 

Also as time passes and the student sees the truth and applicability of the rules, he will discard most of the wish-thinking and excuse-giving that he has used since early childhood about the mind; and he will see the advantage of judging the mind by what worth-while things it can do and does do, voluntarily, day after day. 

It is fully as important to recognize a congenitally poor mind as it is to recognize a congenitally poor body; not only for the sake of fairness and justice to the person concerned, but also as the first step in doing whatever can be done to prevent or to solve our many social problems. Also the earlier in life one is able to recognize superior minds, the better off he will be. 

After the rules have been learned and applied daily for a year or two, there is no way to discontinue their

PROGRESS 

247 use-except to die! A physician of wide experience may discontinue the practice of medicine and in a few years forget much of his medical knowledge, but not all of it; a good musician may discontinue playing the piano and finally become so out-of-practice that he can not even play well a simple piece of music; but one who is an adept in the observation and evaluation of mental qualities has no way to forget his knowledge, nor to discontinue the use of it, so long as he comes in contact with people. To borrow and change an expression of 

Moliere's he will be a psychologist in spite of himself. 

All serious study has a motive. Very likely most persons who study evidences of mental superiority do so at first, primarily, to learn about themselves. This is a commendable thing to do. Probably the most commonly known of all the advice and philosophy of Socrates is his admonition to know thyself. And yet, relatively few persons have more than a very superficial knowledge of themselves either physically or mentally; nor do they appear to want to have more. This ignorance, as far as the mind is concerned, is probably due largely to a lack of suitable books that make self-knowledge attainable for the general reader. 

The student will dissect himself slowly. He may find that he has feet of clay, although he is more likely to be disappointed with his head than he is with his feet. But whether he has feet of clay or not, he is almost certain to put his best foot forward. Also he is likely to develop some convenient blind spots in his mind's eye when he takes a personal inventory. 

And as to the student's personal defects the best that can be hoped for, socially, is that he will not become highly subjective and hold a public teaching-clinic every day, using his own mind as a clinic subject for demonstration after the manner of physicians demonstrating a diseased condition to their colleagues. No. The student should keep his closet door closed and neither

KNOW THYSELF 

invite nor permit anything but strictly impersonal discussion of mental qualities with other laymen. Confessions should be made only to professional confessors, that is, to physicians and the clergy. The student may be said, then, to have made useful progress, if he has learned to be silent, to be strictly non-communicative, about himself. He should neither boast of his good qualities nor admit and discuss his bad qualities with other laymen.

After a student has learned a few evidences of mental superiority he is almost certain to begin to make observations, to analyze, and even to dogmatize, on the mental qualities of other persons. Very likely most of his earlier observations will be on the lack of mental superiority that he sees in some of his daily associates and in men, women, and children that he meets from time to time, for that kind of clinical material is abundant. Here, again, the rule of secrecy, the rule followed by professional confessors, should be used; namely, not to permit the name of the person being discussed to be known. It is an honorable thing to keep a secret nine times out of ten. The Golden Rule applies in talking about other persons' defects in a way that might harm them. Would the student want his defects discussed by others persons, either friends or foes?

On the other hand it is sometimes very desirable to mention some of the good mental qualities of children and young adults, either directly to them or indirectly, so they may know the statements have been made. To state, for example, that Merrill and Jean solve their daily problems intelligently; that Lester and Robert appear to have scientific minds; that Catherine and Teddy are language-conscious; and that Floyd has a closely integrated mind, may help them to know themselves better.

An ounce of truthful commendation is worth a pound of fault-finding in dealing with young persons of superior mentality. There is no danger of their becoming pertly

PROGRESS 

249 egotistical; while, on the other hand, to increase their self-esteem is helpful to them.

And after the student's enthusiasm has diminished somewhat and the novelty of the study has lost some of its interest, what use will be made of the fragmentary knowledge gained? Will a large oak grow from the acorn? One must answer in the negative unless the acorn was planted in a scientific mind. The will to learn something useful is a part of all good minds, but the will to learn mental qualities thoroughly will be found in very few persons; most students will remain dabblers.

Nearly all adults having superior mentality know a little anatomy and physiology; and yet few persons know as much about these subjects as they should know for their own good. In talking to physicians many adults locate the stomach anywhere between the chin and the knees, and their knowledge of physiology is even less accurate than their knowledge of anatomy. 

Most adults in the upper five per cent of our people have had more opportunities to study anatomy, physiology, and psychology than they have used. But up to recent years they appear to have been satisfied with very meager knowledge about themselves. Conditions have changed somewhat, lately, and psychology-good, bad, and worse is now read and discussed by many thousands of persons. 

One should not despise or disparage even a little knowledge about mental qualities, however, for a little of anything that is good is better than none. If all adults from the moron grade on up knew and used every day the first two rules in this study, the world would be immeasurably better off. The voluble dabbler in worth-while matters may sometimes be a social nuisance, but he is of a higher mental grade, usually, than the person who takes no interest in learning the same subject. However, the good mind, if interested at

KNOW THYSELF 

all, usualy wants to have more than a superficial knowledge of useful things. 

How much self-knowledge can a layman acquire?

If one considers the fact that physicians and psychologists combined know very little about the body, the mind, and heredity-compared to what may be known -the layman, at best, is likely to remain only a novice.

This should not, however, discourage the student of mental qualities, for with a year or two of intelligent study and observation he should accumulate much valuable information about himself. It should be encouraging for one to know the good mental qualities he has, just as it is encouraging to know that one has a good heart, good lungs, or normal blood-pressure. And it should also be a cause for intelligent restraint for one to know his bad mental qualities, just as it is a cause for restraint to know that one's stomach can not handle boiled cabbage or raw onions.

Besides, if one knows his defects one is more likely to try to overcome them instead of allowing them to overcome him. To do otherwise would be a frank admission of faults with no desire and determination to correct them-a mental attitude that one would expect to find in an obstinate, low-grade moron. A person with fair intelligence is likely to wash his face if he knows it is dirty.

Before selecting an occupation-and whether one intends to make a living with his hands or his head, or both, matters not-one should know what he is mentally equipped to do. There are entirely too many misfits in the world. They are found, not only in the professions, but in every walk of life. Probably from twenty-five to fifty per cent of the men and women in the professions are misfits. The author is reasonably sure this is true of the medical profession, and a less intimate and less extensive knowledge of other professions leads him to surmise that it is true of them. Misfits in a profes-

PROGRESS 

251 sion are not necessarily wholly bad; but both they and the profession they are in-to say nothing of the longsuffering public-would be better off had they selected some other lifework.

Occupational guidance is a vast, untilled field in which all persons in the upper mental brackets should work intelligently when they have an opportunity to do so. 

Society, on the whole, would be greatly improved if there were more intelligent forethought and guidance and less ambitious but childish wish-thinking used in the selection of an occupation. A few persons in their late 'teens and early twenties select their occupations wisely or, perhaps, luckily. Many more take whatever comes along; and many of these continue throughout life-taking whatever comes along, and in most cases their economic condition is far from satisfactory. 

The time to teach and to emphasize the importance of occupational selection and welfare is between the fifteenth and twentieth years. We physicians know the uselessness of talking personal responsibility to inferior men and women who are forty years of age or over. 

Many of these ignorant day laborers and others with small wages have assumed enough financial responsibility-mostly family responsibility-for a man having from five to ten times their actual income. They are, for the most part, too dull and too stupid to see that they have never had enough income to support more than one or two persons, and usually not more than enough to support themselves. Their outlook is indeed dreary. Millions of persons in the lower mental brackets are hopelessly blind, careless, or indifferent about assuming personal responsibility that they can not meet. 

Because of this, alone, avenues of occupational progress are often closed to them.

Then there is companionship, the greatest problem in life after the animal necessaries and comforts have been provided for. Companionship of choice is often

KNOW THYSELF 

a miserable failure and ends in ill feeling and bitterness because of ignorance of mental qualities. The mental or physical quality that attracted on slight acquaintance and brought forth the companionship proved to be of far less importance than the bad mental qualities, blindly unseen when the association began. And yet the bad mental qualities were there, as plain as a traffic signal, to anyone who knows mental qualities well.

The bad results of mental misalliance are seen daily. 

The emotionally-controlled person may marry a neurotic and then live in hell until divorce or death ends the association. The highly subjective person-more often the female of the species-talks incessantly of herself and her family, drives women to tears and men to profanity. Persons who are not manners-conscious, language-conscious, or clothes-conscious, especially the first, irritate other persons who possess these qualities. 

The resourceful person, the person who knows how to solve his problems, loses all patience with the parasite, especially the financial parasite. A man with a resolute, decisive mind loses respect for the irresolute, indecisive person. The person with strong integrity is often angered or irritated because of having had relations or association, either voluntary or involuntary, with persons who are conspicuously deficient in integrity. The person who limits and fulfils his personal responsibility rigidly, has no patience with irresponsible persons. The person who does not discuss his personal affairs freely is embarrassed and offended by the uncouth lout who boldly asks him personal questions.

In this way one could enumerate scores of mental qualities where the bad clashes with the good and makes voluntary association undesirable and finally impossible. 

An intelligent, thorough study of mental qualities is worth anyone's time, more especially between the ages of fifteen and twenty-one, for the help it will give in

PROGRESS 

253 selecting good companions and avoiding bad companions.

The good mind prevents problems as well as solves them. A person with a good mind thinks first of preventing economic dependency, and then he is likely to give much thought to obtaining agreeable companions and to preventing association, more especially involuntary association, with persons whose mental make-up is objectionable to him. He selects his companions cautiously and wisely. If an employer, he tries to select dependable employees; also it may be observed that a learning mind is prone to select another learning mind as an associate.

And this brings up another interesting observation, namely, that persons having superior mentality are prone to have the same intimate companions and friends, year after year. Satisfactory companions are hard to get in the upper mental brackets, so it is not strange that these people are bound to each other with hoops of steel. But this is not true of persons having inferior mentality. They are often seen to become greatly enamored of Tom, Dick, and Harry, and of Babe, Edythe, and Mayme, for a few days or a few weeks, and then they become disillusioned, they disagree and fall out, and at once "take up" with a new set-just as trashy.

The species makes mental progress slowly; so too does society. But the individual person, especially if he is well supplied with innate intelligence, can make progress rapidly enough so he can see it, himself, from year to year, just as a child is aware of his physical growth after about the tenth year. An intelligent man is conscious of the fact that he has put away many childish things; but an inferior person still cherishes his childish ideas and prejudices as a chld does its favorite toy.

A mind should be judged by what it does, notably by

KNOW THYSELF 

the way it voluntarily sees problems, prevents problems, avoids problems, solves problems, and by its creative. results, if it is of the creative type. A person with a good mind can judge the quality of his own mind's action better than he can judge the action of another person's mind, if he is not on the defensive; he knows whether he has solved his problems well or not. 

It should not be embarrassing for one to ask himself personal questions relative to talents and other mental qualities. Why should it, for example, embarrass a person to admit-to himself-that he has little or no talent for music, that he has mediocre mental integration, poor language-consciousness, and that he could live happily in a boiler factory? And most surely it should not embarrass a man to recognize that he has enough problem-solving ability to be honestly and honorably self-supporting, to be able to support his offspring if he has any, and to live amicably with the persons with whom he comes in contact daily.

An intelligent man also knows whether he is making progress or not, and, if so, whether much or little. 

Any person who can read this and the preceding paragraph is likely to pause for a moment and take a brief personal inventory, after which he may have a feeling of satisfaction; or he may feel somewhat deflated. Taking stock, both of what one has and of what one has done, has a way of putting a person in his proper place. 

Too often both children and adults are judged mentally-and, in fact, often judge themselves-by the amount of schooling they have had; but that is a poor basis for judgment. School work is largely a matter of memory; besides, very few persons know the student's standing in the class; whether in high school the teacher gave him a passing grade in order to get rid of him, or whether as a college student he had to be tutored and coached by his roommate, his classmates, and others to keep him from flunking. What a person does with

PROGRESS 

255 problems together with his creative work, if any, are the final tests and not his school work either good or bad, much or little. How inane and stupid it would be to judge Franklin, Washington, Lincoln, Burbank,

Steinmetz, and Edison by their experience at school! 

For social reasons, as well as for what little they can learn, it is altogether desirable that boys and girls finish. high school. But weeds can not be made to grow into great oaks, even though they grow in the same soil only a hundred feet apart. One is making progress in mental studies when he has learned what to use as measuring rods, namely, worth-while things done voluntarily. 

Also one is making noticeable progress in the study of mental qualities when he thinks and, when necessary, speaks of persons as having superior minds, mediocre or average minds, or inferior minds; and knows in detail why he thinks so. Parents and others who deal with wishes, and do not even want to know the facts, will continue to speak of persons as bright; or, if the persons mentioned are not bright, they will probably continue to make excuses of the usual kind for them. 

The truth is too harsh and unacceptable for habitual wish-thinkers. And all of this is about as intelligent to a physician as it would be for him to speak of a cancer as a sore, or to speak of tuberculosis as weak lungs. 

Persons who think accurately, speak accurately.

The wise, well-informed physician looks for and observes details in examining the body to note whether it is normal and healthy, congenitally abnormal, or diseased after birth. The whole trend of his observation is for more detail, and of his language, for more accuracy. In making a diagnosis he does not merely state that the person is sick, for that is the language of the ignorant layman. The physician sums up the details of what he finds that is wrong or abnormal with the patient; he uses accurate language, and he makes his diagnosis from the evidences of disease or abnormality

KNOW THYSELF 

that he has found. If he has insufficient evidence he defers the diagnosis until he finds more evidence. 

The same mental process should be used by the student of mental qualities. If the person being studied lacks resolution, lacks ability to solve his problems, lacks control of his major instincts, lacks control of his emotions, lacks ability and will to learn-or, whatever it is he is lacking-the details should be known by the student before he arrives at a conclusion. In medicine we say that a misfit, poorly educated doctor, who makes a diagnosis with insufficient evidence, is a slap-stick diagnostician. The student of mental qualities too would do well to avoid slap-stick methods. The world will probably go on just the same and society will be just as well off, if the student will refrain from announcing his half-baked opinions. 

If the student finds, for example, six evidences of mental superiority in someone he is studying, let him say that he has found six evidences, and then let him remember there are ninety-four more to look for. It is easy for the tyro to overtalk his knowledge and thereby announce one more foolish opinion of which there are millions announced daily. Mental qualities are a good subject to know about, but a poor subject to talk about unless one talks very intelligently to intelligent people.

There are great potential social values that might be brought into use by a more general knowledge of mental qualities. More and more, men are asking—what is to happen to society? For society has more troubles at the present time than it knows what to do with, seemingly more and bigger problems than it can solve. The pot calls the kettle black. Those in the upper ten per cent say the fault lies in the lower thirty or forty per cent; and those in the lower twenty-five per cent say the fault lies in the upper five or ten per cent-financially speaking. The sentimentalists and religionists

PROGRESS 

257 say-give, give, give; meaning, of course, to give money. The upper classes have given and continue to give either voluntarily or because they are forced to give through taxes, but the problem is still unsolved. The economists say the economic system is at fault. The eugenicists say the trouble should be prevented-that the stock is faulty and is getting more faulty every year, and that we shall get nowhere with charity and taxing the intelligent to take care of a growing horde of the inferior. The politicians are afraid to speak plainly on the matter, lest they lose votes. Some of the religious, interested in counting noses, apparently would rather see millions in the breadlines, than to sanction intelligent limitation of offspring. The foolish look into space and wish that somebody, somehow, somewhere, would do something to change the what-is into something that is a great deal better. 

For ages men who could make a living for themselves have had to give to the mentally inferior. Religions of all kinds have taught that it was right and necessary to give to the poor, and there has always been tax-raised money spent on the poor. These practices will doubtless continue to be. But there has been very little effort to make the inferior avoid being poor, to make them have a definite, clear-cut feeling of personal responsibility about keeping out of parasitism and poverty. 

Thousands and hundreds of thousands of pages have been written on the wholesale cause and cure of poverty. 

For the most part the cure advocated has been to take money from those who have more than enough for their current needs and give it to those who, for any cause either good or bad, do not have enough to provide for the necessaries of life. It is very seldom that anything has been said to the poor as to why they are poor or how to avoid being in the parasitical class. That is the greatest defect in the practices, both of the past and of the present, having to do with the problem of poverty.

KNOW THYSELF 

It is not known just when the optimistic thought came into the minds of the upper classes that children born of inferior parents should be expected to be high grade citizens, regardless of the fact that they are likely to be of poor mental quality inherently and that many of them have not had intelligent teaching, either by precept or example, relative to honest, honorable living.

The upper classes must ultimately see the necessity of thinking for the poor and the inferior, and of teaching them what to do and what not to do to avoid poverty, just as they teach them—and provide the means for their learning-the three R's and more advanced school work. If individuals and families could be taught, as they were quite generally many years ago, that poverty, illiteracy, and crime are disgraceful, there would follow very noticeable improvement in personal and family honor and integrity. Millions of our presentday parasites are apparently neither embarrassed nor worried about their condition half as much as the taxpayers are about paying their taxes. 

There appears to be no logical reason why a course in personal and social ethics should not be written down to the mental level of the average high school student and taught even required-in all secondary schools. 

Such teaching would be far more profitable in later years than, let us say, a course in algebra or plane geometry. Many of our high school graduates have no philosophy of life, no personal code of ethics, other than to do or to get whatever they can get away with. 

Nor does there seem to be any very good reason why mental qualities should not be taught to secondary school students. If this were done, millions of boys and girls would learn in their 'teens what some would learn only at forty or sixty, and that others would never learn at all.

An adult without an honorable code of ethics, and integrity enough to follow it, is a social and economic

PROGRESS 

259 liability. The optimistic upper classes have tried dispensing sweetness and light and the politicians have compelled them to dispense their money. But the problem of our lower twenty-five per cent is greater now than ever before. The habitually parasitical and the criminal need stronger medicine than sweetness and light. Many of them need surgery. 

If there were a hundred times as much teaching and publicity given to personal and family responsibility for economic security as we now have that would still not be much, for we now have almost none-society would be much better off. The political mountebanks, whose mouthings fill thousands of columns of newspaper space, do every thing they can to get votes, hence they are silent in this matter. But they are prolific with assertions and promises that Congress-which after all means the taxpayers-should and will take care of those who are either wilfully or inherently parasitical. Because of the vast amount of publicity given political promises and panaceas originating in Washington and the several state capitals to help the helpless, the improvident, and the innately inferior, millions of our people have given up personal and family responsibility for being self-supporting, and daily recite their hard luck stories and extend their hands for public aid or for private charity.

There may be too much rugged individualism in the way of unfair competition in business and in overproduction of manufactured goods; but there is entirely too little rugged individualism and personal honor and integrity in the maintenance of personal and family economic independence. We physicians learn this latter fact by dealing with the poor extensively and not by reading about it in books and periodicals; we have first-hand, incontrovertible knowledge in the matter. 

Political mountebanks know the value of promises to get votes. Also they have never hesitated to buy votes

KNOW THYSELF 

with the taxpayers' money which they disburse as political pie, political pork, and pensions. Any system of economics that ignores personal and family responsibility for economic dependency is bound to be harmful and ultimately a failure. Taking earned income from the intelligent to provide for the lazy and the improvident may be good political propaganda and practice, but it is not good sense. So much for the political encouragement rather than cure of economic dependency. 

But, asks the student-what of the rich, what of the corruption that exists in high places? One can only answer that a writer should write of things about which he knows. Physicians know the poor and the inferior, therefore they should write on what they know about. 

There is no dearth of printed matter coming off the press daily about the faults of big business, but there is a dearth of information, fearlessly expressed, about the personal causes of poverty and crime.

Knowledge of mental qualities also enables one to understand better the motives and activities of the herd, as well as of the individual person, in religion, patriotism, and other mass beliefs, prejudices, fears, joys, sorrows, and expectations. One finds that persons in a given mental stratum have basically about the same type of religion, regardless of a few differences in external details. Persons in the lower mental brackets seemingly need definite religious authority—either book or official rule-that they hold to as infallible, while persons in the upper mental brackets will follow a rule of reason. Also it may be observed that the religion of the inferior is largely parasitical and that they go in much for rewards and punishments, while the religion of the upper mental classes is largely idealistic, and they are prone to feature a socialized religion having to do with the here and now. 

The best of patriotism in the lower mental brackets is a mingled feeling of fear and glory, while patriotism

PROGRESS 

261 in its worst form is shot through with revenge, oppression, and graft. In the upper mental brackets, fairness, justice, and peace between nations are the dominant thoughts. Patriotism for pay, professional patriots, and sword rattlers were probably in Dr. Johnson's mind when he said that "patriotism is the last refuge of the scoundrel."

Men of the same mental make-up, especially in the upper mental brackets, have or may have had very different taught-beliefs, but their beliefs that they have thought out themselves will be very similar. Also it may be observed that when superior men meet for a common purpose-religious, charitable, educational, or what-not -that they are prone to suppress their taught-beliefs, and proceed with reason and experience in quest of the highest good for all.

At the present time about one person in every thousand has given some serious thought to mental qualities. 

These persons are professional teachers of psychology, an occasional schoolmarm who has been assigned the task of guessing at problem pupils in public schools, psychologists employed by large business firms to examine employees and prospective employees, and a very few physicians. Lack of progress in the study of mental qualities has been due very largely to lack of useful information written in simple, layman's language.

Probably five per cent of our people-the so-called reading public would study the subject if they had a suitable textbook to read.

A study of mental qualities should help a person to know his own worth. Knowledge of mental qualities also causes one to read biography with increased interest and understanding; it enables one to consider and accept or reject more wisely the printed matter one reads from day to day in newspapers and elsewhere. Likewise in the conversation and small talk of the day such knowledge tends to make one consider the speaker in

KNOW THYSELF 

conjunction with that which is spoken. Doubt, discrimination, and improved judgment come into the mind of the man who knows mental qualities, and disappointment and disillusionment are correspondingly diminished.

The individual student, if inclined to face facts, should be able to know himself with fair accuracy after a year or two of study and use of what he has learned; and added years of study and observation will give him more knowledge. This knowledge should increase his general understanding of human behavior, his sense of justice and fairness toward other persons, his ability to help other persons who are inherently capable of being helped, and his peace of mind. 

If the student is inclined to self-improvement-and most students of mental qualities are the recognition of his remediable defects will stimulate his desire to overcome them. The man who succeeds in any worthwhile work uses the best of his mental qualities and tries to avoid being harmed by his poorer qualities; it is, for example, a matter of good sense for a man who can not make a good public speech to refuse to try to do so.

To understand the motives and behavior of human beings, either past or present, or to predict with fair accuracy their future behavior, it is necessary to know the mental make-up of the individual or of the herd that is being judged. To misuse, to despise, or to ridicule the mentally inferior is as unfair as it is to misuse, to despise, or to ridicule persons because of their congenital or acquired physical defects. Many persons never learn how to be firm with the inferior without being unnecessarily harsh or even cruel to them. Many persons never learn with whom not to be familiar, nor the difference between inoffensive familiarity and illmannered vulgarity with their equals and superiors.

PROGRESS 

263 A person who recognizes the mental qualities of men, women, and children; who is intelligent enough to know what his social attitude, his duty, and his responsibility, if any, toward them should be, has acquired useful knowledge that will help him daily to pass through life with the maximum amount of pleasure and the minimum amount of grief.

THE END
